\gamma\in\mathsf{REG}
.

\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
extends
\mathsf{FC}
such that regular constraints are atomic formulas.
We extend the semantics as follows: for a pattern substitution
\sigma
we have that
\sigma\models(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
, if
\sigma(x)\in\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
and
\sigma(x)\sqsubseteq\sigma(\mathfrak{u})
.

We use
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
to denote the set of
\mathsf{FC}
-formulas extended with regular constraints.
Let
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
be the existential positive fragment of
\mathsf{FC}
, and let
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
be
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
extended with regular constraints.

FC and Document Spanners.

In order to connect
\mathsf{FC}
and document spanners, we must overcome the fact that
\mathsf{FC}
reasons over words, whereas document spanners reason over spans.
To this end, we define how a spanner can
realize
an
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
-formula, and how an
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
-formula can
realize
a spanner.

Definition 4.24
.

A pattern substitution
\sigma

expresses
a
(V,w)
-tuple,
\mu
, if we have that
\mathsf{Dom}(\sigma)=\{x^{P},x^{C}\mid x\in V\}
, and
\sigma(x^{P})=w_{[1,i\rangle}
and
\sigma(x^{C})=w_{[i,j\rangle}
for the span
\mu(x)=[i,j\rangle
for all
x\in V
.

An
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
-formula
\varphi

realizes
a spanner
P
if
\mathsf{free}(\varphi)=\{x^{P},x^{C}\mid x\in{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}\}
and
\sigma\models\varphi
, for all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
where
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=w
, if and only if
\sigma
expresses some
\mu\in P(w)
.

Intuitively, every spanner variable
x
is represented by two
\mathsf{FC}
-variables
x^{P}
and
x^{C}
, such that in each
(V,w)
-tuple
\mu
, we have that
x^{C}
contains the actual content
w_{\mu(x)}
and
x^{P}
contains the prefix of
w
before the start of
w_{\mu(x)}
.

Definition 4.25
.

A spanner
P

realizes

\varphi\in\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
if
{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}=\mathsf{free}(\varphi)
and for all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have that
\mu\in P(w)
if and only if
\sigma\models\varphi
for
\sigma
where
\sigma(x)=w_{\mu(x)}
for all
x\in{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}
.

Using
Definition

4.24
and
Definition

4.25
, the following holds:

Theorem 4.26

(Freydenberger and Peterfreund
[
41
]
)
.

There are two-way polynomial time conversions between:

•

\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
and
\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}}
, and

•

\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
and
\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{gcore}}
.

Therefore,
Theorem

4.26
allows us to use
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
-formulas as a way to express core spanners, and
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
as a way to express generalized core spanners.
The difference between reasoning over words and reasoning over spans brings up an interesting point, spanners can represent factors as spans, however each span is a particular interval of the input text.
Whereas,
\mathsf{FC}
can simulate spans using the prefix and content variables for every span variable, and furthermore can reason about factors without referring to a specific position in the text.

A Note on SpLog.

Prior to the introduction of
\mathsf{FC}
, Freydenberger
[
37
]
defined a syntactic restriction on
\mathsf{EC[REG]}
(the existential theory of concatenation with regular constraints) called
SpLog
.
We briefly discuss this logic, as we draw upon results from
[
37
]
in the coming chapters of this thesis.

Definition 4.27
.

Let
\mathsf{w}\in\Xi
be the
main variable
.
Let
{\mathsf{SpLog}}
be the set of all
SpLog
-formulas.
Then,
\varphi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
if
\varphi
can be obtained from the following recursive rules:

•

\varphi:=(\mathsf{w}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
, where
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}(\Xi\setminus\{\mathsf{w}\}))^{*}
,

•

\varphi:=(\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})
, where
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
,

•

\varphi:=(\varphi_{1}\lor\varphi_{2})
, where
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
and
\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{1})=\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{2})
,

•

\varphi:=\exists x\colon\psi
, where
\psi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
and
x\in\mathsf{free}(\varphi)\setminus\{\mathsf{w}\}
,

•

\varphi:=\psi\land(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
, where
\psi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
and
x\in\mathsf{free}(\psi)
.

The semantics of a
SpLog
-formula follow from the semantics of
\mathsf{EC[REG]}
.
However, due to the fact that we have
\mathsf{w}
on the left-hand side of each word equation, for some substitution
\sigma
, all variables must be substituted with a factor of
\sigma(\mathsf{w})
.
Thus, through the syntactic definition of any
\varphi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
, we have that
\sigma(x)\sqsubseteq\sigma(\mathsf{w})
for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)
.

However, because the finite universe is ensured through syntax, this requires some restrictions; such as regular constraints and disjunction being “guarded”.
We also need restrictions when we extend
SpLog
with negation.

Definition 4.28
.

Let
{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
denote the set of
{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
-formulas. Where
{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
-formulas are defined as
SpLog
-formulas extended with the following recursive rule:
If
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
and
\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{1})\subseteq\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{2})
, then
(\neg\varphi_{1}\land\varphi_{2})\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
.

Due to these restrictions along with the fact that they ensure a finite universe through syntax, Freydenberger and Peterfreund
[
41
]
said that
{\mathsf{SpLog}}
and
{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
are “
…more cumbersome than
\mathsf{FC}
and do not generalize as nicely
”.

SpLog
was introduced as a logic for core spanners (and is short for spanner logic), and therefore it is not too surprising that extending
SpLog
with negation results in the generalized core spanners.
To show this connection, we extend
Definition

4.24
and
Definition

4.25
to
SpLog
and
{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
.
We can then observe the following:

Theorem 4.29

(Freydenberger
[
37
]
)
.

There are two-way polynomial time conversions between:

•

{\mathsf{SpLog}}
and
\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}}
, and

•

{\mathsf{SpLog}}^{\neg}
and
\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{gcore}}
.

In
Chapter

5
, we shall introduce a conjunctive query fragment of
\mathsf{FC}
.
The fragment we introduce is similar to the
\mathsf{PC}
fragment of
SpLog
introduced in
[
37
]
.

Definition 4.30
.

Let
\varphi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
.
We say that
\varphi\in{\mathsf{PC}}
if

\varphi=\exists\vec{x}\colon\bigl(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(\mathsf{w}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}(x_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{j})\bigr).

A
SpLog
formula is in DPC-normal form if it is a finite disjunction of
{\mathsf{PC}}
-formulas.

As the term DPC-normal form suggests, for a given
\varphi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
, we can compute an equivalent
\psi\in{\mathsf{SpLog}}
, where
\psi
is in DPC-normal form (see Lemma 5.6 of
[
37
]
).
Freydenberger
[
37
]
conjectures that the blowup from a
SpLog
-formula to a
SpLog
-formula in DPC-normal form is exponential.

While we never directly work with
SpLog
in this thesis, from time to time we make reference to it, and make reference to results from
[
37
]
.

4.7
Computational Model and Complexity Measures

We use the
random access machine
model with uniform cost measures.
That is, we assume a computational model that executes pseudo-code where basic operations (such as addition, subtraction, multiplication, divide, comparisons, variable definitions and access, etc.) each take constant time – see Chapter 2 of Cormen, Leiseron, Rivest, and Stein
[
15
]
for more details.

The size of each machine word is logarithmic in the size of the input.
Factors of a word
w\in\Sigma^{*}
are represented as spans of
w
which allows us to check whether
u=v
for
u,v\sqsubseteq w
in constant time after preprocessing that takes linear time and space
[
51
,
62
]
(see
Proposition

6.1
for more details).

Unless stated otherwise, the complexity results stated in this thesis are in terms of
combined complexity
.
That is, both the query and the “data” are considered part of the input.
When considering relational databases, the
data
refers to the input database. For information extraction, by data we mean the input word.

Data complexity
refers to when the query is fixed, and the data is the input.
Analogously,
query complexity
refers to when the data is fixed, and the query is the input.

When considering the enumeration of results for a query executed on a word, we say that we can enumerate results with
polynomial delay
if there exists an algorithm which returns the first result in polynomial time, the time between two consecutive results is polynomial, and the time between the last result and terminating is polynomial.

We assume the canonical definitions, and acronyms (for example,
\mathsf{NP}
for non-deterministic polynomial time and
\mathsf{PSPACE}
for polynomial space) for complexity classes. See, for example, Arora and Barak
[
8
]
.

Chapter 5
Conjunctive Queries for FC

This chapter gives results on the expressive power of, and the complexity of various decision problems for
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s in the context of information extraction.
A particular focus is the introduction of a conjunctive query fragments for
\mathsf{FC}
and
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
, which we denote as
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
respectively.

First, we define conjunctive queries for core spanners, called
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s, originally introduced by Freydenberger, Kimelfeld and Peterfreund
[
39
]
.
These
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s are a projection over a sequence of equalities on a join of regex formulas.
One issue with
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s is that regex formulas allow for variables to be in disjunction subexpressions which could be seen as un-CQ-like.
Because of this, we define
synchronized

\mathsf{ERCQ}
s, or
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s, that do not allow a variable in subexpressions of the form
(\gamma_{1}\lor\gamma_{2})
.

Then, we define a conjunctive query fragment for
\mathsf{FC}
which we call
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Like with
\mathsf{FC}
, we can extend
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
with regular constraints, which we denote with
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We also extend
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
with union, giving us
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
respectively.

The first results of this chapter are on the expressive power of the aforementioned models.
We show that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
has the same expressive power as
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s, and from
Theorem

4.29
we can immediately determine that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
has the same expressive power as core spanners (while
Theorem

4.29
uses the logic
SpLog
, this is just a normal form of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
).
We also consider the comparative expressive power of various fragments of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
(such as pattern languages, regular languages, etc.).
The results of the section regarding the expressive power of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
and related models are summarized in
Figure

5.3
.
The question of whether
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is strictly less expressive than
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
is left open.
While the author believes this to be the case, we briefly consider certain cases where
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
can simulate union.

The next focus is on the complexity of various decision problems for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
– the results of which are summarized in
Table

5.1
.
Of particular interest are
static analysis problems
, as they have strong connections to query optimization.
For example, the
containment problem
for relational
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s is decidable, and hence can be used for tasks like query minimization, see Chapter 6 of
[
1
]
.

While
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness for model checking
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s immediately follows from the erasing pattern language membership problem
[
30
]
, we show that
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness holds even if we make rather large restrictions, such as the input word being of length one, and the query being acyclic (under the view that each word equation is an atom).

One of the main results of this chapter is that the so-called
universality problem
is undecidable for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
This, in turn, implies that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
equivalence, and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
regularity are also undecidable.
Another main result is that
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
regularity is neither semi-decidable nor co-semi-decidable, the proof of which can be easily adapted to show that
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
equivalence is undecidable.
These undecidability result have many consequences for query optimization.
For example, there does not exist a computable function that takes an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and returns an “minimal equivalent”, according to any
complexity measure
.

Split-correctness, in relation to information extraction, was introduced by Doleschal, Kimelfeld, Martens, Nahshon, and Neven
[
25
]
.
The main idea is that one may not wish to query a whole document.
Instead, in certain circumstances it is advantageous to first split the document into sections, and query these individual sections.
This then opens questions regarding whether the semantics for a query changes if one first splits the document and runs the original query over these section (in comparison to running the query over the whole document).
For the purposes of this chapter, we assume that
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s are used both as a way to split a document (using a unary
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
), and to query the document.
We show that three static analysis problems considered in
[
25
]
known as
split-correctness
,
splittability
, and
self-splittability
are all undecidable for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
This answers a problem left open in
[
25
]
regarding split-correctness when equality operators are incorporated.

The final section of this chapter considers the size of the output relations that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s extract.
To this end, we adapt the idea of
ambiguity
from Mateescu and Salomaa
[
80
]
.
Deciding whether a
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
k
-ambiguous (produces a table of size at most
k
for any input word) is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete.

5.1
ERCQs and SERCQs

One way to define conjunctive queries for information extraction would be to define conjunctive queries over regex formulas.
In this section, we consider such regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s, and give some existing results on these regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s and related models.

Definition 5.1
.

A regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
is a projection over a join of regex formulas.
That is, queries of the form
\pi_{Y}\left(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{n}\right)
where
\gamma_{i}\in\mathsf{RGX}
for each
i\in[n]
.

One issue with the definition of regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s is that regex formulas allow for disjunction over variables, which leads to “un-CQ-like” behaviour.

Theorem 5.2

(Freydenberger, Kimelfeld, and Peterfreund
[
39
]
)
.

Model checking for a regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, even if:

1.

The query is acyclic,

2.

each regex formula is of bounded size, or

3.

the input word is of length one.

Thus, unlike relational
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s, restricting the regex query to be
acyclic
does not lead to a tractable fragment.
Furthermore, regex formulas allow for disjunction over variables, and nesting of variable.
Therefore, analysing the structure of a regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
, or providing syntactic restrictions on the query is not a straightforward task.
One caveat to
Theorem

5.2
is that
[
39
]
considers
\gamma
-acyclicity, which is an even more restrictive class than
\alpha
-acyclicity.
Freydenberger and Holldack
[
38
]
proved that model checking for a regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete (i. e., without the restrictions given in
Theorem

5.2
).

Definition 5.3
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathsf{RGX}
.
We say that
\gamma
is
synchronized
if for every subexpression of
\gamma
of the form
(\gamma_{1}\lor\gamma_{2})
, neither
\gamma_{1}
nor
\gamma_{2}
contain any variables. The set of synchronized
\mathsf{RGX}
-formulas is denoted
\mathsf{sRGX}
.

This definition of synchronized regex formulas is adapted from
[
89
]
, which defines synchronization based on a set of variables.
That is,
\gamma
is synchronized for a variable
x\in\Xi
if for every subexpression of the form
(\gamma_{1}\lor\gamma_{2})
, neither
\gamma_{1}
nor
\gamma_{2}
contains an
x
.
Then,
\gamma
is synchronized for
X\subseteq\Xi
if
\gamma
is synchronized for every
x\in X
.
We simply use the term
synchronized
if the regex formula is synchronized for
\Xi
.

Definition 5.4
.

The class of
equality regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s
(denoted as
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s) is defined as expressions of the form:

P:=\pi_{Y}\left(\zeta^{=}_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cdots\zeta^{=}_{x_{l},y_{l}}(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{k})\right),

where
l,k\in\mathbb{N}
,
Y\subset\Xi
, and
\gamma_{i}\in\mathsf{RGX}
for each
i\in[k]
. We call an
\mathsf{ERCQ}
a
synchronized

\mathsf{ERCQ}
, or just
\mathsf{SERCQ}
, if every regex formula is a synchronized
\mathsf{RGX}
-formula.
3
3

3

This nomenclature differs slightly from
[
46
]
, where
\mathsf{SERCQ}
is shorthand for
string equality regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
, and the term
synchronized
\mathsf{SERCQ}
was used.

Since
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s do not allow for disjunction over variables, some may consider
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s to be “closer” to relational
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s than
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s.

Example 5.5
.

Consider
P:=\zeta^{=}_{x_{1},x_{2}}\left(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\gamma_{2}\right)
where
\gamma_{1}:=\Sigma^{*}\cdot x_{1}\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\Sigma^{*}
and
\gamma_{2}:=\Sigma^{*}\cdot x_{2}\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\Sigma^{*}
. Given
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have that
\llbracket P\rrbracket(w)
contains those
\mu
such that the factor
w_{\mu(x_{1})}
is non-empty, and is immediately followed by the symbol
\mathtt{a}
, the factor
w_{\mu(x_{2})}
is immediately followed by the symbol
\mathtt{b}
, and
w_{\mu(x_{1})}=w_{\mu(x_{2})}
. Since both
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are synchronized,
P
is a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
.

With regards to model checking for
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s, due to the pattern language membership problem, even one regex formula is sufficient for
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness.

Theorem 5.6

(Freydenberger and Holldack
[
38
]
)
.

Model checking for a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, even if the query one has only one regex formula.

While
[
38
]
states that the so-called
evaluation problem
for Boolean core spanners is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, this is equivalent to the model checking problem being
\mathsf{NP}
-complete.
Furthermore, the proof in
[
38
]
reduces from the erasing pattern language membership problem (see
[
2
]
), resulting in a core spanner that is an
\mathsf{SERCQ}
.

Theorem

5.6
gives more evidence that finding a tractable class of
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s using a simple restriction on the structure of the query (akin to acyclicity for conjunctive queries) is not a straightforward task.
This is one reason why, for the majority of the thesis, we consider a different representation for conjunctive queries over words.

Note that sufficient criteria for tractable
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s were given in
[
39
]
– namely, if we restrict to the class of
\mathsf{ERCQ}
s to have a bounded number of joins and a bounded number of equality operators, then we can enumerate results with polynomial delay – consequently, model checking is tractable.

5.2
Introducing FC-CQs

In this section, we define
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, the conjunctive query fragment of
\mathsf{FC}
.

Definition 5.7
.

An
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is denoted as
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
where for all
i\in[n]
we have
x_{i}\in\Xi
, and
\alpha_{i}\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
. All variables that appear in
\vec{x}
must appear in some word equation
x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i}
. For the purposes of defining semantics, we consider this notation to be shorthand for
\varphi(\vec{x}):=\exists\vec{y}\colon\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
where
\vec{y}
is the tuple of all variables
that
appear in some
\eta_{i}
but not in
\vec{x}
.

In
[
41
]
,
\mathsf{FC}
was extended to
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
by adding regular constraints.
We extend
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
in the same way.
Also, we extend
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
(unions of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
formulas) canonically.
More formally,
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
is a query of the form
\varphi:=\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}\varphi_{i}

where
\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
for each
i\in[m]
, and for every
i,j\in[m]
we have that
\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{i})=\mathsf{free}(\varphi_{j})
.
The semantics are defined as
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w):=\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\llbracket\varphi_{i}\rrbracket(w)
for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.
We define
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
analogously.

For
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of the form
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\psi
where
\psi
is quantifier-free, let
\mathsf{body}(\varphi):=\psi
.
Utilizing
\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
allows us to talk about a substitution for all the variables of
\varphi
, since for a Boolean query
\varphi
, any substitution
\sigma\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
only needs to be defined for
\mathfrak{u}
.
If
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is Boolean, then for any
\sigma
such that
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
, and
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=w
, we have that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.

Recall the definition of acyclicity for
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s (see
Section

4.3
).
We say that
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
weakly acyclic
if the
{\mathsf{CQ}}
is acyclic, where word equations are treated as atoms.
Analogously, we define
weak join trees
for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Example 5.8
.

Say that we wish to find pairs of sentences such that the two sentences in the pair start with the same word.
To that end, Let
\Sigma
be an alphabet of ASCII characters, let
\gamma_{\mathsf{sen}}
be a regular expression that accepts well-formed sentences, and let
\gamma_{\mathsf{word}}
be a regular expression that accepts individual words.
For this example, we use
word
to mean a sequence that starts with a space, then contains a sequence of uppercase and lowercase letters, and ends with either a full stop or a space.
Now consider the following
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(x,y)\leftarrow(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{3})\land(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{sen}})\land(y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{sen}})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{word}}).

Given some natural language input text
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
extracts a binary relation consisting of those sentences
\sigma(x)
and
\sigma(y)
such that the first word in
\sigma(x)
and
\sigma(y)
are the same.
Notice that
\varphi
does not enforce
x
and
y
to be substituted with different sentences from the input text.
We can see that
\varphi
is weakly acyclic by observing the weak join tree in
Figure

5.1
.

x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2}
y\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{3}
y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{sen}}
x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{sen}}
z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{\mathsf{word}}

Figure 5.1
:
The query
\varphi
from
Example

5.8
is weakly acyclic due to the weak join tree illustrated here.

The reasoning behind the term
weak acyclicity
, is that a single word equation can be considered shorthand for a binary concatenation term and therefore some may not consider a word equation to be atomic.
The fact that word equations are shorthand for a concatenation term has some consequences with regards to tractability – weak acyclicity is not sufficient for tractable queries.
In
Chapter

6
, we address this issue by “splitting” the word equation into small tractable components for which a tractable notion of acyclicity for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s is defined.

5.3
Expressive Power

This section considers the expressive power of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and related models.
The main result of this section is
Theorem

5.16
which presents an inclusion diagram of the various classes we consider.

We first consider the expressive power of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s in comparison to
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s.
To that end, let us define a
parse trees
for
\gamma\in\mathsf{sRGX}
.
The parse tree for
\gamma
that we define is specific for our use, and is different to the standard definition of
\gamma
-parse trees which are used to define the semantics for regex-formulas, see
[
31
]
.

Definition 5.9
.

Let
\gamma\in\mathsf{sRGX}
. A
parse tree
for
\gamma
is a rooted, direct tree
T_{\gamma}
. Each node of
T_{\gamma}
is a subexpression of
\gamma
. The root of
T_{\gamma}
is
\gamma
. For each node
v
of
T_{\gamma}
, the following rules must hold.

1.

If
v
is
(\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1})
where
{\mathsf{vars}\left(\gamma_{1}\right)}\neq\emptyset
or
{\mathsf{vars}\left(\gamma_{2}\right)}\neq\emptyset
, then
v
has a left child
\gamma_{1}
, and a right child
\gamma_{2}
,

2.

if
v
is
x\{\gamma^{\prime}\}
, then
v
has
\gamma^{\prime}
as a single child, and

3.

if
v
is any other subexpression, then
v
is a leaf node.

The proof of the following proposition is similar to proofs from
[
38
,
37
,
41
]
, however we include this proof for completeness sake.

Recall
Definition

4.24
of how a formula
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
realizes a spanner.

Proposition 5.10
.

Given
P\in\mathsf{SERCQ}
, we can construct in polynomial time an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
that realizes
P
.

Proof.

Let
P:=\pi_{Y}\left(\zeta^{=}_{x_{1},y_{1}}\cdots\zeta^{=}_{x_{m},y_{m}}(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{k})\right)
be a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
. We realize
P
using the following
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
:

\varphi_{P}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}^{C}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{i}^{C})\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\varphi_{\gamma_{i}},

where
\vec{x}
contains
x^{P}
and
x^{C}
for every
x\in Y
and
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
for each
i\in[k]
is to be defined.
If
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
realizes
\gamma_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
, then
\varphi_{P}
realizes
P
since equalities are handled by the word equations
x_{i}^{C}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{i}^{C}
, and projection is handled by the choice of free variables in the head of
\varphi_{P}
.

Now we give the construction for each
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
.
We start with dealing with the content variables
x^{C}
for each
x\in\mathsf{vars}(P)
by giving construction that utilizes the parse tree
T_{\gamma_{i}}
.

For each
i\in[k]
, we define
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
as follows: Take the parse tree
T_{\gamma_{i}}
for
\gamma_{i}
and associate every node
n
of
T_{\gamma_{i}}
with a variable
v_{n}
:

•

If
n
is a variable binding
x\{\cdot\}
, let
v_{n}:=x^{C}
.

•

Otherwise – that is, if
n
is a concatenation
(\gamma\cdot\gamma^{\prime})
where
\gamma
or
\gamma^{\prime}
contains a variable, or a regular expression – let
v_{n}:=z_{n}
, where
z_{n}
is a new variable that is unique to
n
.

The construction shall ensure that, when matching
\gamma_{i}
against a word
w
, each variable
v_{n}
contains the part of
w
that matches against the subexpression of the node
n
.
To this end, for every node
n
, we also define an atom
A_{n}
as follows:

•

If
n
is a concatenation with left child
l
and right child
r
, where
l
or
r
contains a variable, then
A_{n}
is the word equation
(v_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{l}\cdot v_{r})
.

•

If
n
is a variable binding, let
A_{n}
be the word equation
(v_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{c})
, where
c
is the child of
n
.

•

If
n
is a regular expression
\gamma^{\prime}
, then
A_{n}
is the regular constraint
(v_{n}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma^{\prime})
.

We join these atoms (defined by the above construction) via conjunction.
Then, we define
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
as this conjunctive query with the extra word equation
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{n})
, where
v_{n}
is the variable associated to the root of the parse tree
T_{\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}}
.
Up to this point, we have that every
\sigma\in\llbracket\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}\rrbracket(w)
encodes the contents of the spans of some
\mu\in\llbracket\gamma_{i}\rrbracket(w)
.
The only part that is missing in the construction are the prefix variables.

Recall that for every node
n
in the parse tree
T_{\gamma_{i}}
, we defined a variable
v_{n}
that represents the part of
w
that matches against the subexpression of
n
.
To obtain the corresponding prefix, we define a function
\mathsf{p}
that maps each node
n
to a pattern
\mathsf{p}(n)\in\Xi^{*}
as follows.
Given a node
n
, we look for the lowest node above
n
that is a concatenation and has
n
as a right child or as a descendant of its left child.
If no such node exists – that is, if no node above
n
is a concatenation, or every concatenation above
n
has
n
as a descendant on the left side – let
\mathsf{p}(n):=\varepsilon
.
If such a node exists, we denote it by
m
and we denote its left child by
l
and define
\mathsf{p}(n):=\mathsf{p}(m)\cdot v_{l}
.
In other words,
\mathsf{p}(n)
is the concatenation of all
v_{l}
that belongs to nodes that refer a part of
w
that is to the left of the part that belongs to
n
.
Hence, to get the values for prefix variables, we take each node
n
that is a variable binding
x\{\cdot\}
and add the word equation
(x^{P}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathsf{p}(n))
to
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
.

Complexity.

First, we build the parse tree
T_{\gamma_{i}}
which can be constructed in time polynomial in the size of
\gamma_{i}
. Then, we mark each node of
T_{\gamma_{i}}
with a variable and add a word equation or regular constraint to
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
, which takes polynomial time.
To ensure the spanner
\gamma_{i}
represents is correctly realized, we add an extra word equation for the prefix variable – this clearly takes polynomial time.
There are linearly many regex formulas in
P
, we can construct
\varphi_{\gamma_{i}}
for all
i\in[k]
in polynomial time.
The final step of computing
\varphi_{P}
takes polynomial time – we consider each equality and add the corresponding word equation, and consider each variable in the projection and add the corresponding variables to the head of the query.
Therefore, the overall complexity is polynomial in the size of
P
.
∎

Note that the main part in the proof of
Proposition

5.10
is converting some
\mathsf{sRGX}
to an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, as join, equality operators, and projection can be easily handled using conjunction, word equations, and the choice of free variables respectively.

Example 5.11
.

Let
\gamma:=\bigl((\Sigma^{*}\cdot(x\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{a}))\cdot\Sigma^{*}\bigr)
.
We can realize
\gamma
as

\varphi_{\gamma}:=\mathsf{Ans}(x^{P},x^{C})\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{1})\land(v_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{2}\cdot v_{3})\land(v_{3}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\Sigma^{*})\land(v_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{4}\cdot v_{5})\\
\land(v_{4}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\Sigma^{*})\land(v_{5}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x^{C}\cdot v_{6})\land(v_{6}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a})\land(x^{C}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{7})\land(v_{7}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\Sigma^{+})\land(x^{P}\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{4}).

We can see that
\varphi
realizes
\gamma
by considering the construction given in the proof of
Proposition

5.10
along with the parse tree for
\gamma
given in
Figure

5.2
.

\bigl((\Sigma^{*}\cdot(x\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{a}))\cdot\Sigma^{*}\bigr)
(\Sigma^{*}\cdot(x\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{a}))
\Sigma^{*}
\Sigma^{*}
(x\{\Sigma^{+}\}\cdot\mathtt{a})
x\{\Sigma^{+}\}
\mathtt{a}
\Sigma^{+}

Figure 5.2
:
This figure illustrates a parse tree for the
\mathsf{RGX}
given in
Example

5.11
.

Next, we work towards showing a polynomial-time construction that given an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, outputs a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
that realizes the given
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Definition 5.12
.

We say that
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is in
structured normal form
if

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}(y_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{j}),

where
\alpha_{i}\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}(\Xi\setminus\{\mathfrak{u}\}))^{*}
for each
i\in[n]
.

Less formally,
\varphi
is in structured normal form if every word equation has
\mathfrak{u}
on the left-hand side, and the right-hand side of each word equation does not contain the universe variable
\mathfrak{u}
.

While the following lemma is not particularly interesting by itself, it helps us to streamline some subsequent proofs.

Lemma 5.13
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we can construct in polynomial time an equivalent
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\varphi^{\prime}
is in structured normal form.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We construct an equivalent
\varphi^{\prime}
in structured normal form in two steps.

Step one.
While there exists some atom of
\varphi
of the form
x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{u}\cdot\beta_{2}
, we replace this atom with
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta_{1})\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta_{2})\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\varepsilon)
.
We can show that the resulting formula is equivalent to
\varphi
using a length argument.
It is clear that for any substitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma\models\varphi
, the equality
|\sigma(x)|=|\sigma(\beta_{1})|+|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|+|\sigma(\beta_{2})|
holds.
Since
|\sigma(x)|\leq|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|
, we know that
|\sigma(x)|=|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|
, and
|\sigma(\beta_{1})|=|\sigma(\beta_{2})|=0
.
This immediately implies that
\sigma(x)=\sigma(\mathfrak{u})
and
\sigma(\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2})=\varepsilon
.

Step two.
While there exists some atom of
\varphi
of the form
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
where
x\neq\mathfrak{u}
, then we replace this atom with
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}p_{x}\cdot x\cdot s_{x})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}p_{x}\cdot\alpha\cdot s_{x})
, where
p_{x},s_{x}\in\Xi
are new variables that are unique to
x
.
It is clear that this re-writing step maintains equivalence between the given and resulting formulas using an analogous length argument to step one.

This construction can be done in polynomial time, as we consider each atom of
\varphi
, and if either it has
\mathfrak{u}
on the right-hand side, or
x\in\Xi\setminus\{\mathfrak{u}\}
on the left-hand side, then we replace that atom with an easily constructed
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
∎

We now prove that
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s are at least as powerful as
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Proposition 5.14
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we can construct in polynomial time a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
that realizes
\varphi
.

Proof.

We first show a conversion from a word equation of the form
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
or a regular constraint
(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
, into a pair
(\gamma,S)
where
\gamma\in\mathsf{sRGX}
, and
S
is a set of equality operators.
Then, these pairs constructed from atoms are combined into a single
\mathsf{SERCQ}
that realizes the input query.

Word equations.

We describe a procedure that takes a word equation
\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta
where
\beta\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}(\Xi\setminus\{\mathfrak{u}\}))^{*}
, and returns a pair consisting of an
\mathsf{sRGX}
-formula
\gamma
, and a set of equalities
S
.
Let
\beta=\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{l}
, where
\beta_{i}\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)
for each
i\in[l]
.
We then define
\gamma:=\gamma_{1}\cdot\gamma_{2}\cdots\gamma_{l}
as follows: If
\beta_{i}\in\Sigma
, then
\gamma_{i}=\beta_{i}
. If
\beta_{i}=x
where
x\in\Xi
, then we carry out one of the following:

•

If
\beta_{i}
is the left-most occurrence of
x
, let
\gamma_{i}=x\{\Sigma^{*}\}
,

•

otherwise, let
\gamma_{i}=x_{i}\{\Sigma^{*}\}
, where
x_{i}
is a new and unique variable, and add the equality operator
\zeta^{=}_{x,x_{i}}
to the set
S
.

Thus, from any word equation
\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta
, we construct a set of equalities
S
and a regex formula
\gamma
. It is clear that
\pi_{Y}S\gamma
realizes
\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta
, where
Y
is the set of variables in
\vec{x}
.

Regular constraints.

We convert a regular constraint
x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma
into a pair consisting of an
\mathsf{sRGX}
-formula
\gamma
, and set
S
consisting of a single equality.
Let
\gamma:=\Sigma^{*}\cdot x^{\prime}\{\gamma\}\cdot\Sigma^{*}
, and let
S:=\{\zeta_{x,x^{\prime}}^{=}\}
, where
x^{\prime}\in\Xi
is a new and unique variable.

Bringing it all together.

In the prior two steps of this construction, we have shown ways to construct a pair consisting of an
\mathsf{sRGX}
-formula and a set of equalities from a word equation or a regular constraint.
To finalize this construction, we assume
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is in structured normal form.
That is,
\varphi
is of the following form:

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\land\bigwedge_{j=n+1}^{m}(y_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{j}).

To convert
\varphi
into an equivalent
\mathsf{SERCQ}
, we convert each atom
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
into a pair
(\gamma_{i},S_{i})
where
\gamma_{i}\in\mathsf{sRGX}
and
S_{i}
is a set of equality operators, using the previous described procedure.
Likewise, we convert
(y_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{j})
into
(\gamma_{j},S_{j})
.
Then, we define
P
as follows:

P:=\pi_{Y}S_{1}\cdots S_{m}\left(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{m}\right),

where
Y=\mathsf{free}(\varphi)
.
Notice that we use the sets of equality operators in the definition of
P
.
This should be considered as shorthand for applying a sequence consisting of the equality operators in each set
S_{i}
.

Complexity.

To construct
P\in\mathsf{SERCQ}
that realizes
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we first consider each word equation, and construct a pair consisting of a regex formula and a set of equalities in polynomial time, by replacing variables with
x\{\Sigma^{*}\}
or
x_{i}\{\Sigma^{*}\}
and adding the corresponding equalities to the set.

Then, we consider regular constraints, which result in a regex formula which can also be constructed in polynomial time.
Joining the regex formulas constructed from each atom and converting the sets of equalities into a sequence of equality operators can clearly be done in polynomial time.
The last step is to add the projection – which uses the set of free variables of
\varphi
, meaning that this step can be easily done in polynomial time.

As each step in the construction of
P
can be done in polynomial time, we have constructed a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
that realizes an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
in polynomial time.
∎

The proofs for
Proposition

5.10
and
Proposition

5.14
follow closely to proofs from
[
41
,
38
,
37
]
.
Observing these previous works,
Proposition

5.10
and
Proposition

5.14
do not seem very surprising.
This thesis contains the proofs for
Proposition

5.10
and
Proposition

5.14
for the sake of completeness.

Let us now consider various fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
and known language generators (such as regular languages and pattern languages).
The results of this section are illustrated in
Figure

5.3
.
Recall that a Boolean query
\varphi
generates a language
\mathcal{L}(\varphi):=\{w\in\Sigma^{*}\mid w\models\varphi\}
.
If
\mathsf{A}
is a fragment of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
, by
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})
we denote the class of languages definable by a query
\varphi\in\mathsf{A}
.
It follows from the definitions that if
\mathsf{A}
is a syntactic fragment of
\mathsf{B}
, and
L\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{B})\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})
, then
\mathsf{A}
is strictly less expressive than
\mathsf{B}
, denoted
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{B})
.

Definition 5.15
.

A
regularly typed pattern
is a pair
\alpha_{T}:=(\alpha,T)
where
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
is a pattern, and
T
is a function that maps each
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
to a regular language
T(x)
.
We denote the language
\alpha_{T}
generates as
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
and this language is defined as
\{\sigma(\alpha)\mid\sigma\text{ is a substitution, and }\sigma(x)\in T(x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\}
.

The set of regular typed patterns is denoted by
\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}]
, and the class of languages definable by a regularly typed pattern is denoted by
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
.

Typed pattern languages have been considered in the context of
learning theory

[
65
,
48
]
, the details of which will not be considered in this thesis.
The expressive power of regularly typed patterns has been considered in
[
97
]
, which looks at the expressive power of regular typed patterns in comparison to so-called REGEX and so-called
pattern expressions
.

\mathsf{PAT}
\mathsf{REG}
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}]
\mathsf{SERCQ}
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}}
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}

Figure 5.3
:
This figure illustrates the expressive power of different fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
. A directed edge from
\mathsf{A}
to
\mathsf{B}
denotes that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{B})
. A dashed, undirected edge between
\mathsf{A}
and
\mathsf{B}
denotes that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{B})
are incomparable. An undirected edge from
\mathsf{A}
to
\mathsf{B}
denotes
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{A})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{B})
. Edge labelled with a question mark indicate an open problem.

Theorem 5.16
.

Figure

5.3
is correct for all
\Sigma
where
|\Sigma|\geq 2
.

To prove this theorem, we consider pairs of language representations from
Figure

5.3
, and show that the corresponding relationship holds.
For readability, this proof is given its own section, and is split into a series of lemmas.

First, we give the definition of
quotients
.
It is known that regular languages are closed under left and right quotients.
We use this closure property in the proof of
Lemma

5.22
.

Definition 5.17
.

For a language
L\subseteq\Sigma^{*}
and a symbol
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
, the
right quotient of
L
by
\mathtt{a}
, denoted by
L\mathrel{/}\mathtt{a}
, is the language of all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
where
w\cdot\mathtt{a}\in L
.
Likewise, the
left quotient of
L
by
\mathtt{a}
, denoted by
L\mathrel{\backslash}\mathtt{a}
, is the language of all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
\mathtt{a}\cdot w\in L
.

Proof of
Theorem

5.16
.

The lemmas used to prove this theorem are as follows:

•

Lemma

5.18
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
.

•

Lemma

5.19
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.20
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.20
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.21
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.22
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.22
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.22
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.23
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{SERCQ})
.

•

Lemma

5.23
.
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}})
.

Since the pattern
xx
generates the non-regular language
\{ww\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}
, and the simple regular expression
\emptyset
is not expressible by a pattern,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
.
This further implies that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
.

Lemma 5.18
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
are incomparable.

Proof.

It is clear that
\{ww\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\}
is in
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and is not regular.
Furthermore, we know from
[
41
]
that the more general
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
cannot represent all regular languages.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and regular languages are incomparable.
∎

Lemma 5.19
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

Proof.

The inclusion
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
follows trivially from the definitions.
We now show that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT})\neq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathtt{a})\land(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathtt{b})
. Thus, we have that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\{w\mid w\in\Sigma^{*}\text{ and }|w|_{\mathtt{a}}\geq 1\text{ and }|w|_{\mathtt{b}}\geq 1\}.

Working towards a contradiction, assume that there exists
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
We first prove that
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{a}}=1
and
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{b}}=1
.
To show this, assume that
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{a}}=0
.
Then, consider the pattern substitution
\sigma
where
\sigma(x)=\mathtt{b}
for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
.
Hence, there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
such that
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=0
, a contradiction.
The same reasoning shows that
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{b}}\geq 1
.
Now, if
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{a}}>1
or
|\alpha|_{\mathtt{b}}>1
, then
\mathtt{ab}\notin\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
which is a contradiction.

We now have two possibilities for
\alpha
.
If the symbol
\mathtt{a}
appears before
\mathtt{b}
in
\alpha
, then
\mathtt{ba}\notin\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
.
If the symbol
\mathtt{b}
appears before
\mathtt{a}
in
\alpha
, then
\mathtt{ab}\notin\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
.
Since
\mathtt{ab},\mathtt{ba}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, we have reached a contradiction and therefore
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is not a pattern language.
∎

Lemma 5.20
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
, and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.

Proof.

From the definitions, we immediately can determine that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.
The fact that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\neq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\neq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
follows from the fact that
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
cannot represent all regular languages (see
[
41
]
).
∎

The following lemma is used to show that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.
We shall also use it in
Corollary

5.30
to prove
\mathsf{NP}
-completeness for the so-called
universality problem
for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Lemma 5.21
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. We have that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\Sigma^{*}
if and only if for at least one
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
, we have that
\varepsilon,\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

The only-if direction follows immediately, and therefore we focus on the if direction.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
is in structured normal form.
Since
\varepsilon\in\mathcal{L}(\psi)
, it follows that for all
i\in[n]
, the pattern
\alpha_{i}
is terminal-free.

We know that
\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, therefore
\mathtt{a}\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{i})
for each
i\in[n]
.
It follows that for any
\sigma
where
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
where
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=\mathtt{a}
, each
\alpha_{i}
has exactly one variable
x_{i}\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{i})
such that
\sigma(x_{i})=\mathtt{a}
and for all
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{i})\setminus\{x_{i}\}
, we have that
\sigma(y)=\varepsilon
.
Therefore, for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
we can define new substitution
\tau\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
, where
\tau(x_{i})=w
for all
i\in[n]
, and
\tau(y)=\varepsilon
for all
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)\setminus\{x_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
.
Thus,
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.
∎

Lemma 5.22
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

Proof.

Notice that for every
\alpha_{T}\in\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}]
, there is an equivalent
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Let
\alpha_{T}:=(\alpha,T)
, then let

\varphi_{\alpha_{T}}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)\land\bigwedge_{x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)}\bigl(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}T(x)\bigr).

It is clear from the definitions that
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})=\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{\alpha_{T}})
.
It therefore follows that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.
Therefore, if
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
, then the inclusion
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
immediately follows.
The rest of this proof focuses on showing
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

We know that there are regular languages that cannot be expressed in
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\neq\emptyset
: For example, consider
Lemma

5.21
and
(\mathtt{a}\lor\varepsilon)
.
Next, we show that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\neq\emptyset
.
Consider

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathtt{a})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathtt{b}\cdot y\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot y\cdot\mathtt{b}).

We can represent
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
as

\mathcal{L}(\varphi):=(\Sigma^{*}\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\Sigma^{*})\mathrel{\cap}(\Sigma^{*}\cdot\{\mathtt{b}\cdot u\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot u\cdot\mathtt{b}\mid u\in\Sigma^{*}\}\cdot\Sigma^{*}).

First, we show that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is not regular.
To the contrary, assume that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is regular.
Since regular languages are closed under intersection we have that

L_{1}:=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\mathrel{\cap}(\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{*}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{*}\cdot\mathtt{b})

is regular. Where, from the definition of
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, we have

L_{1}=\{\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Furthermore, since regular languages are closed under quotients, we have that
L_{2}
is regular where
L_{2}:=(L_{1}\mathrel{\backslash}\mathtt{b})\mathrel{/}\mathtt{b}
and consequently
L_{2}:=\{\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
This is a contradiction, since proving
L_{2}
is non-regular is straightforward exercise in the pumping lemma for regular languages (for example, see
[
54
]
).
Therefore, we can continue with the proof with the knowledge that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is not regular.

Assume there exists a regularly typed pattern language
\alpha_{T}:=(\alpha,T)
such that
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Further assume that
\alpha:=\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{n}
where
\alpha_{i}\in\Xi
for all
i\in[n]
.
We can make the assumption that
\alpha
is terminal free because every terminal symbol
\mathtt{a}
can be represented as a new variable
x_{\mathtt{a}}
with the regular type
T(x_{\mathtt{a}}):=\{\mathtt{a}\}
.

Since for all
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, we have that
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}\geq 1
, it follows that there is some
i\in[n]
such that for all
u\in T(\alpha_{i})
, we have that
|u|_{\mathtt{a}}\geq 1
.
Otherwise, there is a word
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
such that
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=0
.
This can be seen by picking a substitution
\tau
such that
\tau(\alpha)\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
and
|\tau(x)|_{\mathtt{a}}=0
for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
.
Note that
|\alpha|_{\alpha_{i}}=1
and there cannot exist
i^{\prime}\in[n]\setminus\{i\}
where for all
u\in T(\alpha_{i^{\prime}})
we have that
|u|_{\mathtt{a}}\geq 1
.
Otherwise,
\mathtt{abbb}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
would not be expressible.
We call
\alpha_{i}
the
\mathtt{a}
-keeper
.

Likewise, there must be some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
, where for all
v\in T(x)
we have that
|v|_{\mathtt{b}}\geq 1
.
But,
x
may not be unique, and
|\alpha|_{x}=1
does not necessarily hold.
We call such a variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
a
\mathtt{b}
-keeper
.
Let
j\in[n]
be the smallest (or left-most) position such that
\alpha_{j}
is a
\mathtt{b}
-keeper, and let
k\in[n]
be the largest (or right-most) position such that
\alpha_{k}
is a
\mathtt{b}
-keeper.

For any
i^{\prime}\in[n]
where
\alpha_{i^{\prime}}
is not the
\mathtt{a}
-keeper, nor a
\mathtt{b}
-keeper, then
\varepsilon\in T(\alpha_{i^{\prime}})
.
This is because we have shown there is exactly one
\mathtt{a}
-keeper, and there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
such that
|w|_{\mathtt{c}}=0
for all
\mathtt{c}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
.

We now look at some cases for the relative positions of
i
,
j
, and
k
, and prove a contradiction for each:

Case 1.

i<j
: For this case, we consider those
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
where
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=1

|w|_{\mathtt{b}}=3
and
|w|_{\mathtt{c}}=0
for all
\mathtt{c}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
. Due to the fact that
i<j
, we have that the
\mathtt{a}
-keeper appears to the right of the right-most
\mathtt{b}
-keeper.
Therefore, for any word in
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
, the
\mathtt{a}
-symbol must appear to the right of some
\mathtt{b}
-symbol. Thus,
\mathtt{abbb}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\setminus\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
.

Case 2.

i>k
: For this case, we again consider those
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
where
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=1

|w|_{\mathtt{b}}=3
and
|w|_{\mathtt{c}}=0
for all
\mathtt{c}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
.
Analogously to Case 1, the
\mathtt{a}
-symbol must come before some
\mathtt{b}
-symbol. It therefore follows that
\mathtt{bbba}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\setminus\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
.

Case 3.

j<i<k
:
Again, we consider those
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
where
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=1
,
|w|_{\mathtt{b}}=3
, and
|w|_{\mathtt{c}}=0
for all
\mathtt{c}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\}
.
For any
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
where
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}=1
, we have that the
\mathtt{a}
-symbol must come between two
\mathtt{b}
-symbols (analogously to Case 1 and Case 2). It therefore follows that
\mathtt{bbba}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\setminus\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
.

Case 4.

i=j=k
: Let
x=\alpha_{i}
.
We know that for all
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
where
y\neq x
, we have that
\varepsilon\in T(y)
since it is neither an
\mathtt{a}
-keeper, nor a
\mathtt{b}
-keeper.
We hence claim that
T(x)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
To prove this claim, let
w\in T(x)\setminus\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
We can then define a substitution
\sigma
such that
\sigma(x)=w
and
\sigma(y)=\varepsilon
for all
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
where
y\neq x
.
Consequently, for all
w\in T(x)
, we have that
|w|_{\mathtt{a}}\geq 1
and
|w|_{\mathtt{b}}\geq 3
.
Since otherwise,
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\neq\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})
.
We now claim that
T(x)\mathrel{\cap}(\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{*}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{*}\cdot\mathtt{b})=\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}
. To prove this claim, assume the contrary.
Then, there is some
w\in T(x)
where
w=\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{p}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot\mathtt{a}^{q}\cdot\mathtt{b}
where
p,q\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\neq p
. This is a contradiction, since then
T(x)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
does not hold.
Consequently,
T(x)
is not regular which is a contradiction.

Hence,
\mathcal{L}(\alpha_{T})\neq\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
and thus there does not exist a regularly typed pattern that can generate
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Consequently,
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
.
∎

The focus of the proof of
Lemma

5.22
is giving a language that is in
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
but is not in
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
.
This immediately implies the following:

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\mathrel{\#}\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
as
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
cannot represent all regular languages and we have given some
\mathcal{L}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\setminus\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
, and

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
as each regularly typed pattern language can be easily written as an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Lemma 5.23
.

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{SERCQ})
and
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}})
.

Proof.

The fact that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{SERCQ})
follows directly from
Proposition

5.10
and
Proposition

5.14
.
To show that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{core}})
, we consider
Theorem

4.29
which shows that any core spanner can be realized by a so-called
SpLog
-formula, and any
SpLog
-formula can be realized by a core spanner.
Furthermore, we can assume that any
SpLog
-formula is in DPC-normal form, which can also be considered a normal form of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
(see
Definition

4.30
).
∎

There are a couple of points of emphasis for
Lemma

5.23
.
Firstly, fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
reason over words and document spanners reason over spans, meaning that for a non-Boolean query, the resulting relations are not technically “equal”.
This first point is not of much importance for
Lemma

5.23
as we only consider Boolean queries.
Secondly, the connection between
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s is stronger than just generating the same class of languages: Conversions between the two models can be done in polynomial time.

5.3.1
Simulating Union

The reader may have noticed that
Figure

5.3
contains an edge labelled with a question mark between
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
.
While the author believes that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
is a strict subset of
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
, a proof not immediately obvious to the author.
This is because some restricted forms of union are expressible in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We now examine cases where
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
can express union.

Example 5.24
.

The language
L_{<}:=\{\mathtt{a}^{n}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}^{m}\mid n,m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }n<m\}
can be expressed in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
using the following formula:

\varphi_{<}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot y)\land(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}z\cdot x)\land(y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a}^{+})\land(z\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a}^{+}).

Furthermore, we can represent the language
L_{\neq}:=\{\mathtt{a}^{n}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}^{m}\mid n,m\in\mathbb{N}\text{ and }n\neq m\}
in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
using a union of two queries analogous to
\varphi_{<}
.
However, as we observe with the following query,
L_{\neq}
can be expressed in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y_{\mathtt{b},1}\cdot z\cdot y_{\mathtt{b},2}\cdot x)\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a}^{*}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}^{*})\land\\
(z\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a}^{+})\land(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{a}^{*})\land(y_{\mathtt{b}}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{\mathtt{b},1}\cdot y_{\mathtt{b},2})\land(y_{\mathtt{b}}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{b}).

The use of
y_{\mathtt{b}}
ensures that
y_{\mathtt{b},1}=\mathtt{b}
and
y_{\mathtt{b},2}=\varepsilon
, or vice versa.
Thus, the word
\sigma(z)
, for any satisfying substitution
\sigma
, appears one side of the
\mathtt{b}
symbol.
This ensures that
\mathtt{a}^{m}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{a}^{n}
and
m\neq n
.

In
Example

5.24
, we use concatenation and the fact that for any
w\in L_{\neq}
, we have that
|w|_{\mathtt{b}}=1
to express
L_{\neq}
in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
While there may be more simple
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s that generate the language
L_{\neq}
considered in
Example

5.24
, the query given demonstrates the complexity one can encode using concatenation.
We shall later see that this leads to undecidability for many decision problems.
Next, we consider classes of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
that allow for some form of union.

Peterfreund, ten Cate, Fagin, and Kimelfeld
[
90
]
showed that for unary alphabets,
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{RGX}^{\mathsf{gcore}})=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
.
This is observed from the fact that a generalized core spanner over a unary alphabet generates a so-called
semi-linear
language.
This allows us to make the following simple observation about the comparative expressive power of fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
over unary alphabets.

Observation 5.25
.

If
|\Sigma|=1
, then the following are equivalent

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}])
,

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
,

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
,

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])
, and

•

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
.

Proof.

From the definitions, we know

\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{PAT}[\mathsf{REG}])\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]).

Furthermore, for unary alphabets, generalized core spanners generate exactly the regular languages
[
90
]
.
From
[
41
]
we know that the class of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
languages is exactly the class of generalized core spanner languages.
Thus, for unary alphabets
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}])=\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})
.
This means that the inclusions all collapse, which concludes this proof as the stated observation follows directly.
∎

Therefore, if
|\Sigma|=1
, then for any
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
, there exists
\gamma\in\mathsf{REG}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
.
Since
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{REG})\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
it follows directly from
5.25
that there exists some
\psi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\psi)=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.

Next, we show a sufficient condition for
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
to hold for any alphabet size.
This is based on the condition that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is closed under so-called
single letter quotients
.
For a logic
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
, we say that
\mathcal{C}
is closed under
single letter quotients
if for any
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}
and any
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
, there exists
\varphi_{\mathrel{\backslash}\mathtt{a}}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\varphi_{\mathrel{/}\mathtt{a}}\in\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{\mathrel{\backslash}\mathtt{a}})=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\mathrel{\backslash}\mathtt{a}
and
\mathcal{L}(\varphi_{\mathrel{/}\mathtt{a}})=\mathcal{L}(\varphi)\mathrel{/}\mathtt{a}
.
Refer back to
Definition

5.17
for the definition of quotients.

The following proof is similar to the proof of Lemma 3.2 in
[
44
]
, where it was shown that that one can effectively construct a so-called CRPQ with equalities, from a union of H-systems.
Without going into details, an H-system is equivalent to a regularly typed pattern language, where each regular type must include the empty word.
Then, the resulting CRPQ with equalities encodes the language of a union of H-systems with “extra padding”.
The following proof uses a similar way to encode union by utilizing concatenation.

Proposition 5.26
.

If
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
is closed under single letter quotients, then
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
is strictly less expressive than
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
.

Proof.

We know that there are languages in
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
that are not in
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
, since
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
cannot express every regular language.
This follows from the fact that
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
cannot express all the regular languages
[
41
]
.

Now assume that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
is closed under single letter quotients.
For intuition, given
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
, we shall construct
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, such that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdot\mathtt{a}_{2}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot\mathcal{L}(\psi)\cdot\dagger\cdot\$,

where
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{a}_{1},\mathtt{a}_{2},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{m}\}
and we have the meta-symbols
\dagger,\$\notin\Sigma
, where
\dagger\neq\$
.
Therefore, if
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
is closed under single letter quotients, we can remove the “padding” from
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
to get a query
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
.

The rest of this proof is dedicated to showing how we construct such a query
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
from
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
.
Let
\psi:=\bigvee_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}
where
\psi_{i}
is a Boolean
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
for each
i\in[k]
.
We safely make the following assumptions about
\psi
:

1.

\psi_{i}
is in structured normal form for all
i\in[n]
(see
Lemma

5.13
), and

2.

\mathsf{vars}(\psi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\psi_{j})=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
.

We can assume the second assumption stated due to the fact that each
\psi_{i}
is Boolean, and thus the set of free variables of
\psi_{i}
is always empty for every
i\in[k]
.
Therefore, a simple renaming of variables for each
\psi_{i}
to a variable set unique to
\psi_{i}
does not change the semantics of the query.

We then define

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}^{\mathsf{sel}}\cdot x_{2}^{\mathsf{sel}}\cdots x_{k}^{\mathsf{sel}}\cdot\$\cdot x_{1}^{\mathsf{cod}}\cdot x_{2}^{\mathsf{cod}}\cdots x_{k}^{\mathsf{cod}}\cdot\$)\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}},

where each
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}
and
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}
are subformulas that shall be defined later, and

\gamma:=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdot\mathtt{a}_{2}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot\Sigma^{*}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$.

It is clear that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\psi)
if
w\in\mathcal{L}(\psi_{i})
for at least one
i\in[k]
.
We use a variable
x_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}
along with
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}
for each
i\in[k]
to “select” which
\psi_{i}
for
i\in[k]
we wish to use.
Then,
x_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}
along with
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}
is used to
encode
the query
\psi_{i}
.
Therefore, for any
w\in\mathcal{L}(\psi)
, we only need
w
to match for one such
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}
and
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}
pair.

For every
i\in[k]
, we define
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}
as follows:

\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}:=(x_{i}^{\mathsf{sel}}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot x_{i}^{\mathtt{a}_{1}}\cdot x_{i}^{\mathtt{a}_{2}}\cdots x_{i}^{\mathtt{a}_{m}}\cdot x_{i}^{\dagger})\land\bigl(x_{i}^{\dagger}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}(\dagger\lor\varepsilon)\bigr)\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}\bigl(x_{i}^{\mathtt{a}_{j}}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}(\mathtt{a}_{j}\lor\varepsilon)\bigr).

Note that for any substitution
\sigma
where
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
, there is at most one
i\in[k]
such that
\sigma(x_{i}^{\dagger})=\dagger
, and for all
j\in[k]\setminus\{i\}
, we have that
\sigma(x_{j}^{\dagger})=\varepsilon
.
This is because
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})\in\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
must hold, and therefore
|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|_{\dagger}=4
.
However, if
\sigma(x_{j}^{\dagger})=\sigma(x_{i}^{\dagger})=\dagger
where
i\neq j
, then
|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|_{\dagger}\geq 8
.
Analogously, there is at least one
i\in[k]
such that
\sigma(x_{i}^{\dagger})\neq\varepsilon
.

For each
i\in[k]
let
\psi_{i}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\bigwedge_{j=1}^{l_{i}}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i,j})
, and let
\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}
be defined by

\psi_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}:=\bigwedge_{j=1}^{l_{i}}(x_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{\dagger}\cdot\bar{\alpha}_{i,j}\cdot x_{i}^{\dagger})\land\bigl(x_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}((\dagger\cdot\Sigma^{*}\cdot\dagger)\lor\varepsilon)\bigr),

where
\bar{\alpha}_{i,j}:=h(\alpha_{i,j})
for the partial morphism
h
defined as
h(x)=x_{i}
for all
x\in\Xi
, where
x_{i}
is a new variable, unique for
x
and
i\in[k]
, and
h(\mathtt{a})=x^{\mathtt{a}}_{i}
for
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
.

Correctness.

We now prove that
v\in\mathcal{L}(\psi)
if and only if
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
where

w=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot v\cdot\dagger\cdot\$.

If direction.

Since for any
\sigma
where
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
we have that
\sigma(x_{i}^{\dagger})\neq\varepsilon
for exactly one
i\in[k]
, it follows that
\sigma(x_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}})\neq\varepsilon
, and
\sigma(x_{j}^{\mathsf{cod}})=\varepsilon
for all
j\in[n]\setminus\{i\}
.
Thus,
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot v\cdot\dagger\cdot\$
where
v\in\mathcal{L}(\psi_{i})
for at least one
i\in[k]
.

Only if direction.

For any
v\in\mathcal{L}(\psi_{i})
where
i\in[k]
, let

w=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot v\cdot\dagger\cdot\$.

Then, there is a substitution
\sigma
where
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
such that
\sigma(x_{i}^{\mathsf{cod}})=\dagger v\dagger
.

Thus

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\dagger\cdot\mathtt{a}_{1}\cdot\mathtt{a}_{2}\cdots\mathtt{a}_{m}\cdot\dagger\cdot\$\cdot\dagger\cdot\mathcal{L}(\psi)\cdot\dagger\cdot\$,

where
\Sigma=\{\mathtt{a}_{1},\mathtt{a}_{2},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{m}\}
and
\dagger,\$\notin\Sigma
, where
\dagger\neq\$
are meta symbols.
Consequently, if indeed
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is closed under single letter quotients, then there exists some
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi^{\prime})=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
.
∎

In this section, we have considered the expressive power of many fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
, with a focus on conjunctive query fragments.
The results of this section have been summarized in
Figure

5.3
.
The biggest open problem from this section is whether
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})
is a strict subclass of
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.

The author believes that
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
, however, a proof is not immediately obvious.
To demonstrate this, we have given cases for which
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
can simulate union using properties of combinatorics on words, namely via concatenation (for example, see
Proposition

5.26
).

It is even open as to whether
\llbracket\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}\rrbracket\subset\llbracket\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}\rrbracket
.
That is, does there exist a query (perhaps a non-Boolean query) that can be expressed in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
but not in
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
While
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
implies that
\llbracket\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}\rrbracket\subset\llbracket\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}\rrbracket
, the converse may not hold.

5.4
Decision Problems and Tradeoffs

We now consider the decidability and complexity of fundamental decision problems for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
,
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
, and related models.
One of our main focuses is on
static analysis problems
, which have important implications for query optimization.
Many of these static analysis problems are intractable or undecidable.
Consequently, certain query optimizations, such as
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
query minimization, are not possible.

Table

5.1
gives a summary of the complexity and decidability results from this section.

It is easily observed that model checking for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete by considering the membership problem for pattern languages
[
30
]
and
\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha
.
However, we show that this holds even for rather restricted cases.
One may think that simply restricting the query to a weakly acyclic
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, restricting the class of word equations to very simple word equations, or restricting the size of the input word would result in tractable model checking.
We prove in
Theorem

5.28
that for each of these cases, we still hit
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness.

But first, we define the class of
regular patterns
for which the membership problem (is
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
?) can be solved in linear time
[
104
]
.

\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}

\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}

\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}

Model checking

\mathsf{NP}
-c (
5.28
)

\mathsf{NP}
-c (
5.28
)

\mathsf{NP}
-c
[
37
]

Satisfiability

\mathsf{NP}
-h (
5.31
)

\mathsf{PSPACE}
-c (
5.31
)

\mathsf{PSPACE}
-c
[
41
]

Universality

\mathsf{NP}
-c (
5.30
)

Undec. (
5.35
)

Undec.
[
37
]

Regularity

Undec. (
5.36
)

Undec. (
5.35
)

Undec.
[
41
]

Equivalence

Undec. (
5.37
)

Undec. (
5.37
)

Undec.
[
41
]

Table 5.1
:
A summarization of results from
Section

5.4
and previous research. We use “
\mathsf{A}
-h” to mean
\mathsf{A}
-hard, and “
\mathsf{A}
-c” to mean
\mathsf{A}
-complete. The numbers next to each of these results are a reference to the corresponding result in this thesis. For example, (
5.28
) is shorthand for
Theorem

5.28
.

Definition 5.27
.

A pattern
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
is a
regular pattern
if we have
|\alpha|_{x}=1
for every variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
.
An
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
consists only of regular patterns if it has a body of the form
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
where
\alpha_{i}
is a regular pattern for each
i\in[n]
.

It is clear that we can construct an equivalent regular expression from a regular pattern by replacing each occurrence of a variable by
\Sigma^{*}
.
Consequently, the regular patterns enjoy desirable algorithmic properties.

Model checking for Regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for words of length one (refer back to
Section

5.1
for more details).
However, as we will now observe, this also holds for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
Furthermore, model checking is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for weakly acyclic
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s consisting only of regular patterns.

Theorem 5.28
.

Model checking for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, and remains
\mathsf{NP}
-hard even if one of the following conditions hold

1.

the query is weakly acyclic,

2.

the query consists only of regular patterns, or

3.

the input word is of length one.

Proof.

The upper bound for model checking follows immediately from the upper bound for
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
, see
[
41
]
. Thus, we focus on the
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness proofs for each of the restricted class of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s considered in the Theorem’s statement.

Given
\alpha\in\Xi^{*}
and
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, deciding whether there is a substitution
\sigma\colon\Xi^{*}\to\Sigma^{*}
with
\sigma(\alpha)=w
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete (see Ehrenfeucht and Rozenberg
[
30
]
).
Thus, model checking for weakly acyclic
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s of the form
\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard.

From Theorem 4 of
[
18
]
, it is known that the membership problem is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for patterns of the form
\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2}
with
\mathsf{vars}(\beta_{1})=\mathsf{vars}(\beta_{2})
and
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}
being regular patterns.
Thus, model checking is
\mathsf{NP}
-hard for queries of the form

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot\beta_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta_{1}\cdot z_{2}),

where
z_{1},z_{2}\in\Xi\setminus\mathsf{vars}(\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2})
.
Furthermore, it is clear that since
\beta_{1}
and
\beta_{2}
are regular patterns, that
\varphi
consists only of regular patterns.

We now consider the less straightforward proof on the lower bounds for model checking when the input word is of length one.
To prove this, we reduce from the
\mathsf{NP}
-complete problem of
1-in-3-SAT
.
An instance of 1-in-3 SAT consists of a conjunction of clauses
P:=C_{1}\land C_{2}\land\dots\land C_{m}
and a set of variables
\{y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{k}\}
.

Each clause
C_{i}
is defined as a disjunction of exactly three literals, where each literal is either
y_{i}
or
\neg y_{i}
for
i\in[k]
.
A satisfying assignment for
P
is an assignment
\tau\colon\{y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
that satisfies
P
, and exactly one literal in every clause is evaluated to
1
(every other literal must evaluate to
0
).

Given an instance
P:=C_{1}\land C_{2}\land\dots\land C_{m}
of 1-in-3-SAT with
k
variables, we let
\sigma(\mathfrak{u}):=\mathtt{a}
for some
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
, and we construct
\varphi_{P}
as follows:

\varphi_{P}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{y_{i}}^{t}\cdot x_{y_{i}}^{f})\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\bigl((\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{C_{i}})\land(x_{C_{i}}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{C_{i}}\cdot z_{C_{i}}^{\prime}\cdot z_{C_{i}}^{\prime\prime})\bigr),

where
z_{C_{i}}=x_{y_{j}}^{t}
if
y_{j}
appears in
C_{i}
, and
z_{C_{i}}=x_{y_{j}}^{f}
if
\neg y_{j}
appears in
C_{i}
.
The variables
z_{C_{i}}^{\prime}
and
z_{C_{i}}^{\prime\prime}
are defined analogously.
Next, we prove that
\sigma\models\varphi_{P}
if and only if
P
is satisfiable.

Assume there exists a substitution
\tau
such that
