uses these variables (which are not mapped to the empty-word if
i
is the selected error for
\sigma
) to encode some error that prohibits
\sigma(\beta_{i})
from being in
\mathsf{VALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

Correctness.

For this correctness proof, we show that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi):=0\#0\#^{3}\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})\#^{3}.

In other words,
0\#0\#^{3}w\#^{3}\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
if and only if
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

If direction:

Let
v=0\#0\#^{3}w\#^{3}
where
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.
Since
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
, the word must contain at least one error.
For every possible
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
, we have some
i\in[\mu]
such that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\beta_{i},T_{i})
.
Thus, there exists
i\in[\mu]
and
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
where
i
is the selected error for
\sigma
and

\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot\sigma(\beta_{i})\cdot\#^{3},

and
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=v
.
It follows that
\sigma(\alpha_{i^{\prime}})=\varepsilon
for all
i^{\prime}\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
.
Thus, we have dealt with one direction for the correctness proof.

Only if direction:

Let
v\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
From the structure of
\varphi
, we know that
v=0\#0\#^{3}w\#^{3}
for some
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.
Furthermore, we know that for any substitution
\sigma
where
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
such that
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=v
, we have that
\#^{3}w\#^{3}=\sigma(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})
.

Let
v=0\#0\#^{3}w\#^{3}
where
v\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
.
Let
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
where
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=v
, and let
i\in[\mu]
be the selected error for
\sigma
.
We now look at two cases.

•

Case 1.
For all
j\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
, we have that
\sigma(\alpha_{j})=\varepsilon
.

•

Case 2.
There exists
j\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
such that
\sigma(\alpha_{j})\neq\varepsilon
.

Recall that since
i
is the selected error for
\sigma
, we have that
\sigma(x_{0,i})=0
,
\sigma(x_{\#,i})=\#
, and
z_{i,r}\in 0^{+}
for
r\in[4]
.

Case 1:

For all
j\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
, we have
\sigma(\alpha_{j})=\varepsilon
.
Therefore,

•

\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\sigma(z)
,

•

\sigma(z)=\sigma(\alpha_{i})
, and

•

\sigma(\alpha_{i})=\#^{3}\cdot\sigma(\beta_{i})\cdot\#^{3}
.

Consequently,
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})=0\#0\#^{3}\cdot\sigma(\beta_{i})\cdot\#^{3}
where
\sigma(\beta_{i})\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

Case 2:

Let
j\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
such that
\sigma(\alpha_{j})\neq\varepsilon
.
For any
\sigma\models\mathsf{body}(\varphi)
, we know that:

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathfrak{u})

\displaystyle=0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot v\cdot\#^{3},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathfrak{u})

\displaystyle=0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\sigma(z),

where
\sigma(z)=\sigma(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{i}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})
.
Thus,

\sigma(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{i-1})\cdot\sigma(\alpha_{i})\cdot\sigma(\alpha_{i+1}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})=\#^{3}\cdot v\cdot\#^{3}.

Recall that
i
is the selected error for
\sigma
and therefore
\sigma(\alpha_{i})\in\#^{3}\cdot\Sigma^{*}\cdot\#^{3}
.
Because there exists some
j\in[\mu]\setminus\{i\}
where
\sigma(\alpha_{j})\neq\varepsilon
, we have that

w_{1}\cdot\underbrace{\#^{3}\cdot\sigma(\beta_{i})\cdot\#^{3}}_{\sigma(\alpha_{i})}\cdot w_{2}=\#^{3}\cdot v\cdot\#^{3},

where
\sigma(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{i-1})=w_{1}
and
\sigma(\alpha_{i+1}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})=w_{2}
, and
w_{1}\cdot w_{2}\neq\varepsilon
.
The rest of the proof for this direction shows that
v\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

•

Let
|w_{1}|>3
. Then,
w_{1}=\#^{3}w_{1}^{\prime}
where
w_{1}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{+}
. It therefore must hold that
\sigma(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{i})=\#^{3}w_{1}^{\prime}\#^{3}\sigma(\beta_{i})\#^{3}
, and thus we have a structural error due to the occurrence of
\#^{3}
in
w
.

•

Let
|w_{2}|>3
. Then,
w_{2}=w_{2}^{\prime}\#^{3}
where
w_{2}^{\prime}\in\Sigma^{+}
. Thus, we have that
\sigma(\alpha_{i}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})=\#^{3}\sigma(\beta_{i})\#^{3}w_{2}^{\prime}\#^{3}
, and we again have a structural error.

•

Let
|w_{1}|\leq 3
and
|w_{2}|\leq 3
. Then,
\sigma(\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{\mu})=\#^{k_{1}}\cdot\sigma(\beta_{i})\cdot\#^{k_{2}}
for some
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 3
, where
\sigma(\beta_{i})\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.
Therefore,
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

Thus, we have shown that
v\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
where
v=0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot w\cdot\#^{3}
if and only if
w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
.

To conclude, given an extended Turing machine
\mathcal{X}
, we construct
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi):=\{0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot w\cdot\#^{3}\mid w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})\}.

Since regular languages are closed under quotients, it is thus clear that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is regular if and only if
\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})
is regular.
Therefore, observing
Lemma

5.32
, the regularity problem for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is undecidable.
∎

From the proof of
Theorem

5.36
, we can conclude:

Corollary 5.37
.

The following problems are undecidable

•

\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
equivalence,

•

deciding whether an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is equivalent to a fixed pattern, and

•

deciding whether an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is equivalent to a fixed regular expression.

Proof.

The proof of
Theorem

5.36
constructs
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
from a given extended Turing machine
\mathcal{X}
, such that

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\{0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot w\cdot\#^{3}\mid w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})\}.

Furthermore, from
Lemma

5.32
, deciding whether
\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})=\Sigma^{*}
is undecidable.
Since we can easily construct an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, a pattern, or a regular expression that generates the language
0\#0\#^{3}\Sigma^{*}\#^{3}
, the stated corollary holds.
∎

While these undecidability results are themselves of interest, in the subsequent section, we consider their implications with regards to query optimization.

5.4.2
Undecidability and Query Optimization

Let us now consider some of the consequences of the aforementioned undecidability results.
For each of the following results, we give a reference to the undecidability result for which it is a consequence.

To examine the problem of
query minimization
, we first must discuss what complexity measure we wish to minimize for.
Instead of giving a explicit measure, such as the length of the query, we give the more general definition of a
complexity measure
.

Definition 5.38
.

Let
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be a class of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
A
complexity measure
for
\mathcal{C}
is a recursive function
c\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
such that the set of queries
\mathcal{C}
can be enumerated in increasing order, and for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist finitely many
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}
with
c(\varphi)=n
.

As a consequence of
Theorem

5.36
, we have that there is no algorithm that can minimize an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Corollary 5.39
.

Let
c
be a complexity measure for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
There is no algorithm that given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, constructs
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
and
\psi
is
c
-minimal.

Proof.

Consider
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
that was constructed in the proof of
Theorem

5.36
from an extended Turing machine.
We remind the reader that for a given extended Turing machine
\mathcal{X}
, we construct
\varphi
such that:

\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\{0\cdot\#\cdot 0\cdot\#^{3}\cdot w\cdot\#^{3}\mid w\in\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})\}.

Now consider the following query:

\psi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}0\#0\#^{3}x\#^{3}.

From
\psi
and
\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
if and only if
\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})=\Sigma^{*}
.
As determining whether
\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{M})=\Sigma^{*}
is undecidable (recall
Lemma

5.32
), it follows that deciding whether
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
is undecidable.

Since
\psi
is fixed, the set of
c
-minimal queries
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\psi)=\mathcal{L}(\psi^{\prime})
is finite, there exists a recursive function to find such a
c
-minimal query
\psi^{\prime}
.
Now assume that there exists an algorithm
P
that given
\varphi
, gives an equivalent
\varphi^{\prime}
such that
c(\varphi^{\prime})
is minimal.
Then, there would be an algorithm that determines whether
\mathsf{INVALC}(\mathcal{X})=\Sigma^{*}
by checking whether
\varphi^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\psi^{\prime}
.
Consequently, the assumption that
P
exists cannot hold.
∎

Given the complexity measures
c_{1}
for a language generator
\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
c_{2}
for a language generator
\mathcal{F}_{2}
, we say that there is a
non-recursive trade-off
from
\mathcal{F}_{1}
to
\mathcal{F}_{2}
if for every recursive function
f\colon\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, there exists
\varphi\in\mathcal{F}_{1}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{2}
such that
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)=\mathcal{L}(\psi)
, but
c_{1}(\varphi)\geq f(c_{2}(\psi))
.
Hartmanis’ meta theorem
[
52
]
allows us to draw conclusions about the relative succinctness of models from certain undecidability results.
Thus, we can conclude the following.

Proposition 5.40
.

The trade-off from
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s to regular expressions is non-recursive.

Proof.

This proof follows from Hartmanis’ meta theorem
[
52
]
which states the following:
For two systems
A
and
B
of representations, given a representation
r\in B
, if it is not co-semi-decidable whether there exists an equivalent representation
r^{\prime}\in A
, then there is a non-recursive trade-off from
B
to
A
. See
[
68
]
for more details.

From the proof of
Theorem

5.36
we know that determining whether
\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
is regular for a given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is not decidable, semi-decidable, or co-semi-decidable.
Thus, we can invoke Hartmanis’ meta theorem, to determine that there is a non-recursive trade-off from
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to regular expressions.
∎

Less formally,
Proposition

5.40
states that even for those
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s that generate a regular language, the size blowup from the
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to the regular expression that accepts the same language is not bound by any recursive function.

It was shown in Theorem 4.10 of
[
38
]
that the trade-off from core spanners to regular spanners is non-recursive.
Then, in
[
41
]
it was shown that the trade-off from
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
-formulas to regular expressions is non-recursive.
In this section, we have shown that even for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, the trade-off to regular expressions is non-recursive.

5.4.3
Split-Correctness for FC-CQs

In certain cases, one may not wish to query the whole input document.
Instead, it can be advantageous to first split the document into sections, and then perform a query on each of these sections.
For example, if we wanted to query an email conversation, it would be wasteful to query the whole conversation when simply querying the individual emails is sufficient.

We adapt the formal definition of information extraction on split documents given in
[
25
]
.
That is, first a so-called
splitter
converts an input word into a set of factors of the input word, then the resulting relation is the union of a query performed on each of these factors.
We shall give a formal definition of this process.

This brings up many intriguing questions regarding querying “split documents”.
In particular, we look at the static analysis problems of whether we get the same result if we first split the document and then query the smaller segments individually, or query the whole document.
In
[
25
]
, these questions were introduced and explored with regards to
regular spanners.
With regards to the relational setting, parallel correctness has been considered in
[
5
,
4
]
.
The complexity of various decision problems regarding this so-called
split-correctness
when incorporating equalities were left open.

Using
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
as both queries and splitters, we show that so-called
splittabillity
,
split-correctness
, and
self-splittability
are all undecidable.
To show this, we reduce from the
inclusion problem for pattern languages
over fixed alphabets
[
42
]
.

w
w_{1}
w_{2}
\vdots
w_{k}
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w_{1})
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w_{2})
\vdots
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w_{k})
\llbracket\varphi\circ\psi\rrbracket(w)
\mathcal{L}(\psi,w)

Figure 5.5
:
An illustration of querying a split document using
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. In this figure, we use
\psi
as the splitter, and
\varphi
as the query.

We call
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
a
splitter
if
\varphi
is a unary query (that is,
|\mathsf{free}(\varphi)|=1
).
It follows that
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
produces a set of subwords.
Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and assume that
\mathsf{free}(\varphi)=\{x\}
.
Then, for each
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we define
language of
\varphi
over
w
and denote it by
\mathcal{L}(\varphi,w):=\{u\mid\sigma\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)\text{ and }\sigma(x)=u\}
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and let
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be a splitter.
We write
\llbracket\varphi\circ\psi\rrbracket(w)
to denote
\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{L}(w,\psi)}\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(v)
.
If
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)=\llbracket\varphi^{\prime}\circ\psi\rrbracket(w)
for all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, then we simply write
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\psi
.
For an illustration, see
Figure

5.5
.
Note that if
\mathcal{L}(\psi,w)=\emptyset
for some splitter
\psi
, then
\llbracket\varphi\circ\psi\rrbracket(w)=\emptyset
for any
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, and any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.

We now define decision problems for parallel-correctness of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
The decision problem definitions given here are based on the definitions given in
[
25
]
, however, as we work with
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and not document spanners, the definitions differ slightly.

Definition 5.41
.

For
\varphi,\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and a splitter
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we define the following decision problems.

1.

Split-correctness.
Does
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}\circ\psi
?

2.

Self-splittability
is a special case of split-correctness where
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}
.

3.

Splittability.
Does there exists
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\circ\psi
?

For patterns
\alpha,\beta\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
where
|\Sigma|=2
, it is undecidable to determine whether
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, see
[
42
]
.
Using this undecidability result, we get the following result:

Theorem 5.42
.

Each of splittability, split-correctness, and self-splittability for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is undecidable.

Proof.

We prove that both self-splittability and splittability are undecidable by reducing from the pattern inclusion problem.

Self-Splittability is undecidable.

We reduce from the inclusion problem for pattern languages over a fixed alphabet, which is undecidable
[
42
]
.
For
|\Sigma|=2
, we know that deciding whether
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
for
\alpha,\beta\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
is undecidable.
Thus, we define a splitter
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and a query
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
as follows:

\displaystyle\psi:=\mathsf{Ans}(x)\leftarrow

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta),

\displaystyle\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha).

If there exists
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\setminus\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, then
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
and
\mathcal{L}(w,\psi)=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
\varphi\neq\varphi\circ\psi
.
If
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, then for all
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
, we have that
\mathcal{L}(w,\psi)=\{w\}
.
Therefore,
\varphi=\varphi\circ\psi
.
Thus,
\varphi=\varphi\circ\psi
if and only if
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
.
Consequently, self-splittability is undecidable.

Since self-splittability is a special case of split-correctness, it follows that split-correctness is undecidable.

Splittability is undecidable.

Let
\varphi_{1}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
and let
\psi:=\mathsf{Ans}(x)\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\beta)
.
If
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, then
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)=\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\mathrel{\cap}\mathcal{L}(\beta)
.
Therefore, let
\varphi_{2}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
.
It follows that if
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, then there exists
\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket(w)=\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{L}(\psi,w)}\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket(v)
for all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
.

If
\mathcal{L}(\alpha)\not\subseteq\mathcal{L}(\beta)
, then there exists some
w\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
w\in\mathcal{L}(\varphi)
and
\mathcal{L}(w,\psi)=\emptyset
.
Therefore,
\varphi_{1}=\varphi_{2}\circ\psi
cannot hold for any
\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
This is because
\llbracket\varphi_{1}\rrbracket(w)\neq\emptyset
, but
\bigcup_{v\in\mathcal{L}(w,\psi)}\llbracket\varphi_{2}\rrbracket(v)=\emptyset
for any
\varphi_{2}\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
∎

While it may seem that certain ways of splitting the document would give tractable evaluation of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, even if one assumes the “strongest” possible splitter, that splits the document into single characters,
Theorem

5.28
shows that under combined complexity we still hit
\mathsf{NP}
-hardness.
Therefore, for tractable evaluation of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s over a split document, the structure of the
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
would also need to be taken into account.

5.4.4
Ambiguity

One key difference between
\mathsf{FC}
and first-order logic is that an
\mathsf{FC}
formula materializes the relations it works with from the query and the input word, whereas for relational first-order logic, the relations are considered part of the input.
Due to the unbounded arity of word equations, the number of tuples in the materialized relation may be exponential.
Consequently, model checking can be intractable, even if the underlying query is tractable (refer back to
Theorem

5.28
).
This raises the following question: For which
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s is the resulting relation small?

In the present section, we consider the problem of whether an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
always materializes a constant size output relation with respect to the length of the input word.
We show that deciding whether an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
materializes a constant size relation is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete.

Definition 5.43
.

We say that a pattern
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
is
ambiguous
if there exists substitutions
\sigma,\tau
such that
\sigma(\alpha)=\tau(\alpha)
but
\sigma(x)\neq\tau(x)
for some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
.
A pattern is
unambiguous
if it is not ambiguous.
Likewise,
\alpha
is finitely-ambiguous if for every
w\in\mathcal{L}(\alpha)
, the number of pattern substitutions
\sigma
such that
\sigma(\alpha)=w
is constant; that is, ignoring those substitutions that are equal for the variables that appear in
\alpha
, but are different for variables that do not appear in
\alpha
.

The definition for unambiguous patterns and finitely ambiguous patterns follows from
[
80
]
. More details on finite degrees of ambiguity in pattern languages can be found in this work. Recall
Section

3.3
for related literature on ambiguity.

Example 5.44
.

Let
\alpha:=x_{1}\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot x_{1}\cdot x_{2}
, and let
w:=\mathtt{ababababb}
.
Consider two substitutions
\tau,\sigma
such that
\tau(\alpha)=\sigma(\alpha)=w
, but
\tau\neq\sigma
.
Let
\tau(x_{1})=\mathtt{ab}
and
\tau(x_{2})=\mathtt{abb}
. Now, let
\sigma(x_{1})=\varepsilon
and
\sigma(x_{2})=\mathtt{abababb}
.

\displaystyle w=

\displaystyle\overbrace{\mathtt{ab}}^{\tau(x_{1})}\cdot\mathtt{ab}\cdot\overbrace{\mathtt{ab}}^{\tau(x_{1})}\cdot\overbrace{\mathtt{abb}}^{\tau(x_{2})},

\displaystyle w=

\displaystyle\underbrace{\varepsilon}_{\sigma(x_{1})}\cdot\mathtt{ab}\cdot\underbrace{\varepsilon}_{\sigma(x_{1})}\cdot\underbrace{\mathtt{abababb}}_{\sigma(x_{2})}.

Thus,
\alpha
is an ambiguous pattern.

We generalize the idea of ambiguity to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Definition 5.45
.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
For any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and any substitution
\sigma\in\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
, we assume that
\mathsf{Dom}(\varphi)=\mathsf{free}(\varphi)
.
We say that
\varphi
is
unambiguous
if
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|\in\{0,1\}
for every
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, and is
ambiguous
otherwise.
For all
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, if
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|
is finite, then
\varphi
is
finitely-ambiguous
, and
if
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|\leq k
for some fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\varphi
is
k
-ambiguous.

It may seem that simply restricting the number of variables in a pattern could guarantee a certain degree of ambiguity.
However, as shown in
[
80
]
, this is not the case.

Theorem 5.46

(Mateescu and Salomaa
[
80
]
)
.

For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
there exists a pattern
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
such that
|\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)|=k
and
\alpha
is unambiguous.

While
[
80
]
considers patterns, this result carries over to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
immediately by considering queries of the form
\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
.

We can encode the problem of determining whether
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
k
-ambiguous as a formula in
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
.
To do so, we make
k
copies of
\varphi
with the variables of each copy being renamed.
Then, we see whether
\varphi
conjuncted each of the
k
copies is satisfiable when at least two copies “disagree” on same variable.
In the formula we give to encode this problem, we use the shorthand
x\neq y
to show that the two variables represent different words.
Consequently, utilizing the fact that satisfiability for
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete, we can show the following result (see Theorem 7 in
[
80
]
for an analogous result for pattern ambiguity).

Theorem 5.47
.

For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, deciding if
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
k
-ambiguous is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
be inputs.
From
\varphi
, we construct
\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
for every
i\in[k+1]
, where
\varphi_{i}
is constructed by taking
\varphi
and replacing each variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)
with
x_{i}\in\Xi\setminus\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)
, a variable that is unique to
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)
and
i\in[k+1]
.
Then, construct
\psi\in\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
as follows:

\psi:=\exists\vec{x}\colon\bigl(\varphi\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}(\varphi_{i}\land\bigvee_{x\in\mathsf{free}(\varphi)}(x\neq x_{i}))\land\bigwedge_{i\in[k+1]}\,\bigwedge_{j\in[k+1]\setminus\{i\}}\,\bigvee_{x\in\mathsf{free}(\varphi)}(x_{i}\neq x_{j})\bigr),

where
\vec{x}
is the vector of quantified variables from
\varphi
, and the corresponding variables for
\varphi_{i}
for all
i\in[k+1]
.
We use inequality in
\psi
as syntactic sugar, as inequality can be expressed in
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
, see
[
37
]
.
If
\psi
is satisfiable, then we have the pattern substitutions
\sigma,\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{k}
such that
\sigma\models\varphi
and
\sigma_{i}\models\varphi_{i}
for each
i\in[k]
. Furthermore, for some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\varphi)
, we have that
\sigma(x)\neq\sigma_{i}(x_{i})
for all
i\in[k+1]
, and for each
i,j\in[k+1]
where
i\neq j
, there is some
x_{i},x_{j}\in\mathsf{vars}(\psi)
such that
\sigma_{i}(x_{i})\neq\sigma_{j}(x_{j})
.
Since
\varphi
and
\varphi_{i}
are equivalent up to renaming for all
i\in[k+1]
, we have that
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|>k
for some
w\in\Sigma^{*}
and thus
\varphi
is not
k
-ambiguous.
Deciding the satisfiability of
\psi
is clearly in
\mathsf{PSPACE}
with respect to
|\psi|
since
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
satisfiability is in
\mathsf{PSPACE}

[
38
,
37
]
.
Furthermore,
|\psi|
is polynomial in the size of
\varphi
, since we consider
k
“copies” of
\varphi
, and we consider every pair of corresponding variables from these copies, and therefore it is clear that
\psi
can be constructed in polynomial time.

We now consider the lower bounds by reducing from the
intersection problem for regular expressions
to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}

1
-ambiguity.
That is, given a set
S:=\{\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
of regular expressions, deciding whether
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{i})=\emptyset
is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete
[
66
]
.
Now consider the following
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
:

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(x_{1})\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1})\land\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{i}).

We have two possibilities:

1.

There does not exist
u\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u\in\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{i})
for all
i\in[k]
. Hence,
\varphi
is not satisfiable. Thus, for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we have that
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|=0
and
\varphi
is 1-ambiguous.

2.

There exists
u\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u\in\mathcal{L}(\gamma_{i})
for all
i\in[k]
. Hence,
\varphi
is satisfiable for any
w\in\Sigma^{*}
where
w=p\cdot u\cdot s
for some
p,s\in\Sigma^{*}
. Thus,
|\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)|
is quadratic in the size of
w
since we return every factor
w
and hence,
\varphi
is not 1-ambiguous.

It therefore follows that if we can determine whether
\varphi
is ambiguous, we can also determine whether there does not exist
u\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
u
is accepted by all regular expressions in the given set
S
.
∎

In this section, we have defined ambiguity for
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
.
We proved that it is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete to deciding whether an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
k
-ambiguous.
Further research in the area of ambiguity for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
could have important insights into reducing the size of intermediate tables when enumerating results.
This could be useful for finding tractable fragments of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, and for heuristic query optimization.

Chapter 6
Splitting FC Atoms

Syntactic restrictions on relational conjunctive queries have been incredibly fruitful for finding tractable fragments.
Recall Yannakakis’ algorithm (see
Definition

4.11
or
[
109
]
) which performs model checking for acyclic conjunctive queries in polynomial time.
Further research on the complexity of acyclic conjunctive queries
[
50
]
and the enumeration of results for acyclic conjunctive queries
[
9
]
has shown the efficacy of this restriction. On the other hand, for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and document spanners, such syntactic restrictions are yet to unlock tractable fragments.
Model checking for regex
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for acyclic queries (see
Theorem

5.2
), and model checking for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete for weakly acyclic queries (see
Theorem

5.28
).

The relation defined by a word equation can have an exponential number of tuples.
It is therefore intractable to simply materialize the relation for each atom in an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and apply usual algorithms for acyclic
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s.
This is due to the unbounded arity of word equations.
However, an
\mathsf{FC}
atom can be considered shorthand for a concatenation term.
For example, the word equation
y\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{4}
can be represented as
y\mathbin{\dot{=}}f(f(x_{1},x_{2}),f(x_{3},x_{4}))
where
f
denotes binary concatenation.
This lends itself to the “decomposition” of the word equation into a
{\mathsf{CQ}}
consisting of smaller word equations.
We can express the above word equation as
(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{4})
.
For such a
decomposition
, the relations defined by each word equation can be stored in linear space and we can enumerate them with constant delay.
Thus, if the resulting query is acyclic, then the tractability properties of acyclic conjunctive queries directly translate to the
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
by materializing the relations and then applying the usual acyclic
{\mathsf{CQ}}
algorithms.

In this chapter, we define the decomposition of an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
into a
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, where
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
denotes the set of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s where the right-hand side of each word equation is of at most length two.
Our first main result is a polynomial-time algorithm that decides whether a pattern can be decomposed into an acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
(
Section

6.1
).
Building on this result, we give a polynomial-time algorithm that decides whether an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
can be decomposed into an acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
(
Section

6.2
).
While both of these are decision problems, each algorithm constructs an acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
in polynomial time, if one exists.

As soon as we have an acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, the upper bound results for model checking and enumeration of results follow from previous work on relational acyclic
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s
[
50
,
9
]
.
This chapter mainly focuses on
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s (i. e., no regular constraints) due to the fact that we can add regular constraints for “free”.
This is because regular constraints are unary predicates, and therefore can be easily incorporated into a join tree.
The work in this chapter therefore defines a class of
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s for which model checking can be solved in polynomial time, and results can be enumerated with polynomial delay (both in terms of combined complexity).

To conclude this chapter, we generalize the process of decomposing patterns into
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s to decomposing patterns into acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s with a right-hand side of at most length
k
, which we denote with
k\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We give sufficient criteria for patterns to be decomposed into acyclic
k\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and show that decomposing a pattern into an acyclic
k\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
using this criteria can be done in polynomial time.
This gives a parametrized class of patterns for which the membership problem is tractable.

6.1
Acyclic Pattern Decomposition

This section examines decomposing terminal-free patterns (i. e.,
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
) into acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, where
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
denotes the set of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s where each word equation has a right-hand side of at most length two.
Patterns are the basis for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
atoms, and hence, this section gives us a foundation on which to investigate the decomposition of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
We do not consider regular constraints, or patterns with terminals.
This is because regular constraints are unary predicates, and therefore can be easily added to a join tree; and terminals can be expressed through regular constraints.

We use
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s for two reasons.
Firstly, binary concatenation is the most elementary form of concatenation, as it cannot be decomposed into further (non-trivial) concatenations.
Secondly, this ensures that we can store the tables in linear space and enumerate them with constant delay – as we conclude from the following proposition, which was written by Freydenberger and was published in joint work with the author
[
46
]
.
The proof has been omitted from this thesis, as it is not the author’s work, and is not necessary for understanding its consequences.

Proposition 6.1

(Freydenberger, Thompson
[
46
]
)
.

Given
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we can construct a data structure in linear time that, for
x,y,z\in\Xi
, allows for the enumeration of
\llbracket x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z\rrbracket(w)
with constant delay, and to decide if
\sigma\in\llbracket x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z\rrbracket(w)
in constant time.
Note that
x
,
y
, and
z
are not necessarily distinct.

Although the cardinality of
\llbracket x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z\rrbracket(w)
is cubic in
|w|
,
Proposition

6.1
allows us to represent this relation in linear space.
As we can query such relations in constant time, they behave “nicer” than relations in relational algebra.
Furthermore, after materializing the relations defined by each atom of an
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
,
Proposition

6.1
allows us to treat the
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
as a relational conjunctive query.

We now introduce a way to
decompose
a pattern into a conjunction of word equations where the right-hand side of each atom is at most length two.
We start by looking at a canonical way to decompose terminal-free patterns.
To decompose
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, we first factorize is so that it can be written using only binary concatenation.

Definition 6.2
.

We define the set of all
well-bracketed patterns
, denoted as
\mathsf{BPat}
, recursively as follows:

•

x\in\mathsf{BPat}
for all
x\in\Xi
, and

•

if
\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
, then
(\tilde{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{\beta})\in\mathsf{BPat}
.

For convenience, we tend to use
\tilde{\alpha}
to denote a bracketing of the pattern
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
.
We extend the notion of a factor to a
sub-bracketing
.
We write
\tilde{\alpha}\sqsubseteq\tilde{\beta}
if
\tilde{\alpha}
is a factor of
\tilde{\beta}
and
\tilde{\alpha},\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
.
Let
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, by
\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
we denote the set of all bracketings which correspond to the pattern
\alpha
.

Definition 6.3
.

Every
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
can be converted into
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
using the following:
While there exists
\tilde{\beta}\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
where
\tilde{\beta}=(x\cdot y)
for some
x,y\in\Xi
, we replace every occurrence of
\tilde{\beta}
in
\tilde{\alpha}
with a new and unique variable
z\in\Xi\setminus\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
and add the word equation
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
to
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
via conjunction.
If
\tilde{\alpha}=\tilde{\beta}
, then
z=\mathfrak{u}
.

For the purposes of this section, the choice of free variables for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is not important.
Therefore, we assume a decomposition to be a Boolean query.

Therefore, up to renaming of variables and reordering of atoms, every
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
has a corresponding formula
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We call
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
the
decomposition
of
\tilde{\alpha}
.
The decomposition can be thought of as a logic formula expressing a so-called
straight-line program
of the pattern (see
[
76
]
for a survey on algorithms for SLPs).
We now give an example of
decomposing
a bracketing.

Example 6.4
.

Let
\alpha:=x_{1}x_{2}x_{1}x_{1}x_{2}
and let
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
be defined as follows:

\tilde{\alpha}:=(((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot x_{1})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})).

We now list
\tilde{\alpha}
after each sub-bracketing is replaced with a variable. We also give the corresponding word equation that is added to
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.

\displaystyle((\underline{(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})}\cdot x_{1})\cdot\underline{(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})})

\displaystyle z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2}

\displaystyle(\underline{(z_{1}\cdot x_{1})}\cdot z_{1})

\displaystyle z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1}

\displaystyle\underline{(z_{2}\cdot z_{1})}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{1}

Therefore, we get the decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, which is defined as

\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{1}).

Notice that every sub-bracketing of
\tilde{\alpha}
has a corresponding word equation in
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.

The decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}
is somewhat similar to so-called
Tseytin transformations
, see
[
92
]
, which transform propositional logic formulas into a formula in
Tseytin normal form
.

Our next focus is to study which patterns can be decomposed into an
acyclic

\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
But first note that we use variants of
\chi
to denote atoms of a
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to distinguish them from word equations with arbitrarily long right-hand sides (which we denote with variants of
\eta
).

We define a join tree for
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s the same way as it was defined for relational
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s (see
Definition

4.7
).
Likewise, the GYO algorithm (recall
Definition

4.9
) for deciding whether a
{\mathsf{CQ}}
is acyclic can be directly translated to
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
The only difference being that the atoms of
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s are word equations of the form
x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z
for
x,y,z\in\Xi
.
We consider
\mathfrak{u}
to be a constant symbol (not a variable) since
\sigma(\mathfrak{u})
is always substituted with our input word.
Thus, occurrences of
\mathfrak{u}
do not need to adhere to the conditions required of other variables in a join tree.

If there is a join tree for
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, then
\Psi
is
acyclic
, otherwise
\Psi
is
cyclic
.

Definition 6.5

(Acyclic Patterns)
.

If
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is a decomposition of the bracketed pattern
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
and
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is acyclic, then we call
\tilde{\alpha}

acyclic
.
Otherwise,
\tilde{\alpha}
is
cyclic
.
If there exists
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
which is acyclic, then we say that
\alpha
is
acyclic
.
Otherwise,
\alpha
is
cyclic
.

The first question one may ask is whether there is an acyclic decomposition for every pattern. This is not the case.
The proof of
Proposition

6.6
is not particularly enlightening.
Therefore, if there reader wishes, they can skip to
Section

6.1.1
.

Proposition 6.6
.

x_{1}x_{2}x_{1}x_{3}x_{1}
is a cyclic pattern, and
x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{1}
is an acyclic pattern that has a cyclic bracketing.

Proof.

We prove this Proposition in two parts.

Part 1. There exists a cyclic pattern:

Let
\alpha:=x_{1}x_{2}x_{1}x_{3}x_{1}
. We prove that
\alpha
is cyclic by enumerating every possible bracketing
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, and then show that the decomposition of each bracketing is cyclic.

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{1}:=((x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot(x_{1}\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1})))))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{2}:=(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot((x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{1})))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{3}:=((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1})))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{3}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{4}:=(x_{1}\cdot((x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1})))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{4}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{5}:=(x_{1}\cdot((x_{2}\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{3}))\cdot x_{1}))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{5}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{6}:=(x_{1}\cdot(((x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{1}))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{6}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{7}:=((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot((x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{1}))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{7}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{8}:=(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot x_{1}))\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1}))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{8}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot z_{2}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{9}:=(x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1})))\cdot x_{1})
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{9}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{3}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{10}:=((x_{1}\cdot((x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\cdot x_{3}))\cdot x_{1})
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{10}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot x_{1}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{11}:=(((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{3}))\cdot x_{1})
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{11}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot x_{1}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{12}:=(((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot x_{1})\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1}))
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{12}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot z_{2}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{13}:=(((x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot x_{1}))\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{1})
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{13}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{3})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot x_{1}),

•

\tilde{\alpha}_{14}:=((((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot x_{1})\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{1})
which decomposes into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{14}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{3})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot x_{1}).

For every
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, we have that
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{i}}
is cyclic.

Part 2: There exists an acyclic pattern which has a cyclic bracketing.

Let
\alpha:=x_{1}x_{2}x_{3}x_{1}
, and consider the bracketings

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{1}

\displaystyle:=((x_{1}\cdot(x_{2}\cdot x_{3}))\cdot x_{1}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{2}

\displaystyle:=((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{3}\cdot x_{1})).

These decompose into

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{1})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{3}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{1}).

Executing the GYO algorithm on
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{1}}
shows it to be acyclic. Whereas executing the GYO algorithm on
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}
shows it to be cyclic.
∎

Proposition

6.6
leads us to question which patterns are acyclic.

6.1.1
Characterizing Acyclic Decompositions

Given a pattern
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, we have that
|\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)|=C_{|\alpha|-1}
, where
C_{i}
is the
i
th
Catalan number
, see
[
75
]
.
The
i
th Catalan number can be expressed as follows

C_{i}=\frac{(2i)!}{(i+1)!\,i!}\,.

As the Catalan numbers grow exponentially, a brute-force approach to find an acyclic bracketing would be intractable. This leads to the following key question:
Can we decide whether a pattern is acyclic in polynomial time?

If
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is a decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, then we call the variable
x\in\Xi
which represents the whole pattern the
root variable
. If
x
is the root variable, then the atom
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z)
for some
y,z\in\Xi
, is called the
root atom
. So far, the root variable has always been
\mathfrak{u}
.
In
Section

6.2
, different root variables shall be considered when we generalize from decomposing patterns to
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
. We define the
concatenation tree
of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
as a rooted, undirected, binary tree
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
, where
\mathcal{V}
is a set of nodes and
\mathcal{E}
is a set of undirected edges.
If
v
and
v^{\prime}
have a shared parent node, then we use
v<v^{\prime}
to denote that
v
is the left child and
v^{\prime}
is the right child of their shared parent.
We also have
\Gamma:=\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
and the function
\tau\colon\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\Gamma
that

labels
nodes from the concatenation tree with variables from
\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
.
We use
v_{r}
to denote the root of
\mathcal{T}
.
The construction of the
concatenation tree
for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is as follows:

Definition 6.7
.

Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(z_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}\cdot x_{i}^{\prime})
be the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
with the root variable
z_{n}
.
We define a concatenation tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
in two steps.
First, we define a tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
recursively:

•

Let
v_{n}
be the root, labelled with
z_{n}
,

•

if
v\in\mathcal{V}
is labelled with
z_{i}
for some
i\in[n]
, then
v
has a left and right child that are labelled with
x_{i}
and
x_{i}^{\prime}
respectively.

Then, the tree defined above is pruned to remove redundancies.
For each set of
non-leaf nodes
that share a label, we define an ordering
\ll
.
If
\tau(v_{i})=\tau(v_{j})
and the distance from the root of
\mathcal{T}
to
v_{j}
is strictly less than the distance from the root to
v_{i}
, then
v_{j}\ll v_{i}
. If
\tau(v_{i})=\tau(v_{j})
and the distance from
v_{r}
to
v_{i}
and
v_{j}
is equal, then
v_{j}\ll v_{i}
if and only if
v_{j}
appears to the
right
of
v_{i}
.

For each set of non-leaf nodes that share a label, all nodes other than the
\ll
-maximum node are called
redundant
.
All descendants of redundant nodes are removed.

Concatenation trees for
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s can be understood as a variation of
derivation trees
for straight-line programs
[
76
]
.
IE over SLP-represented documents has received some attention recently
[
101
,
102
]
.
This opens a research direction on query evaluation of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
decompositions on SLP-compressed documents.
5
5

5

The author was made aware of this future direction for research during a brief online discussion with Markus L. Schmid at ICDT 2022.

However, this thesis does not consider this topic.

Due to the pruning procedure, every non-leaf node represents a unique sub-bracketing.
For every non-leaf node
v
with left child
v_{l}
and right child
v_{r}
, we define
\mathsf{atom}(v):=(\tau(v)\mathbin{\dot{=}}\tau(v_{l})\cdot\tau(v_{r}))
.
Note that for any two non-leaf nodes
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
where
v\neq v^{\prime}
, we have that
\mathsf{atom}(v)\neq\mathsf{atom}(v^{\prime})
.
We call
v\in\mathcal{V}
an
x
-parent if one of the child nodes of
v
is labelled
x
.
If
v
is an
x
-parent, then
\mathsf{atom}(v)
must contain the variable
x
.

Definition 6.8
.

Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
and let
\mathcal{T}
be the concatenation tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.
For a variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
, we say that
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized
if all nodes that
lie on the path between any two
x
-parents in
\mathcal{T}
are also
x
-parents.

Since there is only one concatenation tree for a decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
, we can say
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized without referring to the concatenation tree.

Example 6.9
.

Consider the following two bracketings of
\alpha:=x_{1}x_{2}x_{1}x_{2}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{1}

\displaystyle:=((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{2}))\text{ and }

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{2}

\displaystyle:=(((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot x_{1})\cdot x_{2}),

which are decomposed into

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{1}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{1})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{2}).

The concatenation trees for
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are given in
Figure

6.1
. The label for each node is given in parentheses next to the corresponding node. We can see that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{2})=(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})
.

It follows that
\Psi_{2}
is
x_{1}
-localized, but
\Psi_{2}
is not
x_{2}
-localized. Observe that
v_{3}\ll v_{2}
, since
v_{2}
appears to the left of
v_{3}
. Therefore,
v_{3}
does not have any descendants, since it is a redundant node.

v_{1}\;(\mathfrak{u})
v_{3}\;(z_{1})
v_{2}\;(z_{1})
v_{4}\;(x_{1})
v_{5}\;(x_{2})
v_{6}\;(\mathfrak{u})
v_{8}\;(x_{2})
v_{7}\;(z_{2})
v_{9}\;(z_{1})
v_{10}\;(x_{1})
v_{11}\;(x_{1})
v_{12}\;(x_{2})

Figure 6.1
:
Concatenation trees for the decompositions of
((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{2}))
and
(((x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot x_{1})\cdot x_{2})
. This figure is used to illustrate
Example

6.9
.

Let us now observe a straightforward graph theoretic lemma.
While this result is not of much interest by itself, it will help us streamline the proof of
Lemma

6.11
.

Lemma 6.10
.

If
T:=(V,E)
is an undirected tree where
V:=[n]
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
, then for all
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
, every node that lies on the path from
i
to
j
must lie on a path from
k
to
k+1
for some
k\in\{i,i+1,\dots,j-1\}
.

Proof.

Let
T:=(V,E)
be an undirected tree where
V:=[n]
. For any
k,k^{\prime}\in[n]
, let
p_{k\rightarrow k^{\prime}}
be the path from
k
to
k^{\prime}
in
T
.
Then, we construct a subtree
T^{\prime}
of
T
, consisting of all the edges in the path
p_{k\rightarrow k+1}
for all
i\leq k<j
, along with the necessary nodes.
Since
T^{\prime}
contains the path from
i
and
j
, and there can only be one path between any two nodes in a tree, the stated lemma holds.
∎

Lemma

6.10
can clearly be generalized to trees with any vertex set,
V
, by considering some bijection from the vertices of the tree to
[n]
where
n:=|V|
.

If
\eta:=(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z)
is an atom of the acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, then
\eta
can be replaced with
\eta^{\prime}:=(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z\cdot y)
, and
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
remains acyclic.
Therefore, in the following proofs, when the right-hand side of an atom is ambiguous, we can assume one ordering of the right-hand sides without loss of generality.

Utilizing concatenation trees for the decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, and the notion of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
being
x
-localized for
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
, we are now able to state sufficient and necessary
conditions for
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
to be acyclic.

Lemma 6.11
.

The decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
is acyclic if and only if
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
.

Proof.

Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be a decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
with the concatenation tree

\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
.

If-direction.

If
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
, then we can construct a join tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
by augmenting the concatenation tree. First, replace all non-leaf nodes
v\in\mathcal{V}
with
\mathsf{atom}(v)
. Then, remove all leaf nodes.

By the definition of the concatenation tree, every atom of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is a node in the supposed join tree. Also due to the definition of a concatenation tree, if
v
is an
x
-parent, then
x
occurs in
\mathsf{atom}(v)
. Because
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
, it follows that if two nodes in the supposed join tree contain the variable
x
, then all nodes which exist on the path between these two nodes also contains an
x
. Hence, the resulting tree is a join tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.

Only if-direction.

Let
v_{0},v_{n}\in\mathcal{V}
be two
x
-parents for some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
such that the distance between
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
in the concatenation tree
\mathcal{T}
is
n>1
. Let
v_{1},v_{2},\dots v_{n-1}\in\mathcal{V}
be the nodes on the path between
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
where
v_{i}
is not an
x
-parent for all
i\in[n-1]
, hence
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is not
x
-localized. For readability, we assume that
\tau(v_{i})=z_{i}
for all
i\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
.
Because the concatenation tree is pruned,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{j})
if and only if
i=j
, for
i,j\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
. Furthermore, if
\tau(v)=z_{i}
where
v
is a non-leaf node, then
v=v_{i}
, because two different non-leaf nodes cannot share a label.
Figure

6.2
illustrates a subtree of
\mathcal{T}
. The variable that labels each node is given next to the node in parentheses.

v_{k}\;(z_{k})
v_{k+1}\;(z_{k+1})
v_{k-1}\;(z_{k-1})
\vdots
\vdots
v_{0}\;(z_{0})
v_{n}\;(z_{n})
\vdots
\vdots
v\;(x)
v^{\prime}\;(x)
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 6.2
:
The concatenation tree
\mathcal{T}
we use for the only if-direction in the proof of
Lemma

6.11
.

For sake of a contradiction, assume there is a join tree
T:=(V,E)
for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
. Nodes in the join tree are the atoms of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
and therefore any element of
V
can be uniquely determined by
\mathsf{atom}(v)
where
v\in\mathcal{V}
is a non-leaf node of
\mathcal{T}
.

We remind the reader that
\mathsf{atom}(v)=(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot x^{\prime})
if
v
is labeled
z
and the left and right children of
v
are labeled
x
and
x^{\prime}
respectively. To improve readability, we use
v
, and variants such as
v_{1}
, for nodes of the concatenation tree, and we use
\mathsf{atom}(v)
for nodes of the join tree where
v
is some non-leaf of
\mathcal{T}
.

We relax the factor notation to variables in
\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
. For
z,z^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
, we write
z\sqsubset z^{\prime}
if there exists
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
where
v^{\prime}
which is an ancestor of
v
in the concatenation tree, and
\tau(v^{\prime})=z^{\prime}
and
\tau(v)=z
. We do this because the pattern that
z
represents is a strict factor of the pattern that
z^{\prime}
represents.

For any
i,j\in\{0,1,\dots,n\}
, let
p_{i\rightarrow j}
be the path in the join tree
T
from
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{j})
. The atom
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
cannot exist on the path
p_{0\rightarrow n}
because
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
contain the variable
x
, but
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
does not contain the variable
x
. We therefore consider some non-leaf node
v_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
of the concatenation tree such that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
is the atom on the path
p_{0\rightarrow n}
which is closest (with regards to distance) to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
. See
Figure

6.3
for a diagram to illustrate
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
. We know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
has a variable
x
since it lies on the path
p_{0\rightarrow n}
.

We now prove that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
contains some variable
z_{i}
where
i\in[n]
. Since
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
is the node closest to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
on the path
p_{0\rightarrow n}
, we have that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
must also exist on the path
p_{1\rightarrow n}
(see
Figure

6.3
). Therefore, because of
Lemma

6.10
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
must exist on some path
p_{j\rightarrow j+1}
for some
j\in[n-1]
. Since
\mathsf{atom}(v_{j})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{j+1})
share the variable
z_{j}
or
z_{j+1}
(depending on whether
v_{j}
or
v_{j+1}
is the parent) for all
j\in[n-1]
, it follows that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
must contain the variable
z_{i}
for some
i\in[n]
.

We now look at three cases, and conclude a contradiction from each case.

•

Case 1.
v_{n}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

•

Case 2.
v_{0}
is an ancestor of
v_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

•

Case 3.
v_{0}
is not an ancestor of
v_{n}
, and
v_{n}
is not an ancestor of
v_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

Notice that
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
are named arbitrarily and can be exchanged in the proof.
Therefore, Case 2 follows immediately from Case 1.

\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
…
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
\vdots
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
…
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})

Figure 6.3
:
A figure to illustrate paths
p_{0\rightarrow 1}
and
p_{0\rightarrow n}
.

Case 1:
v_{n}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

Since
v_{n}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
, we know that
v_{i}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
(and hence
x\sqsubset z_{0}\sqsubset z_{i}
) for all
i\in[n]
. Furthermore, it follows that
v_{1}
is a
z_{0}
-parent and therefore
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})=(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z^{\prime})
for some
z^{\prime}\in\Xi
. Since
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
lies on the path
p_{0\rightarrow 1}
, it follows that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
contains the variable
z_{0}
. One of the variables of
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
must be the label of
v_{1}^{\prime}
, we therefore consider all the possible labels for
v_{1}^{\prime}
and show a contradiction for each.

•

\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=x
. This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z_{i})
and therefore
z_{0}\sqsubset x
. We know that
x\sqsubset z_{0}
since
v_{0}
is an
x
-parent. Therefore,
z_{0}\sqsubset z_{0}
which is a contradiction and hence
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=x
cannot hold.

•

\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{i}
where
i\in[n]
. We split this case into two parts:

–

\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{i}
where
i\in[n-1]
. This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
. The word equation
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
does not contain an
x
. Since we know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
contains the variable
x
, we conclude that
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{i}
where
i\in[n-1]
leads to a contradiction.

–

\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
. This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
and therefore, without loss of generality,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})=(z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{0})
. However, since we are in the case that
v_{n}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
, it follows that
v_{n}
is a parent of
v_{0}
(since a node labeled
x
or
z_{0}
cannot be an ancestor of
v_{0}
). Therefore
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized, and hence
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
cannot hold.

•

\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{0}
. This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
. Therefore, without loss of generality,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})=(z_{0}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{i})
. We also know that
z_{0}\sqsubset z_{i}
since
v_{i}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
. Therefore,
z_{0}\sqsubset z_{i}\sqsubset z_{0}
. Thus,
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{0}
cannot hold.

We have proven that, for the case where
v_{n}
is an ancestor of
v_{0}
in the concatenation tree, there does not exist a valid label for
v_{1}^{\prime}
. Hence we have reached a contradiction and therefore our assumption
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is acyclic cannot hold.

Case 2:
v_{0}
is an ancestor of
v_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
.

The case where
v_{0}
is an ancestor of
v_{n}
is trivially identical to Case 1 by considering the closest node to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n-1})
on the path
p_{n\rightarrow 0}
. We have therefore omitted the proof.

Case 3:
v_{n}
is not an ancestor of
v_{0}
and
v_{0}
is not an ancestor of
v_{n}
.

Let
k\in[n-1]
such that
v_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
is the lowest common ancestor of
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
in
\mathcal{T}
.
Recall that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
has the variables
x
and
z_{i}
for some
i\in[n]
because
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
lies on the paths
p_{0\rightarrow n}
and
p_{1\rightarrow n}
.
We also have that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})=(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z^{\prime})
for some
z^{\prime}\in\Xi
, because for this case,
v_{1}
must be a parent of
v_{0}
, otherwise
v_{0}
would be an ancestor of
v_{n}
. Therefore, since
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
share the variable
z_{0}
, we know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
also contains
z_{0}
because
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
lies on the path
p_{0\rightarrow 1}
. We now consider each label for
v_{1}^{\prime}
and show a contradiction for each case.
In other words, we consider the following cases:

•

Case 3.1:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=x
.

•

Case 3.2:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{i}
where
i\in[n-1]
.

•

Case 3.3:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
.

•

Case 3.4:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{0}
.

Case 3.1:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=x
.

Without loss of generality,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z_{i})
which implies that
x\sqsubset z_{0}
and
z_{0}\sqsubset x
. This is a contradiction as
x\sqsubset x
cannot hold due to the fact that
\sqsubset
is used to denote the strict factor relation.

Case 3.2:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{i}
where
i\in[n-1]
.

This implies
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
, but
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
cannot have the variable
x
. Thus, we reach a contradiction.

Case 3.3:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
.

This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
and therefore without loss of generality, we know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})=(z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot x)
, because
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
must contain the variable
z_{0}
and
x
. For this case, we first prove that
k\geq 2
where
v_{k}
is the lowest common ancestor of
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
.
That is, we show
k\neq 1
.

For sake of contradiction, assume
k=1
. It follows that the distance from
v_{k}
to
v_{0}
is one and the distance from
v_{k}
to
v_{n}
is greater than or equal to one. Hence, the distance from
v_{k}
to the children of
v_{n}
is greater than or equal to two. Since
v_{n}
is a
z_{0}
parent, and the children of
v_{n}
are further from the root than
v_{0}
, we know that
v_{0}
must be redundant. If this is the case,
v_{0}
would have no children due to the pruning procedure used when defining a concatenation tree. Therefore,
v_{0}
would not be an
x
-parent which we know cannot hold (we have chosen
v_{0}
because it is an
x
-parent). Consequently,
k=1
cannot hold and we can conclude
k\geq 2
.

We now consider
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
. We know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})=(z_{k}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{k-1}\cdot z_{k+1})
and since we have proven that
k\geq 2
, it follows that
z_{k-1}\neq z_{0}
. Since both
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
contain the variable
z_{0}
, we know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
cannot exist on the path
p_{1\rightarrow n}
. Hence, we consider some non-leaf node
v_{k}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
such that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
lies on the path
p_{1\rightarrow n}
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
is the node on
p_{1\rightarrow n}
which is closest node (with regards to distance) to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
. We illustrate a subtree of such a join tree in
Figure

6.4
.

Next, we prove that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
must contain some
z_{j}\in\Xi
, where
j\in[k-1]
.
We know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
lies on the path
p_{1\rightarrow k}
.
Thus, due to
Lemma

6.10
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
must lie on the path
p_{i\rightarrow i+1}
for some
i\in[k-1]
.
Since each atom which lies on the path
p_{i\rightarrow i+1}
must contain the variable
z_{i}
, it follows that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
contains the variable
z_{j}
for some
j\in[k-1]
.
Figure

6.2
illustrates why all nodes on the path
p_{i\rightarrow i+1}
for
i\in[k-1]
must contain the variable
z_{i}
(because
v_{i+1}
is a parent of
v_{i}
for
i\in[k-1]
).

Furthermore, we shall show that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
must also contain the variable
z_{l}\in\Xi
for some
l\in\{k+1,\dots,n\}
.
We know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
lies on the path
p_{k\rightarrow n}
.
Therefore, because of
Lemma

6.10
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
must lies on the path
p_{i\rightarrow i+1}
for some
i\in\{k,\dots,n-1\}
.
Since each atom which lies on the path
p_{i\rightarrow i+1}
must contain the variable
z_{i+1}
for
i\in\{k,\dots,n-1\}
, it follows that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
contains the variable
z_{l}
for some
l\in\{k+1,\dots,n\}
.
Next, we consider the possible labels of
v_{k}^{\prime}
.
That is, we consider the following cases:

•

Case 3.3.1.
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{0}
.

•

Case 3.3.2.
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{j}
where
j\in[k-1]
.

•

Case 3.3.3.
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{l}
where
l\in\{k+1,k+2,\dots,n\}
.

z_{0}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{0}^{\prime}
\vdots
z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{0}
…
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
…
z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z^{\prime}
\vdots
z_{k}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{k-1}\cdot z_{k+1}

Figure 6.4
:
A subtree of a join tree with nodes
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
. This figure is used to illustrate Case 3.3.

Case 3.3.1:
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{0}
.

This implies
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
. We can therefore state, without loss of generality, that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})=(z_{0}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{j}\cdot z_{l})
. However, if this is the case then
x
is not a variable of
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
.
This is a contradiction because
v_{0}
is an
x
-parent.
Hence,
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{0}
cannot hold.

Case 3.3.2:
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{j}
where
j\in[k-1]
.

This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})=(z_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z_{l})
. If this is the case then
j=1
must hold, since this is the only value for
j
such that
(z_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z_{l})
can hold. We can therefore say that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
. For all nodes
v\in\mathcal{V}
, let
D(v)
be the distance from the root of
\mathcal{T}
to
v
. Since
v_{l}
cannot be a redundant node, it follows that
D(v_{1})+1\leq D(v_{l})
. This implies that
D(v_{k})+k-1+1\leq D(v_{k})+l-k
and hence,
k\leq\frac{l}{2}
. Because
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})=(z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot x)
and
v_{0}
is not redundant, we can also say that
D(v_{n})+1\leq D(v_{0})
and hence,
D(v_{k})+n-k+1\leq D(v_{k})+k
and therefore
n+1\leq 2k
. Consequently,
\frac{n+1}{2}\leq k\leq\frac{l}{2}
and hence,
n+1\leq l
. This is a contradiction since
l\in\{k+1,\dots,n\}
. Thus
\tau(v_{k})=z_{j}
cannot hold.

Case 3.3.3:
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{l}
where
l\in\{k+1,k+2\dots,n\}
.

We split this case into two parts:

•

\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{n}
. This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
. Recall
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})=(z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot x)
. Therefore,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
does not contain the variable
z_{j}
for
j\in[k-1]
, yet we know that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
does contain the variable
z_{j}
. Consequently,
\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{n}
cannot hold.

•

\tau(v_{k}^{\prime})=z_{l}
where
l\in\{k+1,\dots,n-1\}
. This implies that, without loss of generality,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})=(z_{l}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{0}\cdot z_{j})
. This cannot hold since if
k<l<n
, then
\mathsf{atom}(v_{l})
contains the variable
z_{l+1}
. However,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
does not contain the variable
z_{l+1}
.

Consequently, we have proven that if
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
, then there does not exist a valid label for the non-leaf node
v_{k}^{\prime}
, where
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k}^{\prime})
is the closest node to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
on the path
p_{1\rightarrow n}
. Therefore
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{n}
cannot hold.

Case 3.4:
\tau(v_{1}^{\prime})=z_{0}
.

This implies that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})
. Without loss of generality,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{0})=(z_{0}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{i})
. It follows that
k<i\leq n
, since if
1\leq i\leq k
then
z_{i}\sqsubset z_{0}\sqsubset z_{i}
which is a contradiction.

We now claim that
n>2
must hold.
Assume that
n=2
.
Since we know that
v_{k}
is the lowest common ancestor of
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
, it follows that
k=1
.
It also follows that
i=n
since
k<i\leq n
.
The distance from
v_{k}
to
v_{n}
is one and the distance from
v_{k}
to the children of
v_{0}
is two.
Since
v_{0}
has a child with the label
z_{n}
, it follows that
v_{n}
is a redundant node, and hence it is not an
x
-parent.
We know this cannot hold and hence
n>2
.

We now consider
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n-1}^{\prime})
which is the atom of the path
p_{n\rightarrow 0}
which is closest to
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n-1})
.
Since
n>2
, it follows that
v_{n-1}\neq v_{1}
.
The nodes
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
are arbitrarily named and therefore
v_{0}
and
v_{n}
can be exchanged in the proof.
Thus, it must hold that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n-1}^{\prime})=(z_{n}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{j})
where
0\leq j<k
in the same way that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})=(z_{0}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z_{i})
where
k<i\leq n
.
Consequently,
z_{0}\sqsubset z_{j}
and
z_{n}\sqsubset z_{i}
since
0<j<k
and
k<i<n
.
Here lies our contradiction, since
z_{i}\sqsubset z_{0}\sqsubset z_{j}
and
z_{j}\sqsubset z_{n}\sqsubset z_{i}
cannot hold simultaneously.

To conclude this proof, we have considered all cases for
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1}^{\prime})
and have shown a contradiction for each, our assumption that
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is acyclic and is not
x
-localized for some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
cannot hold.
∎

Referring back to
Example

6.9
, we can see that
\Psi_{2}
is not
x_{2}
-localized and therefore
\Psi_{2}
is cyclic, whereas we have that
\Psi_{1}
is
x
-localized for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{1})
and hence
\Psi_{1}
is acyclic.

6.1.2
Acyclic Pattern Algorithm

We now use
Lemma

6.11
as the foundation for an algorithm that decides in polynomial time whether a pattern is acyclic.

Theorem 6.12
.

Whether
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
is acyclic can be decided in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha:=\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdots\alpha_{n}
where
\alpha_{i}\in\Xi
for all
i\in[n]
.
For any
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
1\leq i\leq j\leq n
, we use
\alpha[i,j]
to denote
\alpha_{i}\cdot\alpha_{i+1}\cdots\alpha_{j}
.
We now give an algorithm to determine whether
\alpha
is acyclic.
This algorithm is essentially a bottom-up implementation of
Lemma

6.11
.

Algorithm

1
is the main algorithm, which continuously adds larger subpatterns to a set until we reach a so-called
fixed point
.
Algorithm

9
is a “helper procedure”.

Input :

\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, where
|\alpha|=n
.

Output :

True if
\alpha
is acyclic, and False otherwise.

V\leftarrow\{(i,i),(i+1,i+1),(i,i+1)\mid i\in[n-1]\}
;

E^{\prime}\leftarrow\{((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1))\mathrel{|}i\in[n-1]\}
;

E\leftarrow\emptyset
;

while

E^{\prime}\neq E

do

E\leftarrow E^{\prime}
;

for

i,k\in[n]
where
i<k

do

2

for

j\in\{i,i+1,\dots,k-1\}
where
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))\notin E^{\prime}

do

3

if

(i,j),(j+1,k)\in V
and
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}(i,j,k,\alpha,E^{\prime})

then

4

Add
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))
to
E^{\prime}
;
Add
(i,k)
to
V
;
end if

5

end for

6

end for

7

end while

8

Return
\mathsf{True}
if
(1,n)\in V
, and
\mathsf{False}
otherwise;

Algorithm 1

Acyclic pattern algorithm. Given
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, decides if
\alpha
is acyclic.

Input :

i,j,k\in[|\alpha|]
,
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
,
E^{\prime}

Output :

True if
\alpha[i,j]
is acyclic, and False otherwise

if

\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,k]

then

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else if

\mathsf{var}(\alpha[i,j])\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{var}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\emptyset

then

10

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

for

x\in\{i,i+1,\dots,j-1\}

do

11

if

((i,j),(i,x),(x+1,j))\in E^{\prime}
and
\alpha[j+1,k]=\alpha[i,x]

then

12

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else if

((i,j),(i,x),(x+1,j))\in E^{\prime}
and
\alpha[j+1,k]=\alpha[x+1,j]

then

13

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else if

((j+1,k),(j+1,x),(x+1,k))\in E^{\prime}
and
\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,x]

then

14

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else if

((j+1,k),(j+1,x),(x+1,k))\in E^{\prime}
and
\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[x+1,k]

then

15

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

end for

16

Return
\mathsf{False}
;

Algorithm 2

\mathsf{IsAcyclic}(i,j,k,\alpha,E^{\prime})
. Decides whether
\alpha[i,j]\cdot\alpha[j+1,k]
is acyclic.

Correctness.

We first give a high-level overview.
The algorithm works using a bottom-up approach, and continuously adding larger acyclic subpatterns of
\alpha
to the set
V
.
Each subpattern is stored in
V
as two indices for the start and end positions of the subpattern.
To ensure that the subpatterns we are adding are acyclic, we also store an edge relation
E
and call the
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
subroutine.

The subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
is given two acyclic subpatterns (
\alpha[i,j]
and
\alpha[j+1,k]
), and uses
E^{\prime}
to determine whether there exists
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j]\cdot\alpha[j+1,k])
such that
\tilde{\beta}
is acyclic.
That is, the decomposition of
\tilde{\beta}
is
x
-localized for all variables, see
Lemma

6.11
.
The main algorithm is given in
Algorithm

1
, and

\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
is a subroutine called by
Algorithm

1
, and is given in
Algorithm

9
.

Correctness of
Algorithm

1
.

First, assume that
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
returns true (given
i,j,k,\alpha
, and
E^{\prime}
) if and only if there exists
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j])
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[j+1,k])
such that
(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
is acyclic.

Lines 1 and 2 are the “base case” and state that
\alpha[i,i]
,
\alpha[i+1,i+1]
and
\alpha[i,i+1]
are acyclic for all
i\in[n-1]
.
We also add
((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1))
to
E
to denote that
\alpha[i,i+1]
is a concatenation of
\alpha[i,i]
and
\alpha[i+1,i+1]
.

Now consider the while loop given on line 4.
This code block adds
(i,k)
to
V
if and only if there exists
(i,j),(j+1,k)\in V
, and therefore
\alpha[i,j]
and
\alpha[j+1,k]
are acyclic, such that the concatenation
\alpha[i,j]\cdot\alpha[j+1,k]
is an acyclic pattern.
We then add
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))
to
E
to denote that
(i,j)
and
(j+1,k)
are the left and right children of
(i,k)
respectively.

This while loop terminates when
E
reaches a so-called
fixed-point
.
That is, no more acyclic subpatterns of the input pattern can be derived from
E
.
Then, either
(1,n)\in V
and therefore
\alpha
is acyclic, or
(1,n)\notin V
and
\alpha
is cyclic.

Therefore, as long as the subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
is correct, our algorithm is correct.

\alpha[i,k]\;(z^{\prime})
\alpha[j+1,k]\;(x_{1})
\alpha[i,j]\;(z)
\alpha[i,x]\;(x_{1})
\alpha[x+1,j]\;(x_{2})
\vdots
\vdots

:
Illustrating Case 3 for the correctness of the
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
subroutine.

v_{1}\;(z)

:
Illustrating the only-if direction for the correctness of the
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}

z_{1}\neq z_{2}
,
z_{2}\notin\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
, and
z_{1}\notin\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
.

Correctness of
Algorithm

9
.

We now show that the subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
is correct.
Assume
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
is passed
i,j,k
(where
1\leq i\leq j\leq k\leq n
), the pattern
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, and the edge relation
E^{\prime}
.
Since
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
has been passed
i
,
j
, and
k
, it follows that
(i,j),(j+1,k)\in V
and therefore there exists
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j])
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[j+1,k])
such that
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
are acyclic.
We now prove that
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j]\cdot\alpha[j+1,k])
is acyclic if and only if one of the following cases hold:

Case 1.

\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,k]
.

Case 2.

\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[i,j])\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\emptyset
.

Case 3.

\tilde{\alpha}=((\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\cdot\tilde{\beta})\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
for some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
.

Case 4.

\tilde{\alpha}=((\tilde{\beta}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
for some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
.

Case 5.

\tilde{\alpha}=(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\beta}))
for some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
.

Case 6.

\tilde{\alpha}=(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot(\tilde{\beta}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{1}))
for some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
.

Notice that Case 4, Case 5, and Case 6 are analogous to Case 3.
Therefore, once we prove correctness for Case 3, cases 4, 5, and 6 follow analogously.
These six cases mirror the six conditions that cause
Algorithm

9
to return true.

If direction.

We consider Case 1, Case 2, and Case 3.
If any of these cases hold, we show that
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j]\cdot\alpha[j+1,k])
is acyclic.

Case 1.

\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,k]
. Since there exists an acyclic decomposition
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
for some
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,j])
, it follows immediately from
Lemma

6.11
that
(\tilde{\alpha}\cdot\tilde{\alpha})
is acyclic. Hence,
\alpha[i,k]
is acyclic and we can add
(i,k)
to
\mathcal{V}
.

Case 2.
