\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[i,j])\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\emptyset
. Because
\alpha[i,j]
and
\alpha[j+1,k]
are acyclic, there exists acyclic decompositions
\Psi_{1},\Psi_{2}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
where
\Psi_{1}
is the decomposition for some bracketing of
\alpha[i,j]
,
\Psi_{2}
is the decomposition of some bracketing of
\alpha[j+1,k]
, and
\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{1})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{2})=\emptyset
. Therefore,
\Psi:=\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}z^{\prime}\cdot z^{\prime\prime})
is an acyclic decomposition for some
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha[i,k])
, where
z\in\Xi
is a new variable, and
z^{\prime}
and
z^{\prime\prime}
are the root variables for
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
respectively. It follows from
Lemma

6.11
that
\Psi
is acyclic.

Case 3.

\tilde{\alpha}=((\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\cdot\tilde{\beta})\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
for some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
. This implies that
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}:=(\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\cdot\tilde{\beta})
. Let
\Psi_{1}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be an acyclic decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
. Let
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})
be the root atom of
\Psi_{1}
, where
x_{1}
represents the bracketing
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
. Therefore, the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}
can be obtained from adding the atom
(z^{\prime}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z\cdot x_{1})
to
\Psi
where
z^{\prime}\in\Xi
is a new variable. We illustrate a concatenation tree for this case in
Figure

6.5
where nodes of the concatenation tree are denoted by factors of
\alpha
. Assuming
\alpha[i,x]
and
\alpha[x+1,j]
are acyclic, it is clear that
\Psi
is
x
-localized for all
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi)
. Hence,
(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
is acyclic.

Each condition has a corresponding if-condition in the subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
.
Therefore, we know that if
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
returns true, given
i,j,k
(where
1\leq i\leq j\leq k\leq n
), the pattern
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, and the relation
E^{\prime}
, then
\alpha[i,k]
is acyclic.

Only if direction.

Now assume that cases 1 to 6 do not hold.
Let
\Psi_{1}
be the acyclic decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}
and let
\Psi_{2}
be the acyclic decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
.
Let
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot y_{1})
be the root atom of
\Psi_{1}
, and let
(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot y_{2})
be the root atom of
\Psi_{2}
.
The decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}:=(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2})
would be
\Psi:=\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2})
, where
z\in\Xi
is a new variable.
We illustrate part of the concatenation tree for
\Psi
in
Figure

6.6
.
Due to the fact that
\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\neq\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
it follows that
z_{1}\neq z_{2}
.
Also, because Cases 3 to 6 do not hold, we know that
z_{1}\notin\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
and
z_{2}\notin\{x_{1},y_{1}\}
.
However, since
\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{1})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{2})\neq\emptyset
it follows that there exists some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{1})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{2})
such that
\Psi
is not
x
-localized.
Hence
\Psi
is cyclic.
Notice that
x_{1}
(or
y_{1}
)
could
be in the set
\{x_{2},y_{2}\}
.
But then
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
are both
x_{1}
-parents (
y_{1}
-parents) and
z
is not an
x_{1}
-parent (
y_{1}
-parent).

Complexity.

We first consider the subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
.
The first two if-statements (lines 16 and 18), run in
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
time.
Then, the for loop (line 20) iterates
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
times.
The if-statements on lines 21, 23, 25, and 27 run in time
\mathcal{O}(1)
as it takes
\mathcal{O}(1)
time to check whether the two factors of
\alpha
are equal (after linear time preprocessing, see
Section

4.7
).
Therefore,
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
runs in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
.

The set
\mathcal{V}
holds factors of
\alpha
, and therefore
|\mathcal{E}|\leq n^{3}
, since each
(i,k)\in\mathcal{V}
has
\mathcal{O}(n)
outgoing edges.
It follows that the while loop from line 4 to line 14 is iterated
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{3})
times.
The for loop on line 6 is iterated
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{2})
times.
The for loop on line 7 is iterated
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
times.
Therefore, the algorithm runs in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
.
∎

Theorem

6.12
gives a polynomial time for the decision problem of whether a terminal-free pattern is acyclic.
Next, we look at finding a decomposition for an acyclic pattern in polynomial time.

Theorem 6.13
.

If
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
is acyclic, then in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
, we can find an acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\alpha
.

Proof.

Let
(V,E)
be the resulting relations from executing
Algorithm

1
on
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
.
If
(1,n)\in V
, then we know that
\alpha
is acyclic.
We can then use
V
and
E
to derive a concatenation tree
\mathcal{T}
for some acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
.

Input :

V,E

Output :

A concatenation tree
\mathcal{T}

Let
\mathcal{T}
be an empty concatenation tree;

Let
v_{r}=(1,n)
be the leaf node of
\mathcal{T}
;

while

there exists some leaf node
(i,k)
of
\mathcal{T}
where
i\neq k

do

for

j\in\{i,i+1,\dots,k\}

do

18

if

\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,k]
or
\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[i,j])\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\emptyset

then

19

Add
\{(i,k),(i,j)\}
and
\{(i,j),(j+1,k)\}
to
\mathcal{E}
;
Let
(i,j)<(j+1,k)
;
break;

else if

there exists
x\in V
such that
((i,j),(i,x),(x+1,j))\in E
and
\alpha[i,x]=\alpha[j+1,k]
or
\alpha[x+1,j]=\alpha[j+1,k]

then

20

Add
\{(i,k),(i,j)\}
and
\{(i,j),(j+1,k)\}
to
\mathcal{E}
;
Let
(i,j)<(j+1,k)
;
Add
\{(i,j),(i,x)\}
and
\{(i,j),(x+1,j)\}
to
\mathcal{E}
;
Let
(i,x)<(x+1,j)
;
break;

else if

there exists
x\in V
such that
((j+1,k),(j+1,x),(x+1,k))\in E
, and
\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[j+1,x]
or
\alpha[i,j]=\alpha[x+1,k]

then

21

Add
\{(i,k),(i,j)\}
and
\{(i,j),(j+1,k)\}
to
\mathcal{E}
;
Let
(i,j)<(j+1,k)
;
Add
\{(j+1,k),(j+1,x)\}
and
\{(j+1,k),(x+1,j)\}
to
\mathcal{E}
;
Let
(j+1,x)<(x+1,j)
;
break;

end for

22

end while

23

Return
\mathcal{T}

Algorithm 3

\mathsf{ConcateTree}(V,E)
. Given
V
and
E
from
Algorithm

1
, derives a concatenation tree for some
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
.

During the execution of
Algorithm

17
, we assume that
\mathcal{V}
is always updated to be the set of nodes that the edge relation
\mathcal{E}
uses.
For intuition, we are essentially taking the relation
E
, which have been computed by
Algorithm

1
, and choosing one binary tree from this set of edges.
Some care is needed to ensure that the binary tree we choose will result in a concatenation tree for an acyclic decomposition.
This is why we cannot choose any edge from
E
recursively.

Once the tree has been computed, we mark each node with a variable:

•

Mark
(1,n)
with
\mathfrak{u}
,

•

mark
(i,i)
with
x
where
\alpha[i,i]=x
,

•

and each
(i,j)
, where
i\neq j
and either
i\neq 1
or
j\neq n
, is marked with
x_{\beta}
where
\beta=\alpha[i,j]
.

We then prune the tree, as defined in
Definition

6.7
.
The resulting tree is the concatenation tree for some acyclic decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
.

Complexity.

First, we execute the algorithm given in
Theorem

6.12
which runs in
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
.
Note that after the execution of this algorithm, we have a graph
(V,E)
.
There are
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
nodes in a concatenation tree, and given a node
(i,j)
, where
i\neq j
, finding an edge
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))\in E
takes at most
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{3})
time; since there are at most
|\alpha|
such values for
j
, and making sure the relative conditions hold (see
Algorithm

17
) takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{2})
time, as we have previously discussed when discussing the time complexity for
Algorithm

1
.
Therefore, deriving the concatenation tree from
(V,E)
, without pruning, takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{4})
time.
Finally, pruning the concatenation tree takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha^{2})
time, since we consider each variable that labels a node, traverse the tree to find the
\ll
-maximum (see
Definition

6.7
), and prune accordingly.
Therefore, we can derive the concatenation tree from
V
and
E
in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{4})
.
∎
In this section, we have given a characterization for
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
to be acyclic, which lead to a polynomial-time algorithm that determines whether a terminal-free pattern is acyclic.

6.2
FC-CQ Decomposition

In this section, we generalize the idea of decomposing patterns to decomposing
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
The main result of this section is a polynomial-time algorithm that determines whether an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
can be decomposed into an acyclic
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Decomposing a word equation
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
where
x\in\Xi
and
\alpha\in(\Xi\setminus\{x\})^{+}
is the same as decomposing
\alpha
, but whereas
\mathfrak{u}
is the root variable when decomposing a pattern, we use
x
as the root variable when decomposing
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
.

In
Section

6.1
, we studied the decomposition of terminal-free patterns.
If
\varphi
is an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of the form
\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
, then the right hand side of some
\eta_{i}
may not be terminal-free.
Therefore, before defining the decomposition of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, we define a way to
normalize

\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s in order to better utilize the techniques of
Section

6.1
.

Definition 6.14
.

We call an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
with body
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})

normalized
if for all
i,j\in[n]
, we have that
\alpha_{i}\in\Xi^{+}
,
x_{i}\notin\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{i})
,
\mathfrak{u}\notin\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{i})
, and
\alpha_{i}=\alpha_{j}
if and only if
i=j
.
An
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
with body
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{m}(y_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
is
normalized
if the subformula
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
is normalized.

Since we are interested in polynomial-time algorithms, the following lemma allows us to assume that we can normalize
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s without affecting complexity claims.

Lemma 6.15
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we can construct an equivalent, normalized
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
in time
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{2})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
be an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. We give a way to construct a normalized
\varphi^{\prime}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
where
\varphi^{\prime}
is equivalent to
\varphi
.

Step 1.

For all
i\in[n]
assume that
\eta_{i}=(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
where
\alpha\in(\Sigma\mathrel{\cup}\Xi)^{*}
. We now consider the unique factorization
\alpha:=\beta_{1}\cdot\beta_{2}\cdots\beta_{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, where for all
\beta_{j}
where
j\in[k]
, either
\beta_{j}\in\Xi^{+}
or
\beta_{j}\in\Sigma^{+}
. Furthermore, if
\beta_{j}\in\Xi^{+}
then
\beta_{j+1}\in\Sigma^{+}
, and if
\beta_{j}\in\Sigma^{+}
then
\beta_{j+1}\in\Xi^{+}
for all
j\in[k-1]
. We then replace each
\beta_{i}
where
\beta_{i}\in\Sigma^{+}
with a new variable
z_{i}\in\Xi
and add the regular constraint
(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\beta_{i})
to
\varphi
. This takes linear time by scanning each
\eta_{i}
from left to right, and replacing each
\beta_{i}\in\Sigma^{+}
with a new variable and adding an extra word equation.

Step 2.

While there exists an atom of
\varphi
of the form
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot x\cdot\alpha_{2})
, we define
\psi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
with the following body:

(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}z)\land\bigwedge_{y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2})}(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}\varepsilon),

where
z\in\Xi
is a new variable. We then replace
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot x\cdot\alpha_{2})
in
\varphi
with
\psi
. We can show the
\psi
is equivalent to
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot x\cdot\alpha_{2})
by a simply length argument. Given any
\sigma
which satisfies
(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot x\cdot\alpha_{2})
, we have that
|\sigma(x)|\mathrel{=}|\sigma(\alpha_{1})|\mathrel{+}|\sigma(x)|\mathrel{+}|\sigma(\alpha_{2})|
and hence,
|\sigma(\alpha_{1})|+|\sigma(\alpha_{2})|=0
, which implies that
\sigma(\alpha_{1})=\sigma(\alpha_{2})=\varepsilon
.

Step 3.

While there exists an atom of
\varphi
of the form
\eta_{i}=(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot\mathfrak{u}\cdot\alpha_{2})
, we can replace
\eta_{i}
with the subformula
\psi
with body:

(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i})\land\bigwedge_{y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2})}(y\mathbin{\dot{=}}\varepsilon).

We show that replacing
\eta_{i}
with
\psi
results in an equivalent subformula using a length argument. It follows that
|\sigma(x_{i})|\mathrel{=}|\sigma(\alpha_{1})|\mathrel{+}|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|\mathrel{+}|\sigma(\alpha_{2})|
. Furthermore, we know that
|\sigma(x_{i})|\leq|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|
and therefore it must hold that
|\sigma(x_{i})|=|\sigma(\mathfrak{u})|
, which implies that
\sigma(x_{i})=\sigma(\mathfrak{u})
. Therefore,
|\sigma(\alpha_{1})|\mathrel{+}|\sigma(\alpha_{2})|=0
which can only hold if
\sigma(\alpha_{1})\cdot\sigma(\alpha_{2})=\varepsilon
.

The process defined takes polynomial time, since for each atom, we linearly scan the right-hand side. If we find
\mathfrak{u}
, then we replace a word equation with
\psi
, as described above. Since we perform a linear scan, this takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)
time.

Step 4.

If two atoms are identical, then one can be removed. If
\eta_{i}=(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
and
\eta_{j}=(x_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
where
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
, then we can replace
\eta_{j}
in
\varphi
with
(x_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i})
. This takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{2})
time by considering every pair of atoms.

We now conclude the proof.
As we are always replacing a subformula of
\varphi
with an equivalent subformula, it follows that the result of the above construction is equivalent and it is normalized. Furthermore, we have shown that the re-writing procedure defined takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{2})
time, and the blow up in size is
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)
.
∎

The following proof illustrates the rewriting process of queries given in the proof of
Lemma

6.15
.

Example 6.16
.

We define an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
along with an equivalent normalized
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
:

\displaystyle\varphi:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot\mathfrak{u}\cdot x_{2})\land(x_{4}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4})\land(x_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\mathtt{aab}),

\displaystyle\varphi^{\prime}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\varepsilon)\land(x_{4}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2})\land(x_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\mathtt{aab}).

We now generalize the process of decomposing patterns to decomposing
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
For every
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
, we say that
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
where
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{i}
is a
decomposition
of
\varphi
if every
\Psi_{i}
is a decomposition of
\eta_{i}
and, for all
i,j\in[n]
with
i\neq j
, the sets of introduced variables for
\Psi_{i}
and
\Psi_{j}
are disjoint.
Note that the free variables for
\varphi
and
\Psi_{\varphi}
are the same.

Example 6.17
.

Consider the following
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
along with a decomposition for each of the atoms:

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{2}\cdot y_{3})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{2}\cdot y_{3}\cdot y_{3}\cdot y_{4}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{2}\cdot y_{3}),\text{ and }

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle:=(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot y_{4})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{3}\cdot y_{3})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{2}\cdot y_{3}).

Therefore,
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}
is a decomposition of
\varphi
.

6.2.1
Acyclic FC-CQs

If every atom of
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is acyclic, then
\varphi
does not necessarily have tractable model checking.
If this were the case, then any decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of some
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
would have tractable model checking (because every word equation of the form
z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y
is acyclic).
This would imply that the membership problem for patterns can be solved in polynomial time, which contradicts
[
30
]
, unless
\mathsf{P}=\mathsf{NP}
.
Furthermore, weak acyclicity is not sufficient for tractable model checking (refer back to
Theorem

5.28
).
Therefore, we require a more refined notion of acyclicity for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Definition 6.18

(Acyclic
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s)
.

If
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is a decomposition of
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we say that
\Psi_{\varphi}
is
acyclic
if there exists a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
.
Otherwise,
\Psi_{\varphi}
is
cyclic
.
If there exists an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
, then we say that
\varphi
is
acyclic
.
Otherwise,
\varphi
is
cyclic
.

Recall that, since
\mathfrak{u}
is always mapped to
w
, we can consider
\mathfrak{u}
a constant symbol.
Therefore, if
T:=(V,E)
is a join tree for some decomposition of
\varphi
, then there can exist two nodes that both contain
\mathfrak{u}
, yet it is not necessary for all nodes on the path between these two nodes to also contain
\mathfrak{u}
.
Referring back to
Example

6.17
, we can see that
\varphi
is acyclic by executing the GYO algorithm on the decomposition.
Our next focus is to study which
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s are acyclic, and which are not.

If
T:=(V,E)
is a tree and
V^{\prime}\subset V
, then the induced subgraph of
T
on
V^{\prime}
is the graph
G:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
where we have the edge
e\in E^{\prime}
if and only if
e\in E
and the two endpoints of
e
are in the set
V^{\prime}
. Notice that
G
is not necessarily a tree because
G
may not be connected.
However, it is always a so-called
forest
.
For our purposes, a forest is an undirected graph, with no cycles.
The only difference between a forest and a tree is that a forest may not be connected.

Lemma 6.19
.

If
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is a decomposition of
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
, and we have a join tree
T:=(V,E)
for
\Psi_{\varphi}
, then we can partition
T
into
T^{1},T^{2},\dots T^{n}
such that for each
i\in[n]
, we have that
T^{i}
is a join tree for a decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be an acyclic formula defined as
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
.
Let
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
and let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree of
\Psi_{\varphi}
.
By definition,
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{i}
where
\Psi_{i}
is a decomposition of
\eta_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
.
Since
V
contains all atoms of
\Psi
, it follows that all atoms of
\Psi_{i}
are in
V
.

Let
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
be the induced subgraph of
T
on the atoms of
\Psi_{i}
.
We now prove that
T^{i}
is a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
.
By definition, we know that all atoms of
\Psi_{i}
are present in
T^{i}
and that no cycles exist in
T^{i}
(since it is a subgraph of a tree
T
).
Therefore, to show that the resulting structure is a join tree it is sufficient to show that this structure is connected.

We prove that
T^{i}
is connected by a contradiction.
Assume
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{3}),(z_{4}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{5}\cdot z_{6})\in V^{i}
are not connected.
Let
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
be the concatenation tree for
\Psi_{i}
.
Let
v_{1},v_{n}\in\mathcal{V}
be non-leaf nodes of
\mathcal{T}
such that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})=(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{3})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})=(z_{4}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{5}\cdot z_{6})
. Let
(v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{n})
be the sequence of nodes which exist on the path in the concatenation tree from
v_{1}
to
v_{n}
.
Let
k\in[n]
such that
v_{k}\in\mathcal{V}
is the lowest common ancestor of
v_{1}
and
v_{n}
.

Notice that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i+1})
for all
i\in[k-1]
share the variable that labels
v_{i}
.
Therefore, since
T
is a join tree, these nodes are connected via a path where each node that lies on that path contains the variable that labels
v_{i}
.

We know that any node that is removed does not contain the variable
v_{i}
since it is an introduced variable for
\Psi_{i}
.
Therefore, the variable that labels
v_{i}
is not present in any atom of
\Psi_{j}
for any
j\in[n]\setminus\{i\}
.
Hence,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{i+1})
must be connected for all
i\in[k-1]
in the structure resulting from the above manipulating the join tree.
Thus,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
are connected in this structure by transitivity.
The analogous reasoning means that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{k})
are connected in
T^{i}
.
Hence,
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
is connected in the resulting structure and we have reached the desired contradiction.
If
v_{1}
is an ancestor of
v_{n}
(or vice versa), then the fact that
\mathsf{atom}(v_{1})
and
\mathsf{atom}(v_{n})
are connected in
T^{i}
follows trivially.

Consequently, there is a subtree of
T:=(V,E)
that is a join tree for the decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.
Due to the fact that the body of
\Psi_{\varphi}
is
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{i}
where
\Psi_{i}
is a decomposition of
\eta_{i}
such that the set of introduced variables for
\Psi_{i}
is disjoint from the introduced variables for
\Psi_{j}
, where
i\neq j
, it follows that
V^{i}\mathrel{\cap}V^{j}=\emptyset
for
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
and
T^{j}:=(V^{j},E^{j})
.
∎

Our next consideration is sufficient (but not necessary) conditions for an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to be cyclic.
Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
be a normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Recall that a join tree
T:=(V,E)
for
\varphi
where
V=\{\eta_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
is called a weak join tree.
If there exists a weak join tree for
\varphi
, then we say that
\varphi
is weakly acyclic. Otherwise,
\varphi
is weakly cyclic.
As seen in
Theorem

5.28
, weak acyclicity is not sufficient for tractability.

Lemma 6.20
.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
be a normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. If any of the following conditions holds, then
\varphi
is cyclic:

1.

\varphi
is weakly cyclic,

2.

\eta_{i}
is cyclic for any
i\in[n]
,

3.

|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|>3
for any
i,j\in[n]
where
i\neq j
, or

4.

|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|=3
, and
|\eta_{i}|>3
or
|\eta_{j}|>3
for any
i,j\in[n]
where
i\neq j
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
be an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, and let
\Psi_{\varphi}
be an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
.
If
T:=(V,E)
is a join tree of
\Psi_{\varphi}
, then for each
i\in[n]
, we use
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
to denote the subtree of
T
that is a join tree for the decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.
From
Lemma

6.19
, we can conclude that
T^{i}
and
T^{j}
are disjoint for all
i,j\in[n]
where
i\neq j
.

We now prove that if any of the conditions stated in the lemma statement hold, then
\varphi
is cyclic.

Condition 1.

For sake of a contradiction, assume
\varphi
is an acyclic, normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
which is weakly cyclic.
Let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
. From
Lemma

6.19
, it follows that for each
i\in[n]
there exists a subtree
T^{i}
of
T_{\varphi}
which is a join tree for a decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.
From
T
, we construct a weak join tree for
\varphi
.
Let
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
where
V_{w}:=\{\eta_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
, and
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
if and only if there is an edge
\{v_{i},v_{j}\}\in E
where
v_{i}\in V^{i}
and
v_{j}\in V^{j}
for each
i,j\in[n]
where
i\neq j
.
We now prove that this is a weak join tree for
\varphi
.

For sake of contradiction, assume that
T_{w}
is not a weak join tree for
\varphi
.
By the procedure used to compute
T_{w}
we know that
V_{w}=\{\eta_{i}\mid i\in[n]\}
, and that this structure is a tree (because if
T_{w}
is not a tree, then
T
is not a tree).
Therefore, if
T_{w}
is not a join tree, it follows that there exists
\eta_{i}\in V_{w}
and
\eta_{j}\in V_{w}
such that there is some variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})
where some node
\eta_{k}\in V_{w}
exists on the path between
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
in
T_{w}
, and
x\notin\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{k})
.
If this is the case, then
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{j})
, and
x\notin\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{k})
.
Hence there is a path between two nodes in
T
which contain the variable
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\varphi})
, which are atoms of
\Psi_{i}
and
\Psi_{j}
, yet there is a node on the path between these nodes which does not contain the variable
x
, which is some atom of
\Psi_{k}
.
Therefore,
T
is not a join tree and we have reached a contradiction.
Consequently,
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
is a weak join tree for
\varphi
and hence if
\varphi
is weakly cyclic, we can conclude that
\varphi
is cyclic.

Condition 2.

This follows directly from
Lemma

6.19
.
Since for any join tree
T:=(V,E)
of a decomposition of
\varphi
, there exists a subtree which is a join tree for some decomposition of
\eta_{i}
, we can conclude that if
\eta_{i}
is cyclic, then
\varphi
is cyclic.

Condition 3.

For sake of a contradiction, assume that
\varphi
is acyclic, and assume that
|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|>3
for some
i,j\in[n]
where
i\neq j
.
Let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
.
Let
T^{i}
and
T^{j}
be subtrees of
T
which are join trees for the decompositions of
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
respectively.
Note that these trees are disjoint.

Let
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot y_{1})
and
(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot y_{2})
be nodes of
T^{i}
and
T^{j}
respectively, such that
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot y_{1})
is the closest node (with regards to distance) to any node in
T^{j}
, and
(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot y_{2})
is the closest node to any node in
T^{i}
, these nodes are well defined because
T
is a tree. Notice that
|\mathsf{vars}(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot y_{1})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot y_{2})|\leq 3
. Therefore, there is a node of
T^{i}
which shares a variable with some node of
T^{j}
, yet this variable does not exist on the path between these nodes, since
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot y_{1})
must exist on such a path.
Thus, we have a contradiction.

Condition 4.

Again, we work towards a contradiction.
Assume that
\varphi
is acyclic and there exists
i,j\in[n]
, where
i\neq j
, such that
|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|=3
and
|\eta_{i}|>3
(the other case is symmetric).
Let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. Let
T^{i}
be the subtree of
T
which is a join tree for
\eta_{i}
and let
T^{j}
be the subtree of
T
which is a join tree for
\eta_{j}
.
Since we have that
|\eta_{i}|>3
, we decompose
\eta_{i}
into
\Psi_{i}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. Note that for each atom of
\Psi_{i}
, there is a variable
z\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{i})\setminus\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{j})
.
This holds due to the fact that the set of introduced variables for
\Psi_{i}
is disjoint from the set of introduced variables for
\Psi_{j}
, unless
i=j
.

Therefore the maximum number of shared variable between an atom of
\Psi_{i}
and an atom of
\Psi_{j}
is
2
.
Using the same argument in Condition 3, this results in a contradiction.
Therefore, our assumption that
\varphi
is acyclic cannot hold.

To conclude this proof, we have shown that if any of the four conditions given in the lemma statement hold for
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, then
\varphi
is acyclic.
∎

While Conditions 3 and 4 might seem strict, we can pre-factor common subpatterns.
For example, we can write
(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdot\alpha_{3})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{4}\cdot\alpha_{2}\cdot\alpha_{5})
, where
\alpha_{i}\in\Xi^{+}
for
i\in[5]
, as
(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{1}\cdot z\cdot\alpha_{3})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{4}\cdot z\cdot\alpha_{5})\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{2})
where
z\in\Xi
is a new variable.
We illustrate this further in the following example.

Example 6.21
.

Consider the following
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
:

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{2}\cdot y_{3}\cdot y_{4}\cdot y_{5})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{6}\cdot y_{2}\cdot y_{3}\cdot y_{4}\cdot y_{5}).

Using condition 3 of
Lemma

6.20
, we see that
\varphi
is cyclic. However, since the right-hand side of the two word equations share a common subpattern, we can rewrite
\varphi
as

\varphi^{\prime}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot z)\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{6}\cdot z)\land(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{2}\cdot y_{3}\cdot y_{4}\cdot y_{5}).

One could alter our definition of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
decomposition so that if two atoms are bracketing such that they share a common subbracketing, then the two occurrences of that common subbracketing are replaced with the same variable (analogously to decomposing patterns).
The author believes it is likely that such a definition of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
decomposition is equivalent to our definition of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
decomposition after “factoring out” common subpatterns between atoms.

Our next consideration is how the shape of a join tree for a decomposition of an acyclic query
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
relates to the weak join tree for
\varphi
.

Definition 6.22

(Skeleton Tree)
.

Let
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be an acyclic decomposition of the
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
, and let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
.
We say that a weak join tree
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
is the

skeleton tree
of
T
if there exists an edge in
E
from a node in
V^{i}
to a node in
V^{j}
if and only if
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
.

In the proof of
Lemma

6.20
(Condition 1), we show that every join tree for a decomposition has a corresponding skeleton tree.

Example 6.23
.

We define
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and a decomposition
\Psi_{\varphi}
as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{3}\cdot x_{2})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4}\cdot x_{4}\cdot x_{5}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{\varphi}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{1})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{2})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{5})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4}\cdot x_{4}).

The skeleton tree along with the join tree of
\Psi_{\varphi}
are given in
Figure

6.7
.

x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot z_{1}
z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{2}
x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot x_{5}
z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4}\cdot x_{4}
x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{3}\cdot x_{2}
x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4}\cdot x_{4}\cdot x_{5}

Figure 6.7
:
The join tree of (left) and the skeleton tree of the join tree (right) for
Example

6.23
.

One might assume that some skeleton trees are more “desirable” than others in terms of using it for finding an acyclic decomposition of an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
However, as we observe next, any skeleton tree is sufficient.

Lemma 6.24
.

Let
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be a decomposition of
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
. If
\Psi_{\varphi}
is acyclic, then any weak join tree for
\varphi
can be used as the skeleton tree.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\eta_{i}
be a normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and let
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\Psi_{i}
be an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
such that
\Psi_{i}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is the decomposition of
\eta_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
. Let
T:=(V,E)
be a join tree of
\Psi_{\varphi}
and let
T_{s}:=(V_{s},E_{s})
be the skeleton tree of
T
.

We work towards a contradiction. Assume
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
is a weak join tree for
\varphi
, but there does not exist a join tree
T^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
of
\Psi_{\varphi}
such that
T_{w}
is the skeleton tree of
T^{\prime}
. We now transform
T
to obtain the join tree
T^{\prime}
, and thus reach our contradiction.

For each
i\in[n]
, let
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
be the subtree of
T
such that
T^{i}
is a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
. We know that these subtrees are disjoint. Let
F:=(V_{f},E_{f})
be a forest where
V_{f}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}V^{i}
and
E_{f}:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E^{i}
. Then, for each edge
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
, let
\chi_{i,j}
be the atom of
\Psi_{i}
and
\chi_{j,i}
the atom of
\Psi_{j}
such that these are the end nodes in the shortest path from any atom of
\Psi_{i}
to any atom of
\Psi_{j}
in
T
. Then, add the edge
\{\chi_{i,j},\chi_{j,i}\}
to
E_{f}
for each
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
. Let
T^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
be the result of the above augmentation of
T
.

We now prove that
T^{\prime}:=(V^{\prime},E^{\prime})
is a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
. We can see that
T^{\prime}
is a tree, every atom of
\Psi_{\varphi}
is a node of
T^{\prime}
, and that

\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i,j})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{j,i})=\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j}),

(6.1)

which holds because otherwise
T
would not be a join tree (see Conditions 3 and 4 of
Lemma

6.20
).
We use
Equation

6.1
to show that every node that lies on the path between any
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in V^{\prime}
where
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi)\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi^{\prime})
, also contains the variable
x
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\chi\in V^{1}
and
\chi^{\prime}\in V^{k}
where
V^{1}
and
V^{k}
are the set of vertices for the join tree for the decomposition of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{k}
respectively.
Further assume that the path from
\eta_{1}
to
\eta_{k}
in
T_{w}
consists of
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{i+1}\}
for
i\in[k-1]
.
Since
T_{w}
is a weak join tree, and that
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{k}
both contain the variable
x
, it follows that for all
i\in[k-1]
, the word equation
\eta_{i}
contains the variable
x
.
Furthermore, we know that for any any edge
\{\chi_{i},\chi_{i+1}\}\in E^{\prime}
, where
\chi_{i}\in V^{i}
and
\chi_{i+1}\in V^{i+1}
, that
\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})=\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i+1})
. Therefore, it follows that
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})
. Because
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
is a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
, every node that lies on the path between two nodes of
V^{i}
which have the variable
x
, also has the variable
x
. Furthermore, for any edge
\{\chi_{i},\chi_{i+1}\}\in E^{\prime}
, where
\chi_{i}\in V^{i}
and
\chi_{i+1}\in V^{i+1}
, we know that
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})
. Hence, all nodes on the path between
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
contain
x
.
∎

Given a weak join tree of an acyclic query
\varphi
, the proof of
Lemma

6.24
transforms the
join tree of
\Psi_{\varphi}
so that the resulting join tree has the given weak join tree as its skeleton
tree. Thus, we can use any weak join tree as a “template” for the eventual join tree of the
decomposition (assuming the query is acyclic).

While
Lemma

6.20
and
Lemma

6.24
give some insights and necessary conditions for deciding whether
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is acyclic, these conditions are not sufficient.
We therefore give the following lemma which is needed in the proof of
Theorem

6.2.29
to find the sufficient conditions for
\varphi
to be acyclic.
To illustrate the purpose of the subsequent lemmas, consider the following example.

Example 6.25
.

Consider the following
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
along with the decomposition of each of the atoms.

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{2}\cdot y_{3})\land(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{4}\cdot y_{3}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}

\displaystyle:=(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot y_{3})\land(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{2}),

\displaystyle\Psi_{2}

\displaystyle:=(x_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot y_{3})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{1}\cdot y_{4}).

While
\varphi
is weakly acyclic,
\Psi_{1}
and
\Psi_{2}
are acyclic, and the two atoms of
\varphi
only share two variables,
\varphi
is not acyclic.
It is straightforward to show that
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow\Psi_{1}\land\Psi_{2}
is not acyclic using the GYO algorithm.

Thus, we state another key lemma, which shall be given in
Lemma

6.2.27
.
But first, we deal with a special case.

Lemma 6.26
.

Given
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
and
C\subseteq\{\{x,y\}\mid x,y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\text{ and }x\neq y\}
,
we can decide in polynomial time whether there exists an acyclic
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
such that for each
\{x,y\}\in C
, either
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
or
(y\cdot x)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Let
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
and let
C\subseteq\{\{x,y\}\mid x,y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\text{ and }x\neq y\}
.

We assume that every variable that appears in
C
also appears in the input pattern.
Otherwise, we can immediately return
{\mathsf{False}}
.
This initial check can clearly be done in polynomial time.

The algorithm used to solve the problem stated in the lemma is given in
Algorithm

24
.
This is a variation of the algorithm given in
Theorem

6.12
, but
V
and
E^{\prime}
are initialized differently.
There is also an extra subroutine given in
Algorithm

32
to deal with a special case.
It follows from the proof of
Theorem

6.12
that if then
Algorithm

24
returns
{\mathsf{True}}
, then an acyclic concatenation tree can be derived from
E
and
V
in polynomial time.
We first look at the correctness.

Input :

\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
, where
|\alpha|=n
.

Output :

True if
\alpha
is acyclic, and False otherwise.

E^{\prime}\leftarrow\{((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1))\mathrel{|}\text{ for all }c\in C\text{ we have }(i,i),(i+1,i+1)\notin c\}
;

E^{\prime}\leftarrow E^{\prime}\mathrel{\cup}\{((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1))\mathrel{|}\{(i,i),(i+1,i+1)\}\in C\}
;

V
is the set of nodes in
E^{\prime}
;

Add
(i,i)
to
V
for all
i\in[n]
;

E\leftarrow\emptyset
;

while

E^{\prime}\neq E

do

E\leftarrow E^{\prime}
;

for

i,k\in[n]
where
i<k-1

do

25

for

j\in\{i,i+1,\dots,k-1\}
where
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))\notin E^{\prime}

do

26

if

(i,j),(j+1,k)\in V
and
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}(i,j,k,\alpha,E^{\prime})
and
\mathsf{extraCheck}(i,j,k,\alpha,C)

then

27

Add
((i,k),(i,j),(j+1,k))
to
E^{\prime}
;
Add
(i,k)
to
V
;
end if

28

end for

29

end for

30

end while

31

Return
\mathsf{True}
if
(1,n)\in V
, and
\mathsf{False}
otherwise;

Algorithm 4

A variant of the Acyclic Pattern Algorithm. The subroutine
\mathsf{IsAcyclic}
(
Algorithm

9
) is identical to how it was given in the proof of
Theorem

6.12
.

Input :

i,j,k,\alpha,C

Output :

False, if
\{x,y\}\in C
and
x
is concatenated to
\tilde{\beta}
where
\mathsf{vars}(\tilde{\beta})\neq\{x,y\}
.

True, otherwise.

if

i=j
and there exists
\{x,y\}\in C
where
\alpha[i,j]\in\{x,y\}

then

if

\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\{x,y\}

then

33

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else

34

Return
\mathsf{False}
;
end if

35

else if

j=k
and there exists
\{x,y\}\in C
where
\alpha[j+1,k]\in\{x,y\}

then

36

if

\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[i,j])=\{x,y\}

then

37

Return
\mathsf{True}
;

else

38

Return
\mathsf{False}
;
end if

39

else

40

Return
\mathsf{True}

end if

41

Algorithm 5

\mathsf{extraCheck}(i,j,k,\alpha,C)
. This algorithm ensures that if some
x\in\Xi
, where
\{x,y\}\in C
is concatenated to some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
, then
\mathsf{vars}(\tilde{\beta})=\{x,y\}
.

Correctness.

Algorithm

24
initializes
E^{\prime}
such that one of the following conditions must hold:

1.

\bigl\{\bigl((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1)\bigr)\bigr\}\in E^{\prime}
where
\{(i,i),(i+1,i+1)\}\in C
, or

2.

\bigl\{\bigl((i,i+1),(i,i),(i+1,i+1)\bigr)\bigr\}\in E^{\prime}
where for all
c\in C
we have that
(i,i)\notin c
and
(i+1,i+1)\notin c
.

Line 62 ensures that
i<k-1
.
This avoids the case where
(i,i+1)
does not satisfy one of the aforementioned conditions, but is added to
V
.

Algorithm

32
ensures that if some
x\in\Xi
, where
\{x,y\}\in C
is concatenated to some
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}
, then the set of variables in
\tilde{\beta}
is
\{x,y\}
.
We now consider two cases. We note that we use the shorthand
\mathsf{vars}(\tilde{\alpha})
for any
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}
to denote the set variables that appears in
\tilde{\alpha}
.

Case 1: If
\tilde{\alpha}
exists, then
Algorithm

24
returns true.

This direction follows from the proof of
Theorem

6.12
.
However, we need to prove that the new restrictions added to
Algorithm

24
ensures that if such an
\tilde{\alpha}
(that satisfies the conditions given in the lemma statement) exists, then
Algorithm

24
still returns true.

Let
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
and
C\subseteq\{\{x,y\}\mid x,y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\text{ and }x\neq y\}
. Let
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
such that for each
\{x,y\}\in C
, either
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
or
(y\cdot x)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
.

Due to the initialization of
E^{\prime}
and
V
, we know that if
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
for
x,y\in\Xi
, then either there exist some
\{x,y\}\in C
; or for all
\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}\in C
, we have that
x\notin\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}
and
y\notin\{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\}
.
To show that this is the correct behaviour, we prove the following claim:

Claim 1
.

If, without loss of generality,
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
for some
\{x,y\}\in C
, and
(x\cdot z)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
where
z\notin\{x,y\}
, then
\tilde{\alpha}
is cyclic.

Proof of Claim.
To prove this claim, we work towards a contradiction.
Let
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
and assume that
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
is acyclic where
(x\cdot y),(x\cdot z)\sqsubset\tilde{\alpha}
and
z\notin\{x,y\}
.
Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}
.
We can see that both
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
and
(z^{\prime}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot z)
are atoms of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
where
z\neq z^{\prime}
.
Let
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
be the concatenation tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.
It follows that, there exists two nodes
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{V}
where
\tau(v)=z
and
\tau(v^{\prime})=z^{\prime}
where
z
and
z^{\prime}
are
x
-parents.
Consider the lowest common ancestor of
z
and
z^{\prime}
.
This lowest common ancestor is not an
x
-parent, since it must be a parent of two nodes labelled with an introduced variable, yet it lies on the path between
z
and
z^{\prime}
.
Hence,
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is not
x
-localized and it therefore follows that
\tilde{\alpha}
is cyclic.

\blacksquare

(Claim)

Observing this claim, the initialization of
E^{\prime}
and
V
is the desired behaviour.

Next, we look at
Algorithm

32
.
Assume that without loss of generality
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
for all
\{x,y\}\in C
, and
\tilde{\alpha}
is acyclic.
It follows that there exists a node
v_{1}
with two children
v_{2}
and
v_{3}
such that
\tau(v_{2})=x
and
\tau(v_{3})=y
.
Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}
, and let
\mathcal{T}
be the concatenation tree for
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
.
Since
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is acyclic, it must be both
x
-localized and
y
-localized.
Therefore, since
v_{1}
is itself an
x
-parent, all
x
parents form a subtree of
\mathcal{T}
which is connected to
v_{1}
.
Hence, if
x
is concatenated to
\tilde{\beta}
in
\tilde{\alpha}
, it follows that
\mathsf{vars}(\tilde{\beta})=\{x,y\}
must hold.

Case 2: If
Algorithm

24
returns true, then
\tilde{\alpha}
exists.

If after
Algorithm

24
terminates we have
(1,n)\in V
, then
\alpha
is acyclic and we can derive a concatenation tree for some acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, see the proof of
Theorem

6.13
.
The derivation procedure adds edges from
E
to the concatenation tree until the leaf nodes are all
(i,i)
for
i\in[n]
.
Hence, if a node has the children
(i,i)
and
(i+1,i+1)
, it follows that these nodes must satisfy the conditions defined in the initialization of
E
.
We now show that
\{x,y\}\in C
, either
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
or
(y\cdot x)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
.
For sake of a contradiction, assume that there exists some
\{x,y\}\in C
such that, without loss of generality,
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
does not hold.
Due to the initialization of
E^{\prime}
, it follows that there cannot exist some
(x\cdot z)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
such that
z\neq y
.
Furthermore, if
x\in\Xi
is concatenated to some
\tilde{\beta}\sqsubset\tilde{\alpha}
, then it follows that
\mathsf{vars}(\tilde{\beta})=\{x,y\}
.

Hence, without loss of generality,
(x\cdot y)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\beta}
holds.
We also do a preprocessing step to make sure that all the variables that appear in
C
, also appear in
\alpha
.
Therefore, the resulting concatenation tree represents an acyclic bracketing
\tilde{\alpha}
of the input pattern
\alpha
, where
(x\cdot y)
or
(y\cdot x)
is a subbracketing of
\tilde{\alpha}
for all
\{x,y\}\in C
.

Complexity.

Due to the fact that
Algorithm

24
is almost identical to the algorithm given in the proof of
Theorem

6.12
, it is sufficient to prove that it takes polynomial time to initialize
V
and
E
, and that the
Algorithm

32
is in polynomial time. We can assume that we precompute the set
C^{\prime}:=\bigcup_{s\in C}s
.
Precomputing
C^{\prime}
can clearly be done in polynomial time.

We first consider the initialization of
V
and
E^{\prime}
.
For each
i\in[n-1]
, we check whether
\{\alpha[i],\alpha[i+1]\}\in C
, and if that is false, we check whether
\alpha[i],\alpha[i+1]\notin C^{\prime}
.
Therefore, the initialization of
E^{\prime}
takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
, since the checks for each
i\in[n-1]
takes constant time, and adding to
E^{\prime}
takes constant time.
Furthermore, adding all nodes of
E^{\prime}
to
V
takes
\mathcal{O}(|E^{\prime}|)
time, and since for the initialization,
|E^{\prime}|\in\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
, this also takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
time.

Now, we consider the time complexity of the
\mathsf{extraCheck}
subroutine.
Deciding whether
i=j
and
\alpha[i]\in C^{\prime}
takes constant time (line 47), and deciding whether
\mathsf{vars}(\alpha[j+1,k])=\{x,y\}
takes
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
time.
Since the other case is symmetric, the total running time of
\mathsf{extraCheck}
is
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|)
.
Therefore, it follows from the proof of
Theorem

6.12
that
Algorithm

24
runs in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
.

Lemma

6.26
is the main case for the following key lemma.

Lemma 6.2.27
.

Given a normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of the form
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
and a set
C\subseteq\{\{x,y\}\mid x,y\in\mathsf{vars}(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)\text{ and }x\neq y\}
, we can decide in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
whether there is an acyclic decomposition
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\varphi
such that, for every
\{x,y\}\in C
, there is an atom of
\Psi
that contains both
x
and
y
.

Proof 6.2.28
.

If for all
\{x,y\}\in C
, we have that
x,y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)
, then we know that this problem can be decided in time
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
.
We use
Lemma

6.26
and decide whether there exists an acyclic bracketing
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
such that
(x\cdot y)\sqsubset\tilde{\alpha}
or
(y\cdot x)\sqsubseteq\tilde{\alpha}
.
If such a decomposition exists, it follows that
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
or
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot x)
, for some
z_{1}\in\Xi
, is an atom in the decomposition of
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
, where
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
is the bracketing used for the decomposition.

If for some
\{x,y\}\in C
, we have that
x=z
, then we know
y\in\mathsf{vars}(\alpha)\setminus\{z\}
since
x\neq y
.
We now claim that the acyclic decomposition
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
exists, in the case where
x=z
, if and only if there exists
i,j\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\alpha=y^{i}\cdot\beta\cdot y^{j}
where
\beta\in\Xi^{*}
is acyclic and
|\beta|_{y}=0
.

For the if direction, we give the following bracketing of
\alpha
:

\tilde{\alpha}:=(((y\cdot(\cdots(y\cdot(y\cdot\tilde{\beta})))\cdot y)\cdots)\cdot y),

where
\tilde{\beta}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\beta)
and the decomposition,
\Psi_{\tilde{\beta}}
, of
\tilde{\beta}
is acyclic. We can see that
\tilde{\alpha}
is decomposed
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
which is acyclic since
\tilde{\beta}
is acyclic, and we are repeatedly prepending
y
symbols before repeatedly appending
y
symbols. Therefore,
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
y
-localized and
x^{\prime}
-localized for all
x^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\beta}})
. Furthermore, we have that
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}z^{\prime}\cdot y)
, for some
z^{\prime}\in\Xi
, is an atom of the decomposition.

We now prove the only if direction.
Let
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
be an acyclic decomposition of
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
such that some atom of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
contains the variables
z
and
y
.
Let
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
be the concatenation tree for
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}(\alpha)
, where
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is the decomposition of
\tilde{\alpha}
.
Since
z
only appears in the root atom of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
, we know that for
y
and
z
to appear in the same atom, the root atom of
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
must contain the variable
y
.
That is, the root atom is either
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}y\cdot z^{\prime})
or
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}z^{\prime}\cdot y)
for some
z^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
.
It therefore follows that there exists
\{v_{1},v_{2}\},\{v_{1},v_{3}\}\in\mathcal{E}
, where
v_{2}<v_{3}
, such that
\tau(v_{1})=z
and either
\tau(v_{2})=y
or
\tau(v_{3})=y
and where
v_{1}\in\mathcal{V}
is the root of the concatenation tree.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{y}
be the induced sub-tree of
\mathcal{T}
which contains only
y
-parents along with their children.
We know that
\mathcal{T}_{y}
is connected because
\Psi
is
y
-localized; since
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is acyclic.
We also know that the root of the tree is a
y
-parent.
Thus, each
y
can only contribute to the prefix or suffix of
\alpha
and hence
\alpha=y^{i}\cdot\beta\cdot y^{j}
where
|\beta|_{y}=0
must hold (e.g., see
Figure

6.8
).

v_{1}\;(z)
v_{2}\;(y)
v_{3}\;(z_{1})
v_{4}\;(z_{2})
v_{5}\;(y)
v_{6}\;(y)
v_{7}\;(z_{2})
\vdots
\vdots

Figure 6.8
:
A diagram of
\mathcal{T}_{y}
used to illustrate the proof of
Lemma

6.2.27
.

Therefore, to decide whether
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha)
can be decomposed into an acyclic formula
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that there exists an atom of
\Psi
which has the variables
z
and
y
, it is sufficient to decide whether
\alpha=y^{i}\cdot\beta\cdot y^{j}
where
\beta
is acyclic and
|\beta|_{y}=0
.
This can obviously be decided in
\mathcal{O}(|\alpha|^{7})
time by removing the prefix
y^{i}
and the suffix
y^{j}
in linear time, then checking whether
\beta
is acyclic.
Note that exactly one element of
C
contains the variable
z
due to the fact that if two elements of
C
are not disjoint, then we know that
\Psi
does not exist.
Therefore, after we have dealt with this case, we can continue with the procedure defined in
Lemma

6.26
to determine whether whether there is an acyclic decomposition
\Psi\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\varphi
such that for every
\{x,y\}\in C
, there exists an atom
(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{3})
of
\Psi
where
\{x,y\}\subseteq\mathsf{vars}(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{2}\cdot z_{3})
in
\mathcal{O}(n^{7})
.

The purposes of
Lemma

6.2.27
should become clearer after giving the following necessary and sufficient criteria for an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
to be acyclic:
Let

\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\land\bigwedge_{j=1}^{n}(y_{j}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma_{j})

be a normalized
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Then, there exists an acyclic decomposition
\Psi\in 2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\varphi
if and only if the following hold:

1.

\varphi
is weakly acyclic,

2.

the pattern
\alpha_{i}
is acyclic for all
i\in[m]
, and

3.

for every
i\in[m]
, there is a decomposition
\Psi_{i}
of
x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i}
such that for all
j\in[m]\setminus\{i\}
there is a decomposition
\Psi_{j}
of
x_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{j}
where

\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{j})=\mathsf{vars}(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(x_{j}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{j}),

for some atoms
\chi_{i}
of
\Psi_{i}
and
\chi_{j}
of
\Psi_{j}
.

We now give the main result of this chapter.

Theorem 6.2.29
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we can decide in time
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{8})
whether it is acyclic.

Proof 6.2.30
.

Let
\varphi:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\eta_{i}
be a normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, where
\eta_{i}:=(x_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}\alpha_{i})
for all
i\in[m]
. We first rule out some cases where
\varphi
must be cyclic (see
Lemma

6.20
):

1.

If
\varphi
is weakly cyclic, then return “
\varphi
is cyclic”, otherwise let
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
be a weak join tree for
\varphi
.

2.

If there exists
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
such that
|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|>3
then return “
\varphi
is cyclic”.

3.

If there exists an edge
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
where
|\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})|=3
and
|\eta_{i}|>3
or
|\eta_{j}|>3
, then return “
\varphi
is cyclic”.

We then label every edge,
e\in E_{w}
, with the set of variables that the two endpoints share.
For every atom
\eta_{i}
of
\varphi
, we create the set
C_{i}\in\mathcal{P}(\Xi)
.
We define
C_{i}
by considering every outgoing edge of
\eta_{i}
in
T_{w}
, and taking a union of the sets that label of those edges. We now give a construction to find an acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\varphi
, if one exists.

If
|C_{i}|=0
, then let
\Psi_{i}
be any acyclic decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.
If
\mathsf{max}_{k\in C_{i}}(|k|)=1
then let
\Psi_{i}
be any acyclic decomposition of
\eta_{i}
.
If
\mathsf{max}_{k\in C_{i}}(|k|)=2
then we can use
Lemma

6.2.27
to obtain the acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{i}
of
\eta_{i}
such that for all
k\in C_{i}
where
|k|=2
, there is an atom of
\Psi_{i}
which contains the variables of
k
.
If
\mathsf{max}_{k\in C_{i}}(|k|)=3
then we know that
|\eta_{i}|\leq 3
, and therefore
\Psi_{i}=\eta_{i}
(see
Lemma

6.20
).

Claim 2
.

Assume there does not exist an acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{i}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\eta_{i}
such that for all
k\in C_{i}
where
|k|=2
, there is an atom of
\Psi_{i}
which contains all the variables of
k
. Then,
\varphi
is cyclic.

Proof of Claim.
We prove this claim by working towards a contradiction. Assume that there exists
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
which is an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
, and that there exists two atoms
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
such that there does not exist an acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{i}
of
\eta_{i}
where some atom of
\Psi_{i}
is of the form
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
, where
\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})=\mathsf{vars}(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})
.

Let
T:=(V,E)
be the join-tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
. We know from
Lemma

6.19
that there exists a sub-tree of
T
which is a join tree for the decompositions of
\eta_{i}
and
\eta_{j}
. Let
T^{i}
be the sub-tree of
T
which represents a join-tree for
\Psi_{i}
(the decomposition of
\eta_{i}
), and let
T^{j}
be the sub-tree of
T
which is a join-tree for
\Psi_{j}
(the decomposition of
\eta_{j}
).
Let
p
be the shortest in path in
T
from some node in
T^{i}
to some node in
T^{j}
. Because
T
is a tree, this path is uniquely defined. However, there does not exist a node
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
of
T^{j}
such that
\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})=\mathsf{vars}(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})
.
Therefore, there is some variable
z^{\prime}\in\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})
where
z^{\prime}
is not a variable of every atom on the path
p
. Therefore
T
is not a join tree.

\blacksquare

(Claim)

Once we have an acyclic formula
\Psi_{i}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
for all
i\in[m]
, we can define
\Psi_{\varphi}\in\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
as an acyclic decomposition of
\varphi
as
\Psi_{\varphi}:=\mathsf{Ans}(\vec{x})\leftarrow\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\Psi_{i}
.

Complexity.

We now prove that, given the normalized
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
we can decide in polynomial time whether
\varphi
is acyclic.

First, construct a weak join tree for
\varphi
, which takes polynomial time using the GYO algorithm, and we label each edge with the variables that the two end points of that edge share (which takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{2})
time).

We then find an acyclic decomposition of each
\eta_{i}
in polynomial time using
Theorem

6.12
; and if
\eta_{i}
shares two variables with another atom, then we use
Lemma

6.2.27
to find an acyclic decomposition in polynomial time.
Since there are
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)
atoms of
\varphi
, constructing the decomposition
\Psi_{i}
for all atoms
\eta_{i}
of
\varphi
takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi||\eta_{\mathsf{max}}|^{7})
time, where
\eta_{\mathsf{max}}
is the largest
|\eta_{i}|
of any
i\in[m]
.
Then, let
\Psi_{\varphi}
have the body
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\Psi_{i}
and let
\Psi_{\varphi}
have the same free variables as
\varphi
.
This last step takes
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|)
since we are just conjuncting the decompositions of each atoms, and setting the free variables.

Therefore, we can construct the acyclic formula
\Psi_{\varphi}
in time
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi||\eta_{\mathsf{max}}|^{7})
.
Since
|\eta_{\mathsf{max}}|=|\varphi|
if
m=1
, we get the final running time of
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{8})
.
While
\varphi
is not necessarily normalized, we know from
Lemma

6.15
that normalizing
\varphi
can be done in
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{2})
.
Furthermore, in the proof of
Lemma

6.15
, the resulting normalized
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is of size
\mathcal{O}(n)
, where
n
is the size of the input
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
Therefore, this does not affect the complexity claims of this lemma.

Correctness.

To prove that
\Psi_{\varphi}
is acyclic, we construct a join tree for
\Psi_{\varphi}
using the weak join tree
T_{w}:=(V_{w},E_{w})
as the skeleton tree. Let
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
be a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
for each
i\in[m]
.

Let
T:=(V,E)
be a forest where
V:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}V^{i}
and let
E:=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}E^{i}
.
For any
\chi_{i}\in V^{i}
and
\chi_{j}\in V^{j}
, we add an edge
\{\chi_{i},\chi_{j}\}\in E
if and only if
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{j}\}\in E_{w}
and
\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{j})=\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{j})
.
We know that
\Psi_{\varphi}
are nodes of
V
, and
T
is a tree.
Therefore, to show that
T:=(V,E)
is a join tree, it is sufficient to prove that for any
\chi,\chi^{\prime}\in V
where
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi)\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi^{\prime})
, every node that lies on the path between
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
in
T
contains the variable
x
.
We include the proof for completeness sake, even though it is analogous to the proof of
Lemma

6.24
.

Assume
\chi\in V^{1}
and
\chi^{\prime}\in V^{k}
where
V^{1}
and
V^{k}
are the set of vertices for the join tree for the decompositions of
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{k}
respectively.
Further assume that the path from
\eta_{1}
to
\eta_{k}
in
T_{w}
consists of
\{\eta_{i},\eta_{i+1}\}
for all
i\in[k-1]
.
We have a weak join tree
T_{w}
, and two atoms
\eta_{1}
and
\eta_{k}
that both contain the variable
x
.
Therefore, each word equation
\eta_{i}
, for
i\in[k]
, contains the variable
x
.
Thus, for any any edge
\{\chi_{i},\chi_{i+1}\}\in E
, where
\chi_{i}\in V^{i}
and
\chi_{i+1}\in V^{i+1}
, we have that
\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})=\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\eta_{i+1})
.
Consequently,
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})
.

Recall that
(V^{i},E^{i})
is a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
.
Therefore, any path between any two nodes in
V^{i}
that share the variable
x
also contain the variable
x
(due to the fact that
T^{i}:=(V^{i},E^{i})
is a join tree for
\Psi_{i}
).
Furthermore,
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i})\mathrel{\cap}\mathsf{vars}(\chi_{i+1})
for any edge
\{\chi_{i},\chi_{i+1}\}\in E
, where
\chi_{i}\in V^{i}
and
\chi_{i+1}\in V^{i+1}
.
Hence, it follows that all nodes on the path between
\chi
and
\chi^{\prime}
contain the variable
x
.
Therefore,
T:=(V,E)
is a join tree.

Theorem

6.2.29
is the main result from this section, and gives us a tractable notion of acyclicity for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s. As we observe next:

Proposition 6.2.31
.

If
\Psi\in 2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is acyclic, then:

1.

Given
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, the model checking problem can be solved in time
\mathcal{O}(|\Psi|^{2}|w|^{3})
.

2.

Given
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, we can enumerate
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
with
\mathcal{O}(|\Psi|^{2}|w|^{3})
delay.

Proof 6.2.32
.

For each word equation
\chi
of
\Psi
, we can enumerate
\llbracket\chi\rrbracket(w)
in time
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{3})
, since
\chi=(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2}\cdot x_{3})
, or
\chi=(x_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{2})
for some
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}\in\Xi
. For every regular constraint
(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
of
\Psi
, we can enumerate
\llbracket(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)\rrbracket(w)
in polynomial time, since there are
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2})
factors of
w
, and for each factor, the membership problem for regular expressions can be (conservatively) solved in time
\mathcal{O}(|\gamma|\cdot|w|)

[
85
]
). Since there are
\mathcal{O}(|\Psi|)
atoms of
\Psi
, computing
\llbracket\chi\rrbracket(w)
for each atom of
\Psi
takes time
\mathcal{O}(|\Psi|\cdot|w|^{3})
. Then, we can proceed with the model checking problem and enumeration of results identically to relational
{\mathsf{CQ}}
s.

The upper bounds for model checking follow from previous work; for example, see Theorem 6.25
[
73
]
.
Polynomial delay enumeration follows from Theorem 13 of
[
9
]
, where it was proven that given an acyclic (relational) conjunctive query
\psi
and a database
D
, we can enumerate
\psi(D)
with
\mathcal{O}(|\psi||D|)
delay.
Our “database” is of size
\mathcal{O}(|\Psi|\cdot|w|^{3})
because each atom of the form
(z\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)
defines a relation of size
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{3})
.
Consequently, we get the stated time complexities.

For
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, we first find an acyclic decomposition
\Psi_{\varphi}\in 2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\varphi
in
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{7})
, and then proceed as described in
Proposition

6.2.31
.
Considering techniques from
[
9
]
, it may seem that for an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
without projection then we can enumerate
\llbracket\varphi\rrbracket(w)
with constant delay after polynomial-time preprocessing.
However this is not the case.
The decomposition of
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
introduces new variables that are not free.
Hence, the resulting
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
may not be free-connex, which is required for constant delay enumeration
[
9
]
.

Faster algorithms.

Up to this point, this chapter offers a notion of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
acyclicity for which model checking is tractable (that is, polynomial time).
However, finding faster algorithms for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s is an important question that remains open.
Here, we give two possible directions.

The first direction is taking an “engineers-style” approach, and consider algorithms that are efficient for most real-world cases, but may not improve the worst-case complexity bounds.
For example, the approach offered in
Proposition

6.2.31
leaves room for a small optimization: Assume we are dealing with a word equation
\chi
and regular constraint
(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)
for some
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\chi)
.
Instead of computing
\llbracket\chi\rrbracket(w)
and
\llbracket(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)\rrbracket(w)
separately and then joining them, we enumerate
\llbracket\chi\rrbracket(w)
and for each substitution
\sigma
we include the check as to whether
\sigma(x)\in\mathcal{L}(\gamma)
holds.

That is, instead of constructing a relation with
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{3})
elements, constructing a relation with
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{2})
elements, and then combining them, we instead construct
\llbracket\chi\land(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\gamma)\rrbracket(w)
directly.
This does not lower the worst-case time complexity – as we still need to iterate over
\mathcal{O}(|w|^{3})
factors of
w
– but we can avoid constructing unnecessary tables.
This approach would be very beneficial if the regular constraint “filters out” most factors of
w
.

The second direction is to look at criteria that allows us to lower the upper bounds.
One could look at improving the acyclicity algorithms given in this chapter.
For example,
\mathcal{O}(|\varphi|^{8})
time for decomposing acyclic
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s is sufficient for the purposes of this chapter (which looks at a notion of acyclicity that allows for polynomial-time model checking). However, it seems very likely that the exponent can be lowered.
Alternatively, one could consider sufficient criteria for
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s that allows for sub-quadratic time model checking.

6.2.2
Acyclicity for Spanners

Combining
Proposition

5.10
and
Proposition

6.2.31
gives us a class of
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s for which model checking can be solved in polynomial-time, and for which we can enumerate results with polynomial delay.
However, this assumes a fixed conversion from
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s to
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, as opposed to a more “semantic” approach.

The hardness of deciding semantic acyclicity (whether a given
\mathsf{SERCQ}
can be realized by an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s) is an open problem.
The author believes that semantic acyclicity for
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s is undecidable, partly due to the fact that various minimization and static analysis problems are undecidable for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, see
Section

5.4
.

First, let us consider sufficient criteria for an
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be realized by an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Definition 6.2.33
.

Consider
P:=\pi_{Y}\bigl(\zeta^{=}_{x_{1},y_{1}}\zeta^{=}_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cdots\zeta^{=}_{x_{k},y_{k}}\left(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\gamma_{2}\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{n}\right)\bigr)
. We say that
P
is
pseudo-acyclic
if every regex formula
\gamma_{i}
is of the form
\beta_{1}\cdot x_{i}\{\beta_{2}\}\cdot\beta_{3}
for some
x_{i}\in\Xi
, and
\beta_{1}
,
\beta_{2}
, and
\beta_{3}
are regular expressions.

We now show that
Definition

6.2.33
gives sufficient criteria for an
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be realized by an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

Proposition 6.2.34
.

Given a pseudo-acyclic
\mathsf{SERCQ}

P
, we can construct in polynomial time an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
that realizes
P
.

Proof 6.2.35
.

Let
P:=\pi_{Y}\left(\zeta^{=}_{x_{1},y_{1}}\zeta^{=}_{x_{2},y_{2}}\cdots\zeta^{=}_{x_{m},y_{m}}\left(\gamma_{1}\bowtie\gamma_{2}\cdots\bowtie\gamma_{k}\right)\right)
be a pseudo-acyclic
\mathsf{SERCQ}
.
We now define
\varphi_{P}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
such that
\varphi_{P}
is acyclic.

•

For every
x_{i}\in{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}
, we add
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{P}\cdot z_{i})
and
(z_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{C}\cdot x_{i}^{S})
to
\varphi_{P}
.

•

For every
\gamma_{i}
for
i\in[k]
, we add
(x_{i}^{P}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\beta_{1})
,
(x_{i}^{C}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\beta_{2})
and
(x_{i}^{S}\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\beta_{3})
to
\varphi_{P}
.

Since for any
\gamma_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}
for
1\leq i,j\leq k
where
i\neq j
the word equations we add to
\varphi_{P}
are disjoint, it follows that
\varphi_{P}
is (so far) acyclic.
Furthermore,
\varphi_{P}
remains acyclic after adding the regular constraints since they are unary. Next, we deal with equalities.

Let
G_{\zeta}:=(V_{\zeta},E_{\zeta})
be a graph where the set of nodes is
V_{\zeta}:=\{x_{i},y_{i}\mid i\in[m]\}
and the set of edges is
E_{\zeta}:=\{\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\mid i\in[m]\}
.
Let
F_{s}:=(V_{s},E_{s})
be a spanning forest of
G_{\zeta}
.
For every edge
\{x_{i},y_{i}\}\in E_{s}
, we add the word equation
(x_{i}^{C}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{i}^{C})
to
\varphi_{P}
via conjunction.
Finally, for every
x\in Y
, where
Y
is the set of variable in the projection
\pi_{Y}
, we add
x^{P}
and
x^{C}
to the set of free variables for
\varphi_{P}
.

Complexity.

First, we add two word equations to
\varphi_{P}
for every
x\in{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}
, and for each
i\in[k]
, we add three regular constraints to
\varphi_{P}
.
For each regex formula, this takes constant time.

Then, we create an equality graph
G_{\zeta}
, and find a spanning forest of this graph.
Finding this spanning forest takes time
\mathcal{O}(|E_{\zeta}|\cdot\mathsf{log}(|V_{\zeta}|))
using Kruskal’s algorithm (for example, see Section 23.2 of
[
15
]
).
Since, via a basic combinatorial argument, we have that
|E_{\zeta}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|V_{\zeta}|^{2}-|V_{\zeta}|\bigr)
, the total time to find this spanning tree is in

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{O}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(|V_{\zeta}|^{2}-|V_{\zeta}|\bigr)\cdot\mathsf{log}(|V_{\zeta}|)\bigr)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{O}\bigl(\frac{1}{2}\bigl(2m^{2}-2m\bigr)\cdot\mathsf{log}(2m)\bigr),

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{O}(m^{2}\cdot\mathsf{log}(m)).

Finally, for every edge in this spanning forest we add a word equation to
\varphi_{P}
.
For each edge in the spanning forest, adding such a word equation takes constant time.
Consequently, the total running time of the algorithm is in

\mathcal{O}\bigl(m^{2}\mathsf{log}(m)+k\bigr),

where
k
is the number of regex formulas, and
m
is the number of string equalities in the input
\mathsf{SERCQ}
.

Correctness.

To show that
\varphi_{P}
is acyclic, we construct a join tree.
For each tree of
F_{\zeta}
, let an arbitrary node be the root and assume all edges are directed away from the root.
That is, we have a forest consisting of rooted, directed tree where an edge from
n_{1}
to
n_{2}
denotes an equality selection
\zeta^{=}_{n_{1},n_{2}}
.

Then, for each non-leaf node
n
we create an undirected line graph
L_{n}
containing nodes
(n\mathbin{\dot{=}}n^{\prime})
for each
(n,n^{\prime})\in E_{\zeta}
, where
E_{\zeta}
is the set of edges of
F_{\zeta}
.
If
(n,n^{\prime})\in E_{\zeta}
and
n^{\prime}
is not a leaf node, then we find a node of
L_{n}
containing the variable
n^{\prime}
– since
(n,n^{\prime})\in E_{\zeta}
, such a node must exist – and add a new edge to some node in
L_{n^{\prime}}
.
This results is a new forest,
F:=(G,E)
.

Next, pick one node in each tree in
F
, and add edges between these nodes so that no cycles are introduced.
This can be done by assuming an arbitrary ordering on the constituent trees, and adding an edge from a node in one tree to a node in the next tree (with regards to the ordering).
It follows that
F
is now a join tree for
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{k}(x_{i}^{C}\mathbin{\dot{=}}y_{i}^{C})
.

For each variable
x_{i}\in{\mathsf{vars}\left(P\right)}
, we add the nodes
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{P}\cdot z_{i})
and
(z_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{C}\cdot x_{i}^{S})
to
F
, and add an edge between
(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{P}\cdot z_{i})
and
(z_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{C}\cdot x_{i}^{S})
.
Then, add a further edge between any node of some
L_{n}
that contains
x_{i}^{C}
and
(z_{i}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{i}^{C}\cdot x_{i}^{S})
.
Finally, we incorporate every regular constraint into the tree – which can easily be done.
Therefore, we have a join tree for
\varphi_{P}
, and hence
\varphi_{P}
is acyclic.

Freydenberger et al.
[
39
]
proved that fixing the number of atoms and the number of equalities in an
\mathsf{SERCQ}
allows for polynomial delay enumeration of results.
This is in comparison to
Proposition

6.2.34
which allows for an unbounded number of joins and equality selection operators.
However, in order to have this tractability result, the expressive power of each regex formula is restricted to only allow one variable.
While
Definition

6.2.33
gives sufficient criteria for an
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be represented by an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, many other such classes of
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s likely exist.

Since
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
is equivalent to the core spanners (see
Lemma

5.23
),
we can define a class of tractable core spanners as a union of acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Proposition 6.2.36
.

If
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
is a union of acyclic
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, then:

1.

model checking is in polynomial time, and

2.

we can enumerate results with polynomial-delay.

Proof 6.2.37
.

Let
\varphi:=\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
, where
\varphi_{i}\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is acyclic.

First, let us consider the model checking problem.
Since model checking for acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s is in polynomial time (recall
Proposition

6.2.31
), we simply check
w\models\varphi_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
.
If
w\models\varphi_{i}
for any
i\in[n]
, then we return true.
Otherwise, we return false.
Hence, model checking for
\varphi
is in polynomial time.

For enumeration of results, we use Proposition 2.38 in Strozecki
[
106
]
to immediately determine that we can enumerate results with polynomial delay.

As we only have sufficient syntactic criteria for a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be represented as an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s, we only scratch the surface of determining which core spanners can be represented as a union of acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.
While the author believes semantic acyclicity is likely undecidable, finding good sufficient criteria for a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be realized by an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
seems like a very promising direction for future research.

6.3
k
-ary Decompositions

We now generalize the notion of pattern decomposition so that the length of the right-hand side of each word equation in the resulting query is less than or equal to some fixed
k\geq 2
.
Note that if the right-hand side of each word equation in exactly
k
, then many patterns (such as any pattern of length less than
k
) would not be expressible.
Thus, this section focuses on word equations where the length of the right-hand side is less than or equal to
k
.
While binary decompositions may be considered the more natural case, we show that generalizing to higher arities increases the expressive power of acyclic patterns.

By
k\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
we denote the set of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s where all word equations have a right-hand side of at most length
k
.
We define
\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
formally using the following recursive definition: For all
x\in\Xi
we have that
x\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
, and if
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{i}\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
where
i\leq k
, then
(\tilde{\alpha}_{1}\cdot\tilde{\alpha}_{2}\cdots\tilde{\alpha}_{i})\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
.
We write
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}(\alpha)
for some
\alpha\in\Xi^{+}
if the underlying, unbracketed pattern of
\tilde{\alpha}
is
\alpha
.
We can convert
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
into an equivalent
k\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
analogously to the binary case, see
Definition

6.3
, with the only difference being the right-hand side of the introduced word equations can have a length of up to
k
.

Example 6.3.38
.

Consider the following
4
-ary bracketing:

\tilde{\alpha}:=\Bigl(\bigl((x_{1}\cdot x_{2}\cdot x_{3})\cdot(x_{4}\cdot x_{2}\cdot x_{4})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{5}\cdot x_{5})\bigr)\cdot(x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\Bigr).

As with the binary case, we decompose
\tilde{\alpha}
to get the following
4\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
:

\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{4}\cdot x_{2}\cdot x_{4})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot x_{2})\\
\land(z_{4}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{5}\cdot x_{5})\land(z_{5}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2}\cdot z_{3}\cdot z_{4})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{5}\cdot z_{3}).

The definition of
k
-ary concatenation tree for a decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in k\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}_{k}
follows analogously to the concatenation trees for
2
-ary decompositions, see
Definition

6.7
, with the only difference being that a node can have at most
k
children.

More formally, the concatenation tree of the decomposition
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}\in k\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is a rooted, labeled, undirected tree
\mathcal{T}:=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E},<,\Gamma,\tau,v_{r})
, where
\mathcal{V}
is the set of nodes, the relation
\mathcal{E}
is the edge relation, and
<
is used to denote the order of children of a node (from left to right). We have that
\Gamma:=\mathsf{vars}(\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}})
is the alphabet of labels and
\tau\colon\mathcal{V}\rightarrow\Gamma
is the labelling function. The semantics of a
k
-ary concatenation tree are defined by considering the natural generalization of
Definition

6.7
.
We say that
\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}
is
x
-localized
if all nodes which exist on a path between two
x
-parents (of
\mathcal{T}
) are also
x
-parents.

Proposition 6.3.39
.

There exists
\tilde{\alpha}\in\mathsf{BPat}_{3}
such that the decomposition
\Psi\in 3\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
of
\tilde{\alpha}
is acyclic, but there exists
x\in\mathsf{vars}(\Psi)
such that
\Psi
is not
x
-localized.

Proof 6.3.40
.

Consider
\tilde{\alpha}:=((x_{3}\cdot x_{3})\cdot((x_{3}\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{2})\cdot(x_{1}\cdot((x_{3}\cdot x_{3})\cdot x_{2})))
.
This bracketing is decomposed into:

\Psi_{\tilde{\alpha}}:=\mathsf{Ans}()\leftarrow(z_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{3}\cdot x_{3})\land(z_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot x_{2})\land(z_{3}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{1}\cdot z_{2})\land(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}z_{1}\cdot z_{2}\cdot z_{3}).
