\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,z_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{3},v)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(v,z_{4})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)\land(u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}])\big).

We use the shorthand
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
to denote that
u<y_{o}
or
y_{c}<u
.

Assume that
\varphi_{4,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
holds.
We know that
w[x_{o},z_{1}]=w[y_{o},z_{3}]
,
w[z_{2},x_{c}]=w[z_{4},y_{c}]
and
w^{\prime}[u,u]=w^{\prime}[v,v]
.
Therefore, we can write:

w[x_{o},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]\cdot w[z_{2},x_{c}]=w[y_{o},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v,v]\cdot w[z_{4},y_{c}].

Since the only change to the word-structure is that
w^{\prime}[u]=\mathtt{a}
, we know that all factors of the word-structure that do not contain
u
remain unchanged.
Thus,

w^{\prime}[x_{o},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{2},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v,v]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{4},y_{c}].

We are assuming that
\varphi_{4,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
holds.
Hence,
z_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u
and
u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}z_{2}
.
It follows that
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[x_{o},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{2},x_{c}]
and similarly because
v_{3}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v
and
v\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}z_{4}
we have that
w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v,v]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{4},y_{c}]
.
This all follows from
Lemma

7.2.35
.
Consequently,
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
holds.

Case 2.4.

u=x_{c}
and
x_{o}<x_{c}
.

For this case, we define

\varphi_{5,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}:=\exists v_{1}\exists v_{2}\colon\big(R_{\mathsf{eq}}(x_{o},v_{1},y_{o},v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(v_{1},u)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{c})\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(v_{2},y_{c})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{c})\land(u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}])\big).

We now show that if
\varphi_{5,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
holds, then
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
.
Since
w[x_{o},v_{1}]=w[y_{o},v_{2}]
and
w^{\prime}[u,u]=w^{\prime}[y_{c},y_{c}]
, we know that:

w[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]=w[y_{o},v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{c},y_{c}].

Also since the only difference between the word before the update and after the update is that
w^{\prime}[u]=\mathtt{a}
, we can write:

w^{\prime}[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{c},y_{c}].

Moreover, we know that
v_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u
and that
v_{2}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}y_{c}
, therefore, because of
Lemma

7.2.35
, we can write that
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u,u]
and that
w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{c},y_{c}]
. Hence, we have shown that if
\varphi_{5,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
holds, then
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

Case 3.

u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
u\in[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

This case is split into four cases analogous to cases 2.1 to 2.4
Due to the fact that the cases are symmetrical, we have skipped the proofs for these cases.
It should be clear from cases 2.1 to 2.4 that we can write the formulas
\varphi_{i,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
for
i\in\{6,7,8,9\}
to handle each of these cases.

Case 4.

u\in[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
u\in[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

Since
x_{o}<y_{o}
, we have that
u
appears “later on” in
w[x_{o},x_{c}]
compared to
w[y_{o},y_{c}]
. More formally, we have that
\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(y_{o})<\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(x_{o})
.
Therefore, we do not need to consider the case where
u=y_{c}
.
Thus, we can split this case into the following three subcases.

•

Case 4.1.
x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
,

•

Case 4.2.
u=x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
, and

•

Case 4.3.
u=x_{c}
and
u=y_{o}
.

Next, we discuss how each of these subcases can be handled.

Case 4.1.

x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.
We know that

\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(y_{o})<\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(x_{o}).

Thus, we consider the following equality:

\displaystyle w[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{2}]\cdot w[v_{3},v_{4}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[v_{5},x_{c}]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle w[y_{o},v_{6}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[v_{7},v_{8}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{9}]\cdot w[v_{10},y_{c}],

where
w[x_{o},v_{1}],w[v_{5},x_{c}]\in\Sigma^{+}
, since
x_{o}
and
x_{c}
are symbol elements, and where
w[v_{3},v_{4}]\in\Sigma^{*}
.
Similar to previous cases, we can check, using
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
and
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}
, in
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}
whether:

•

w[x_{o},v_{1}]=w[y_{o},v_{6}]
,

•

w[v_{5},x_{c}]=w[v_{10},y_{c}]
,

•

w[v_{3},v_{4}]=w[v_{7},v_{8}]
,

•

w^{\prime}[v_{2}]=w^{\prime}[u]
, and

•

w^{\prime}[u]=w^{\prime}[v_{9}]
.

Since the update ensures that
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)
holds; and per the definition of the relation that we are maintaining, we have that
w^{\prime}[u]\neq\varepsilon
,
w[x_{o},v_{1}]\neq\varepsilon
, and
w[v_{5},x_{c}]\neq\varepsilon
.

Since
u
is the only position that is changed, it follows that the previous equality implies the following:

\displaystyle w^{\prime}[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{3},v_{4}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{5},x_{c}]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle w^{\prime}[y_{o},v_{6}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{7},v_{8}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{9}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{10},y_{c}].

We now show that, if these cases hold, then
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
.
Using a shorthand notation, we first consider the case where
w^{\prime}[v_{3},v_{4}]\neq\varepsilon
by defining:

\varphi_{10,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}:=(w[x_{o},v_{1}]=w[y_{o},v_{6}])\land(w[v_{5},x_{c}]=w[v_{10},y_{c}])\land(w^{\prime}[v_{2}]=w^{\prime}[u])\\
\land(w^{\prime}[u]=w^{\prime}[v_{9}])\land(w[v_{3},v_{4}]=w[v_{7},v_{8}])\land(v_{6}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{7})\land\\
(v_{8}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{9}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{10})\land(v_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{2}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{3})\land(v_{4}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{5}).

Notice that these shorthands can be easily expressed in
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
using the relations
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
and
R_{\mathsf{Next}}
that we maintain along with the
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}
relations for each
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
.

We now handle the case where
w^{\prime}[v_{3},v_{4}]=w^{\prime}[v_{7},v_{8}]=\varepsilon
.
Consider

\displaystyle w^{\prime}[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{5},x_{c}]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle w^{\prime}[y_{o},v_{6}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{9}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{10},y_{c}].

This is easily dealt with an extra formula that does not introduce the variables
v_{3},v_{4},v_{7},v_{8}
, and add the constraint that
v_{2}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u
and
u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{9}
.
Consider the following subformula, denoted in shorthand:

\varphi_{11,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}:=(w[x_{o},v_{1}]=w[y_{o},v_{6}])\land(w[v_{5},x_{c}]=w[v_{10},y_{c}])\land(w^{\prime}[u]=w^{\prime}[v_{2}])\\
\land(w^{\prime}[u]=w^{\prime}[v_{9}])\land(v_{6}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{9}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{10})\land(v_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{2}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{5}).

Therefore, this case is handled by two formulas
\varphi_{10,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
and
\varphi_{11,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
, which we do not give explicitly, but instead give a shorthand notation.
We assume that the free variables for
\varphi_{10,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
and
\varphi_{11,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
are
x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}
.
All other variables are existentially quantified.

Case 4.2.

u=x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.

This case proceeds analogously to Case 4.1. Consider the following equality:

\displaystyle w[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{2}]\cdot w[v_{3},v_{4}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle w[y_{o},v_{5}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[v_{6},v_{7}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{c}].

Note that
u=x_{c}
, therefore
w^{\prime}[x_{o},u]=w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]
.
There is also the case where
w[v_{3},v_{4}]=w[v_{6},v_{7}]=\varepsilon
.
Observing previous cases, it is straightforward to construct such formulas
\varphi_{12,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
and
\varphi_{13,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
that handle this case.

Case 4.3.

u=x_{c}
and
u=y_{o}
.

This case again proceeds analogously to Case 4.1. Consider the following equality:

\displaystyle w[x_{o}]\cdot w[v_{1},v_{2}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[v_{3},v_{4}]\cdot w[y_{c}].

We also need to handle the case where
w[v_{1},v_{2}]=w[v_{3},v_{4}]=\varepsilon
, as previously described.
Therefore, the present case is handled by
\varphi_{14,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
and
\varphi_{15,\mathtt{a}}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
, which we do not give explicitly.

This concludes Part 1 of this proof.
Since we can exhaustively considered each case, and produced a
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
formula that handles the case, it follows that if
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]\neq w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
, then
(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\notin R_{\mathsf{eq}}^{\prime}
.

Part 2 (reset):

For this part, we define the following update formula:

\phi^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}_{{\mathsf{reset}}}(u;x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}):=\bigvee\limits_{i=1}^{5}\big(\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},i}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}\big)\land(x_{o}<y_{o})\land\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(x_{o})\land\\
\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(x_{c})\land\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(y_{o})\land\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(y_{c}).

Again,
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},i}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
for
i\in[5]
is to be defined later.

First note that if we perform the update
\mathsf{reset}(u)
where
u\in\{x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}\}
, then clearly
(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\notin R_{\mathsf{eq}}^{\prime}
.
This is per the definition of
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
, which assumes that each
x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}
is a symbol element.

We consider four cases:

•

Case 1.
u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

•

Case 2.
x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

•

Case 3.
u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.

•

Case 4.
x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.

For cases 1, 2, and 3, we give a
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
to handle that case.
Case 4 is slightly more tricky, and therefore, we given two
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
s.

Case 1.

u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

We define

\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},1}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}:=R_{\mathsf{eq}}(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\land\bigl((u<x_{o})\lor(u>x_{c})\bigr)\land\bigl((u<y_{o})\lor(u>y_{c})\bigr).

This subformula states that
R_{\mathsf{eq}}^{\prime}(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\in R_{\mathsf{eq}}
where
u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
if and only if
R_{\mathsf{eq}}(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})
.

Case 2.

x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

For this case, let

\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},2}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}:=\exists z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}\colon\big(R_{\mathsf{eq}}(x_{o},z_{1},y_{o},z_{3})\land R_{\mathsf{eq}}(z_{2},x_{c},z_{4},y_{c})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(z_{1},u)\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}(u,z_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(z_{3},z_{4})\land(u\notin[y_{o},y_{c}])\big).

We can see that, if
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},2}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
holds, then
z_{1}\leadsto_{w}u\leadsto_{w}z_{2}
.
However, since
\partial=\mathsf{reset}(u)
, it follows that
z_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}z_{2}
.
Therefore,
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[x_{o},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{2},x_{c}]
and
w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[z_{4},y_{c}]
.
Hence,
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
.

Case 3.

u\notin[x_{o},x_{c}]
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.

This case is analogous to Case 2.
Thus, we define
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},3}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
analogously to
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},2}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
.

Case 4.

x_{o}<u<x_{c}
and
y_{o}<u<y_{c}
.

For this case, observe the following equality:

w[x_{o},v_{1}]

\varepsilon

w[v_{2},v_{3}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[v_{4},x_{c}]

=

w[y_{o},v_{5}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[v_{6},v_{7}]

\varepsilon

w[v_{8},y_{c}]
,

where
v_{i}\in\mathrm{D}
for
i\in[8]
.

In order to handle
w[v_{1}+1,v_{2}-1]=\varepsilon
and
w[v_{7}+1,v_{8}-1]=\varepsilon
, we simply check
v_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{2}
and
v_{7}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}v_{8}
.
Assuming that

w[x_{o},v_{1}]\cdot\varepsilon\cdot w[v_{2},v_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{4},x_{c}]=w[x_{o},x_{c}]

and

w[y_{o},v_{5}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{6},v_{7}]\cdot\varepsilon\cdot w^{\prime}[v_{8},y_{c}]=w[y_{o},y_{c}],

we have that
w^{\prime}[x_{o},x_{c}]=w^{\prime}[y_{o},y_{c}]
.
We can handle this case using a formula
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},4}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
in
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
.
Notice that
w[x_{o},v_{1}]=\varepsilon
cannot hold, since we assume that
x_{o}
is a symbol element – likewise,
w^{\prime}[v_{4},x_{c}]=\varepsilon
cannot hold.

The case where
w[v_{2},v_{3}]=w[v_{6},v_{7}]=\varepsilon
can be easily dealt with analogously.
Therefore, this case is handled by
\varphi_{\mathsf{reset},5}^{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}
.

Concluding the proof.

We have proven that we can maintain the factor equality relation under both insertion and deletion of a single symbol.
For each of these parts, we have considered each case and given an update formula (or described how one could be constructed).
While we have only considered the case where
x_{o}<y_{o}
, the other cases follow trivially.
Furthermore, we given update formulas in existential positive
\mathsf{FO}
.
However, since existential positive
\mathsf{FO}
is equivalent to
{\mathsf{UCQ}}
s, and
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}={\mathsf{DynUCQ}}
, we have shown that
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.
Therefore, we conclude the proof.

7.3
Relations in FC[REG] and DynCQ

In this section, we examine the comparative expressive power of
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
and
DynCQ
.
Consider the equal length relation
R_{\mathsf{len}}:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid|w_{1}|=|w_{2}|\}
.
According to Fagin et al.
[
31
]
, this relation is not selectable by core spanners; and therefore, is not expressible in
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
.
Moreover, according to Freydenberger and Peterfreund
[
41
]
, even generalized core spanners (and hence,
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
) cannot express this relation.
We define the equal length relation in the dynamic setting as the following:

\bar{R}_{\mathsf{len}}:=\bigl\{(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{1})\in\mathrm{D}^{4}\mid|w[u_{1},u_{2}]|=|w[v_{1},v_{2}]|\\
\text{ and }w[i]\neq\varepsilon\text{ for }i\in\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\}\bigr\}.

Lemma 7.3.38
.

The equal length relation
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{len}}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.39
.

To maintain
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{len}}
, we take the update formulas from the proof of
Lemma

7.2.18
, used to maintain the factor equality relation, and replace any atoms of the form
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
with the formula
\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
.
We also use
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{len}}
in any update formula, rather than
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
.
For example, take the update formula for insertion given in Case 2.2 of the proof for
Lemma

7.2.18
.
Now consider the alternative update formula to maintain equal length:

\exists v_{1}\exists v_{2}\colon\big(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{len}}(v_{1},x_{c},v_{2},y_{c})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(x_{o},v_{1})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(y_{o},v_{2})\land\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{o}))\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}x_{o})\big).

The only exception to replacing
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
with the formula
\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma^{*}}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
is when such
\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
is a subformula of
\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)
already. An example of such a case is used to ensure that
x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}
are all symbol elements.

While this allows us to separate the languages that are definable by core spanners from the ones that can be maintained in
DynCQ
, let us now consider more wide-ranging examples:
Proposition 6.7 in
[
37
]
establishes that none of the following relations are selectable by core spanners (and hence, cannot be defined by an
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
-formula), yet we show them to be in
DynCQ
:

Proposition 7.3.40
.

The following relations can be maintained in
DynCQ
, but not definable in
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
:

\displaystyle R_{<}

\displaystyle:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid|w_{1}|<|w_{2}|\},

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{rev}}

\displaystyle:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid w_{2}=w_{1}^{R}\},\text{ where $w_{1}^{R}$ is the reversal of $w_{1}$,}

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}

\displaystyle:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid|w_{1}|_{\mathtt{a}}=|w_{2}|_{\mathtt{a}}\}\text{ for }\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma,

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{perm}}

\displaystyle:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid|w_{1}|_{\mathtt{a}}=|w_{2}|_{\mathtt{a}}\text{ for all }\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma\},

\displaystyle R_{\mathsf{scatt}}

\displaystyle:=\{(w_{1},w_{2})\mid w_{1}\text{ is a scattered subword of }w_{2}\},

where
w_{1}
is a scattered subword of
w_{2}
if, for some
n\geq 1
, there exist
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}
and
\bar{s}_{0},\ldots,\bar{s}_{n}\in\Sigma^{*}
such that
w_{1}=s_{1}\cdots s_{n}
and
w_{2}=\bar{s}_{0}s_{1}\bar{s}_{1}\cdots s_{n}\bar{s}_{n}
.

Due to the length of the proof of
Proposition

7.3.40
, and the fact that its consequences are of interest rather than the proof itself, we first discuss extending
\mathsf{FC}
(or
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}
) with
{\mathsf{DynFO}}
(or
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}
) constraints as a useful tool before giving the proof of
Proposition

7.3.40
in
Section

7.3.1
.

One can show that relations like the factor relation, or equality modulo a bounded Levenshtein-distance are all
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
-selectable (see Section 5.1 of
[
37
]
. While
[
37
]
considers
{\mathsf{SpLog}}
instead of
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
, they have equivalent expressive power. See
Theorem

4.29
and
Theorem

4.26
).
By
Theorem

7.2.25
, we can directly use these relations in constructions for
DynCQ
-definable languages and
DynCQ
-selectable relations.
For example, Proposition 5.2 in Fagin et al.
[
31
]
shows factor inequality can be expressed by core spanners.
This leads us to the following example:

Example 7.3.41
.

Let
R_{\neq}:=\{w_{1},w_{2}\mid w_{1}\neq w_{2}\}
. It can be easily shown that
R_{\neq}
is definable in
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
, as demonstrated with the following:

\varphi(w_{1},w_{2}):=\exists p,s_{1},s_{2}\colon\bigl(\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\;\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}}\bigl((w_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}p\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot s_{1})\land(w_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}p\cdot\mathtt{b}\cdot s_{2})\bigr)\bigr)\\
\lor\exists y\colon\bigl((w_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}w_{2}\cdot y)\land(y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\Sigma^{+})\bigr)\lor\exists y\colon\bigl((w_{2}\mathbin{\dot{=}}w_{1}\cdot y)\land(y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}\Sigma^{+})\bigr)

If
\varphi
holds, then either:

•

w_{1}
and
w_{2}
have a different symbol at position
|p|+1
, where
p\in\Sigma^{*}
is the (possible empty) common-prefix,

•

w_{1}
is a proper prefix of
w_{2}
, or

•

w_{2}
is a proper prefix of
w_{1}
.

Hence, it follows that
w_{1}\neq w_{2}
.

Although one could show directly that the factor inequality relation is
DynCQ
-selectable, using
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
and
Theorem

7.2.25
can avoid hand-waving.

This can be generalized beyond
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
.
We can extend
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
with relation symbols for any
DynCQ
-maintainable relation, and use the resulting logic for
DynCQ
over words.
Of course, all this applies to
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
and
DynFO
– one can define
\mathsf{FC}[{\mathsf{DynFO}}]
as the set of
\mathsf{FC}
-formulas extended with constraints that are
{\mathsf{DynFO}}
-selectable.

Example 7.3.42
.

For some input text
w\in\Sigma^{*}
, let

R_{\mathsf{len}}:=\{w_{1},w_{2}\sqsubseteq w\mid|w_{1}|=|w_{2}|\}.

We know that
R_{\mathsf{len}}
is selectable in
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}
, see
Lemma

7.3.38
. Now consider the following
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}[{\mathsf{DynCQ}}]
formula:

\varphi:=\exists x,y\colon(\mathfrak{u}\mathbin{\dot{=}}x\cdot y)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{\in}}(\mathtt{a})^{+})\land(y\mathbin{\dot{\in}}(\mathtt{b})^{+})\land R_{\mathsf{len}}(x,y).

Therefore, we can maintain the language
\{\mathtt{a}^{n}\;\mathtt{b}^{n}\mid n\geq 1\}
in
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}
.
This language is not expressible by generalized core spanners
[
41
]
.

While one could write an update formula to prove that the language

\{w\in\Sigma^{*}\mid w=\mathtt{a}^{n}\;\mathtt{b}^{n}\text{ and }n\geq 1\}

can be maintained in
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}
,
Example

7.3.42
illustrates a proof using
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}[{\mathsf{DynCQ}}]
.

In this section, we have given numerous examples of relations that can be maintained in
DynCQ
, but are not definable by an
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
formula.
Furthermore, we have shown the efficacy of using
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}[{\mathsf{DynCQ}}]
(or
\mathsf{FC}[{\mathsf{DynFO}}]
respectively) as a tool to prove the maintainability of relations in
DynCQ
(or
DynFO
).

7.3.1
Proof of
Proposition

7.3.40

This section is dedicated for the proof of
Proposition

7.3.40
.
The proof of maintaining many of the relations given in
Proposition

7.3.40
mirrors the proof of
Lemma

7.2.18
.
Therefore, in certain circumstances, we reference the case distinctions in the proof of
Lemma

7.2.18
instead of exhaustively considering every case.

The proof of
Proposition

7.3.40
is given as the following lemmas.
That is, the following relations are maintainable in
DynCQ

•

Lemma

7.3.43
.
R_{<}
.

•

Lemma

7.3.45
.
R_{\mathsf{rev}}
.

•

Lemma

7.3.47
.
R_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
.

•

Lemma

7.3.49
.
R_{\mathsf{perm}}
.

•

Lemma

7.3.51
.
R_{\mathsf{scatt}}
.

Lemma 7.3.43
.

R_{<}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.44
.

We maintain this relation with the following update formula:

\phi^{\bar{R}_{<}}_{\partial}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}):=\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\colon\big(R_{\mathsf{len}}^{\prime}(u_{1},u_{2},x_{1},x_{2})\land\\
(x_{1}<v_{1})\land(v_{1}\leq v_{2})\land(v_{2}<x_{2})\land\bigwedge_{z\in\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\}}(\bigvee_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(z))\big).

Thus, almost immediately from
Lemma

7.3.38
, we can maintain
\bar{R}_{<}
in
DynCQ
.

Lemma 7.3.45
.

R_{\mathsf{rev}}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.46
.

We can maintain this with a simple variation of the update formula which maintains
R_{\mathsf{eq}}
.
Whenever
R_{\mathsf{eq}}(\cdot)
is used as a subformula, one would need to use
R_{\mathsf{rev}}(\cdot)
instead.
The slightly more involved aspect of altering the update formulas would be to reverse the ordering of certain indices.
This can be done by checking
y\leadsto_{w}x
instead of
x\leadsto_{w}y
where necessary.

Next, we consider maintaining
R_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
.
To maintain this relation, we consider the proof for maintaining the factor equality relation (see
Lemma

7.2.18
).
Like with maintaining the equal factor relation, we maintain
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
,
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
, and
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
which are relations that point to the first position that carries an
\mathtt{a}
, the last position that carries an
\mathtt{a}
, and point from one position that carries an
\mathtt{a}
to the next.
Then, using these relations, we can augment the proof of
Lemma

7.2.18
to maintain
R_{\mathsf{num}}(\mathtt{a})
.

Lemma 7.3.47
.

R_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.48
.

To maintain
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
, we first maintain the following relations:

•

\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}:=\{i\in\mathrm{D}\mid w[i]=\mathtt{a}\text{ and for all }j<i,w[j]\neq\mathtt{a}\}
,

•

\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}:=\{i\in\mathrm{D}\mid w[i]=\mathtt{a}\text{ and for all }j>i,w[j]\neq\mathtt{a}\}
,

•

\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}:=\{(i,j)\in\mathrm{D}^{2}\mid w[i]=w[j]=\mathtt{a}\text{ where }i<j\text{ and }w[k]\neq\mathtt{a}\text{ for each }k\in\{i+1,\dots,j-1\}\}
.

Let
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
be initialized to
\{\$\}
.
To maintain
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
under
\mathsf{ins}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)
, we use

\phi^{\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land(u>x)\bigr)\lor\bigl((u\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\land\exists v\colon(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)\land(u<v))\bigr).

To maintain
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
under
\mathsf{ins}_{\mathtt{b}}(u)
, for all
\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}
, we use

\phi^{\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land((u<x)\lor(u>x))\bigr)\lor\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(u,x)\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\$)\bigr).

Now consider maintaining
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
under the reset operation.

\phi^{\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{reset}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land((u>x)\lor(u<x))\bigr)\lor\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(u,x)\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}\$)\bigr).

Maintaining
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
is analogous to maintaining
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
.
Let
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
be initialized to
\{1\}
.
First, consider the insertion of an
\mathtt{a}
at position
u\in\mathrm{D}
.

\phi^{\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land(u<x)\bigr)\lor\bigl((u\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\land\exists v\colon(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)\land(u>v))\bigr).

To maintain
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
under the
\mathsf{ins}_{\mathtt{b}}(u)
operation, for all
\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}
, we use the following:

\phi^{\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land((u>x)\lor(u<x))\bigr)\lor\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,u)\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}1)\bigr).

To maintain
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
under the reset operation, we define

\phi^{\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{reset}}}(u;x):=\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land((u>x)\lor(u<x))\bigr)\lor\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,u)\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(u)\land(x\mathbin{\dot{=}}1)\bigr).

Next, we look at maintaining
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
.
We initialize
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
to
\emptyset
.
To maintain
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
under the operation
{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}
, we define

\phi^{\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}(u;x,y):=\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,y)\land((u<x)\lor(u<y))\bigr)\lor\\
\exists v\colon\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,v)\land(x<u<v)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}y)\bigr)\lor\\
\exists v\colon\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,v)\land(x<u<v)\bigr)\lor\bigl(\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(x)\land(x<y)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}y)\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(y)\land(x<y)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}x)\bigr).

Now, to maintain
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
under
{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}
, where
\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}
, let

\phi^{\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}}(u;x,y):=\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,y)\land(((u<x)\lor(u>y))\lor((x<u)\land(u<y)))\bigr)\lor\\
\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x,u)\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(u,y)\bigr).

In regards to maintaining
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
, inserting the symbol
\mathtt{b}
, for some
\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}
, and performing a reset operation at position
u
is essentially the same operation. Therefore, in order to deal with the reset operation, we let
\phi^{\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}}(u;x,y)=\phi^{\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{{\mathsf{reset}}}(u;x,y)
.

Now we consider maintaining
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
under the insertion of
\mathtt{a}
at position
u
.
Before maintaining
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
under
{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}
, we consider a restricted version of the relation which we denote with
D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
.
If
(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\in D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
, then
w[x_{o},x_{c}]_{\mathtt{a}}=w[y_{o},y_{c}]_{\mathtt{a}}
, and
w[i]=\mathtt{a}
for each
i\in\{x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c}\}
.
Since we have the relations
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}
,
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}
, and
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}
, we can utilize the proof of
Lemma

7.2.18
with minor changes.
Consider
\phi^{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}_{R_{\mathsf{eq}}}(u;x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})
as defined in
Lemma

7.2.18
.

Maintaining
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
.

To define
\phi^{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}_{D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}}(u;x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})
, we replace each occurrence of
R_{\mathsf{eq}}(\cdot)
,
R_{\mathsf{first}}(\cdot)
,
R_{\mathsf{last}}(\cdot)
, and
R_{\mathsf{Next}}(\cdot)
with
D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}(\cdot)
,
\mathsf{first}_{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
,
\mathsf{last}_{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
, and
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(\cdot)
respectively.

To maintain
D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
under the
{\mathsf{reset}}
operation, we consider the same procedure of replacing atoms as we did for
\phi^{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}_{D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}}(u;x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})
. Note that the update formula for
D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
under the
{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{b}}}}
, where
\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma\setminus\{\mathtt{a}\}
is the same as the update formula for
{\mathsf{reset}}
.

To generalize from maintaining
D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
to
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
, we use

\phi^{\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}}_{\partial}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}):=\exists\bar{x}_{o},\bar{x}_{c},\bar{y}_{o},\bar{y}_{c}\colon\bigl(\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(\bar{x}_{o},x_{o})\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(x_{c},\bar{x}_{c})\land\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(\bar{y}_{o},y_{o})\land\\
\mathsf{next}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{c},\bar{y}_{c})\land(\bar{x}_{o}<u_{1}\leq x_{o})\land(x_{c}<u_{2}\leq\bar{x}_{c})\land(\bar{y}_{o}<v_{1}\leq y_{o})\land\\
(y_{c}\leq v_{2}<\bar{y}_{c})\land\phi^{D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}}_{\partial}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\bigr)

For intuition, we are stating that
(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\in\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}^{\prime}
if
(x_{o},x_{c},y_{o},y_{c})\in D_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
, and there does not exist an element that holds the symbol
\mathtt{a}
between
u_{1}
and
x_{o}
,
x_{c}
and
u_{2}
,
v_{1}
and
y_{o}
, or
y_{c}
and
v_{2}
.

Since
R_{\mathsf{num}}(\mathtt{a})
for any
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
is
DynCQ
-seletable, we can immediately determine that
R_{\mathsf{perm}}
is
DynCQ
-selectable:

Lemma 7.3.49
.

R_{\mathsf{perm}}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.50
.

We maintain this relation with

\phi^{\bar{R}_{\mathsf{perm}}}_{\partial}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}):=\bigwedge\limits_{\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma}\phi^{\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}}_{\partial}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}),

for each
\partial\in\Delta
.
From
Lemma

7.3.47
,
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{num}(\mathtt{a})}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Lemma 7.3.51
.

R_{\mathsf{scatt}}
can be maintained in
DynCQ
.

Proof 7.3.52
.

Let
u\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}v
denote that
u\in\Sigma^{*}
is a scattered subword of
v\in\Sigma^{*}
.
To prove that we can maintain the scattered subword relation in
DynCQ
, for both insertion and reset operations, we look at a case distinction, and give a formula to handle each case.
The update formula is the disjunction of all the given formulas.
Formally, the relation we maintain is:

\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}:=\{(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\in\mathrm{D}^{4}\mid w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]\text{ and }\\
w^{\prime}[z]\neq\varepsilon\text{ for each }z\in\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\}\}.

Part 1 (insertion).

Consider the insertion of the symbol
\mathtt{a}\in\Sigma
at position
u\in\mathrm{D}
.
First, we handle the “base case” with the following formula.

\varphi_{1}:=\exists z\colon\bigl(\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(u_{1})\land(u_{1}\mathbin{\dot{=}}u_{2})\land(v_{1}\leq z)\land(z\leq v_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(z)\bigr).

For
\varphi_{1}
to hold, we have that
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]=\mathtt{a}
, and
w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]=\bar{s}_{0}\cdot\mathtt{a}\cdot\bar{s}_{1}
for some
\bar{s}_{0},\bar{s}_{1}\in\Sigma^{*}
.
We can therefore see that this is the correct behaviour for this case.

For the inductive step, we have four cases.
The cases we explore are as follows:

1.

u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
,

2.

u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
v\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
,

3.

u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
, and

4.

u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
v\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
.

Cases 2, 3, and 4 are split into further sub-cases.

Case 1.

u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
:

If
w[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},v_{2}]
and
u
is outside of the interval
[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
[v_{1},v_{2}]
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
.
Therefore,
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
should hold.
This is handled by

\varphi_{2}:=\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})\land(u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}])\land(u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]).

We use the shorthand
u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
for
(u<u_{1})\lor(u_{2}<u)
.

If
w[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},v_{2}]
does not hold, and
u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
also does not hold.

We now move on to the next case.

Case 2.

u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
:

We split this case into three subcases:

•

Case 2.1.
u_{1}=u
.

•

Case 2.2.
u_{1}<u<u_{2}
.

•

Case 2.3.
u_{2}=u
.

Notice that
\varphi_{1}
handles the case where
u=u_{1}=u_{2}
and therefore we can safely assume that
u_{1}<u_{2}
.

Case 2.1.

u_{1}=u
:

If
w[x_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{2},v_{2}]
, where
u_{1}=u
,
u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}x_{1}
, and there is some
v
such that
v_{1}\leq v<x_{2}
and
w^{\prime}(u)=w^{\prime}(v)
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
.
That is, as long as
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
.
We handle this case with the following formula:

\varphi_{3}:=\exists x_{1},x_{2},v\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(x_{1},u_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\land(v_{1}\leq v<x_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,x_{1})\land\\
(u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}])\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}u_{1})\bigr).

Case 2.2.

u_{1}<u<u_{2}
:

For this case, we have that
w[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},v_{2}]
if

•

w[u_{1},\bar{x}_{1}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},x_{1}]
,

•

w[\bar{x}_{2},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{2},v_{2}]
for some
\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2}\in\mathrm{D}
,

•

\bar{x}_{1}\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}u\leadsto_{w^{\prime}}\bar{x}_{2}
, and

•

w^{\prime}[u]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{1}+1,x_{2}-1]
. That is, there exists some
v
where
x_{1}<v<x_{2}
and
w^{\prime}[v]=w^{\prime}[u]
.

This can be thought of as being similar to the case illustrated in
Figure

7.1
(generalized to scattered subwords, rather than equal factors).
We handle this case with

\varphi_{4}:=\exists x_{1},x_{2},\bar{x}_{1},\bar{x}_{2},y\colon\bigl((u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}])\land(u_{1}<u<u_{2})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},\bar{x}_{1},v_{1},x_{1})\land\\
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(\bar{x}_{2},u_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(\bar{x}_{1},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,\bar{x}_{2})\land(x_{1}<y<x_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y)\bigr).

Case 2.3.

u_{2}=u
:

This case has analogous reasoning to Case 2.1. We handle this case with

\varphi_{5}:=\exists x_{1},x_{2},v\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},x_{1},v_{1},x_{2})\land(x_{2}<v\leq v_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(x_{1},u)\land\\
(u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}])\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}u_{2})\bigr).

This concludes Case 2.

Case 3.

u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
:

Similarly to Case 2, this case is split into three further subcases:

•

Case 3.1.
v_{1}=u
,

•

Case 3.2.
v_{1}<u<v_{2}
, and

•

Case 3.3.
v_{2}=u
.

The case where
u=v_{1}=v_{2}
is dealt with by
\varphi_{1}
.
Consider
\varphi_{1}
for the case where
v_{1}=z=v_{2}=u
.
Thus, we do not consider the case where
v_{1}=v_{2}=u
.

Case 3.1.

v_{1}=u
:

We handle this case with the following formula:

\varphi_{6}:=\exists x,y,z\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(x,u_{2},y,v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u_{1},x)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{1})\land(v_{1}\leq z<y)\land\\
\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(z)\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(u_{1}))\land(u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}])\bigr).

Case 3.2.

v_{1}<u<v_{2}
:

We realize this case with

\varphi_{8}:=\exists x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{6},y\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},x_{1},v_{1},x_{4})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(x_{3},u_{2},x_{5},v_{2})\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}(x_{1},x_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(x_{2},x_{3})\land\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}\bigl(\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(x_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(y)\bigr)\land\\
(x_{4}<y<x_{5})\land(x_{4}<u<x_{5})\land(u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}])\bigr).

Since this case is slightly more involved than previous cases, we give more explanation regarding
\varphi_{8}
.
If
\varphi_{8}
holds, then the following hold:

•

w[u_{1},x_{1}]
is a scattered subword of
w[v_{1},x_{4}]
,

•

w[x_{3},u_{2}]
is a scattered subword of
w[x_{5},v_{2}]
,

•

w^{\prime}[x_{2}]
is a scattered subword of
w^{\prime}[x_{4}+1,x_{5}-1]
,

•

x_{1}\leadsto_{w}x_{2}\leadsto_{w}x_{3}
, and

•

u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
.

Since
x_{1}\leadsto_{w}x_{2}\leadsto_{w}x_{3}
, it follows that
w[u_{1},u_{2}]=w[u_{1},x_{1}]\cdot w[x_{2}]\cdot w[x_{3},u_{2}]
.
Furthermore,
w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]=w^{\prime}[v_{1},x_{4}]\cdot w^{\prime}[x_{4}+1,x_{5}-1]\cdot w^{\prime}[x_{5},v_{2}]
.
Therefore,
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
.

Case 3.3.

v_{2}=u
:

This is analogous to Case 3.1.
Consider

\varphi_{6}:=\exists x,y,z\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},x,v_{1},y)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(x,u_{2})\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{2})\land(y<z\leq v_{2})\land\\
\bigvee_{\mathtt{b}\in\Sigma}(\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(y)\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}(u_{2}))\land(u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}])\bigr).

Case 4.

u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
:

This is the most involved case, due to the fact that the intervals
[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
[v_{1},v_{2}]
“overlap”.
However, due to the following, we can simplify our case distinction.

First consider following straightforward case:
If
v_{1}\leq u_{1}
and
u_{2}\leq v_{2}
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
, since
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
. This is realized with

\varphi_{11}:=(v_{1}\leq u_{1})\land(u_{2}\leq v_{2}).

Furthermore, if
u_{1}<v_{1}
and
v_{2}<u_{2}
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]
cannot be a scattered subword of
w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
, because then
w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]\sqsubset w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]
; recall that each of
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}
are symbol elements.

Since we are in the case where
u\in[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
u\in[v_{1},v_{2}]
, this limits us to examine the following sub-cases:

•

Case 4.1.
v_{1}<u_{1}
and
v_{2}<u_{2}
, and

•

Case 4.2.
u_{1}<v_{1}
and
u_{2}<v_{2}
.

We now give a proof for each of these cases.

Case 4.1

v_{1}<u_{1}
and
v_{2}<u_{2}
:

Notice that if
v_{1}<u_{1}
and
v_{2}<u_{2}
where
u_{1}<u<u_{2}
and
v_{1}<u<v_{2}
, then
\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u_{1})<\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(u)-\mathsf{pos}_{w^{\prime}}(v_{1})
. This leads to the following:

\displaystyle w[u_{1},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[z_{2},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{2}]\cdot w[z_{4},u_{2}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}\;

\displaystyle w[v_{1},x_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{1}]\cdot w[x_{2},x_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[x_{4},v_{2}],

where the following hold:

•

w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]=w[u_{1},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[z_{2},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{2}]\cdot w[z_{4},u_{2}]
,

•

w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]=w[v_{1},x_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{1}]\cdot w[x_{2},x_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[x_{4},v_{2}]
,

•

w[u_{1},z_{1}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},x_{1}]
,

•

w[z_{2},z_{3}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{2},x_{3}]
,

•

w[z_{4},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{4},v_{2}]
,

•

w^{\prime}[y_{1}]=w^{\prime}[u]
, and

•

w^{\prime}[y_{2}]=w^{\prime}[u]
.

Then, it follows that
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
. This can be realized by the following formula:

\varphi_{11}:=\exists z_{1},x_{1},\dots,z_{4},x_{4},y_{1},y_{2}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},z_{2},v_{1},x_{1})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{2},z_{3},x_{2},x_{3})\land\\
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{4},u_{2},x_{4},v_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{1})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{1},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,z_{2})\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{3},y_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(y_{2},z_{4})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(x_{1},y_{1})\\
\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(y_{1},x_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(x_{3},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,x_{4})\bigr)

Note that in
\varphi_{11}
, we have that
w[z_{2},z_{3}]\in\Sigma^{+}
.
Hence, we now handle the case where
w[z_{2},z_{3}]=\varepsilon
.
For this, consider the following:

w[u_{1},z_{1}]

w^{\prime}[u]

\varepsilon

w^{\prime}[z_{2}]

w[z_{3},u_{2}]
,

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},x_{1}]

w^{\prime}[x_{2}]

w[x_{3},x_{4}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[x_{5},v_{2}]
.

Which is realized by
\varphi_{12}
:

\varphi_{12}:=\exists z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},x_{1},x_{2},x_{5}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},z_{1},v_{1},x_{1})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{3},u_{2},x_{5},v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{1},u)\\
\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,z_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{2},z_{3})\land(x_{1}<x_{2}<u<x_{5})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(x_{2})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(v_{2})\bigr).

Notice that we do not need to introduce the variables
x_{3}
and
v_{4}
in
\varphi_{12}
, since
x_{2}<u
is sufficient (due to the fact that
w[u+1,z-1]=\varepsilon
).

The cases where
u=u_{1}
and
u=v_{2}
follow analogously.
Consider the following:

w^{\prime}[u]

\varepsilon

w[z_{1},z_{2}]

\varepsilon

w[u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},y_{1}]

\cdots

w[x_{1},x_{2}]

\cdots

w^{\prime}[y_{2},u]
,

where
u=u_{1}
and
u=v_{2}
.
Similarly to previous cases, we handle this case with

\varphi_{13}:=\exists z_{1},z_{2},x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{1},z_{2},x_{1},x_{2})\land(v_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{1})\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{1})\land\\
(x_{2}<y_{2}\leq u)\land\mathsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}(y_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(u,z_{1})\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{2},u_{2})\land(v_{1}<z_{1}<z_{2}<u)\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}u_{1})\land(u\mathbin{\dot{=}}v_{2})\bigr).

For intuition,
\varphi_{13}
states that

•

w[z_{1},z_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{1},x_{2}]
,

•

there exists some position
y_{1}
, where
v_{1}\leq y_{1}<x_{1}
such that
w^{\prime}[u_{1}]=w^{\prime}[y_{1}]
, and

•

there exists some position
y_{2}
, where
x_{2}<y_{2}\leq v_{1}
such that
w^{\prime}[y_{2}]=w^{\prime}[v_{2}]
.

Therefore, it follows that
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
.

For the case where
u=u_{1}
and
v_{1}<u<v_{2}
, we have that

w^{\prime}[u]

w[z_{1},z_{2}]

w[z_{3}]

w[z_{4},u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},x_{1}]

w[x_{2},x_{3}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w^{\prime}[x_{3},v_{2}]
.

For the case where
u_{1}<u<u_{2}
and
u=v_{2}
, we have that

w[u_{1}]

w[z_{1},z_{2}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[z_{3},u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w^{\prime}[u,x_{1}]

w[x_{2},x_{3}]

w[x_{4}]

w^{\prime}[x_{5},v_{2}]
.

Since these cases follow analogously, we do not give formulas to realize them.

Case 4.2

u_{1}<v_{1}
and
u_{2}<v_{2}
:

This case is symmetric to Case 4.1. We have that:

\displaystyle w[u_{1},z_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{1}]\cdot w[z_{2},z_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[z_{4},u_{2}]

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}\;

\displaystyle w[v_{1},x_{1}]\cdot w^{\prime}[u]\cdot w[x_{2},x_{3}]\cdot w^{\prime}[y_{2}]\cdot w[x_{4},v_{2}].

Observing Case 4.1, it is clear that a subformula that handles this case can be written.

Concluding the proof for insertion.

The update formula
\phi^{\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}}_{{\mathsf{ins_{\mathtt{a}}}}}(u;u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
is defined by a disjunction over
\varphi_{i}
, where each
\varphi_{i}
realizes a case.
The update formula also ensures that
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1}
, and
v_{2}
are all symbol elements.

Part 2 (reset).

We now consider the reset operation.
Assume
u\in\mathrm{D}
is being reset.
Firstly, we deal with some special cases:

•

If
u
is outside of the intervals
[u_{1},u_{2}]
and
[v_{1},v_{2}]
, then
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
if and only if
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
.

•

If
u\in\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}\}
, then
R^{\prime}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2})
should not hold, since
u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},v_{2}
must be symbol elements.

•

If
u_{1}<u<u_{2}
and
u\notin[v_{1},v_{2}]
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
holds.

•

If
v_{1}\leq u_{1}
and
u_{2}\leq v_{2}
, then
w^{\prime}[u_{1},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w^{\prime}[v_{1},v_{2}]
.

As we have considered such cases many times, we omit the update formula.

Therefore, we only need to consider the cases for which
v_{1}<u<v_{2}
.

Case 1.

v_{1}<u<v_{2}
and
u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}]
:

In this case, if we can, we “stitch” together two scattered subwords.
That is illustrated with

w[u_{1},z_{1}]

\varepsilon

w[z_{2},u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},x_{1}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[x_{2},v_{2}]
,

such that
w[u_{1},z_{1}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[v_{1},x_{1}]
and
w[z_{2},u_{2}]\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}w[x_{2},v_{2}]
where
z_{1}\leadsto_{w}z_{2}
and
x_{1}\leadsto_{w}u\leadsto_{w}x_{2}
.
This can be handled by

\exists z_{1},z_{2},x_{1},x_{2}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},z_{2},v_{1},x_{1})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{2},u_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(z_{1},z_{2})\land\\
R_{\mathsf{Next}}(x_{1},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(u,x_{2})\land(u\notin[u_{1},u_{2}])\bigr).

Case 2.

v_{1}<u<v_{2}
and
u_{1}<u<u_{2}
:

This case has two sub-cases. We only look at Case 2.1. in detail, as Case 2.2. follows analogously.

Case 2.1.

v_{1}<u_{1}
and
v_{2}<u_{2}
:

Consider the following:

w[u_{1},z_{1}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[z_{2},z_{3}]

\varepsilon

w[z_{4},u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},x_{1}]

w^{\prime}[x_{1}+1,x_{2}-1]

w[x_{2},x_{3}]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[x_{4},v_{2}]
.

This can be realized by the following formula:

\exists x_{1},z_{1},\dots,x_{4},v_{4}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},z_{1},v_{1},x_{2})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{2},z_{3},x_{2},x_{3})\land\\
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{4},u_{2},x_{4},v_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(z_{1},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(u,z_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(z_{3},z_{4})\land\\
(x_{1}<x_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(x_{3},u)\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}(u,x_{4})\bigr).

There is also the case where the
w[z_{2},z_{3}]=\varepsilon
. For this case, consider the following:

w[u_{1},z_{1}]

w^{\prime}[u]

\varepsilon

w[z_{2},u_{2}]

\sqsubseteq_{\mathsf{scatt}}

w[v_{1},x_{1}]

w[x_{1}+1,u-1]

w^{\prime}[u]

w[x_{2},v_{2}]
,

which can be realized by the following formula:

\exists x_{1},v_{1},x_{2},v_{2}\colon\bigl(\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(u_{1},z_{2},v_{1},x_{1})\land\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}(z_{2},u_{2},x_{2},v_{2})\land\\
(x_{1}<u<x_{2})\land R_{\mathsf{Next}}^{\prime}(z_{1},z_{2})\bigr).

Case 2.2.

u_{1}<v_{1}
and
u_{2}<v_{2}
:

This case is analogous to Case 2.1, therefore we omit a detailed explanation.

Concluding the proof for reset.

The update formula for the reset operation (akin to the update formula for the insertion operation) is the disjunction of these subformulas. This concludes the proof for maintaining
\bar{R}_{\mathsf{scatt}}
.

Chapter 8
Conclusions

We conclude this thesis with a summary of each chapter, along with open problems and future directions for research.

Chapter

5
.

This chapter introduces
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, and looks at expressive power and decision problems with a particular focus on static analysis problems.

Regarding expressive power,
we show that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s have an equivalent expressive power as
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s.
From Freydenberger
[
37
]
, we immediately know that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
s have the same expressive power as core spanners.
The relative expressive power of fragments of
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
s is also considered in
Chapter

5
and these results are summarized in
Figure

5.3
.
One big open problem from this section is whether
\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax UCQ})
.
In fact, inexpressibility for any fragment of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
is an interesting, but likely difficult problem.
For example, Freydenberger and Peterfreund
[
41
]
showed that the language
\mathtt{a}^{n}\cdot\mathtt{b}^{n}
is not expressible in
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
using the so-called
Feferman-Vaught theorem
.
However, this approach seems to be more difficult for more complicated languages.

Regarding decision problems,
we show that model checking is
\mathsf{NP}
-complete, even for restricted cases such as the input word being of length one or when the query is weakly acyclic.
Reducing from the emptiness problem for extended Turing machines, we show that
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
universality (“
=\Sigma^{*}
”) is undecidable.
Furthermore, reducing from the finiteness problem for extended Turing machines, we show that
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
regularity is undecidable, and is neither semi-decidable nor co-semi-decidable.
These undecidability results have consequences for query optimization:
For example, there is no algorithm that given an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
computes an equivalent minimal
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.

We show that three split-correctness problems (split-correctness, splittability, and self-splittability) for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s are all undecidable.
However, Doleschal, Kimelfeld, Martens, Nahshon, and Neven
[
25
]
consider many other aspects regarding parallel correctness.
Thus, there is still a long way to go until we understand parallel correctness for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
For example, one could look at conditions that make splittability, self-splittability, and split-correctness decidable.
Alternatively, one could consider an evaluation-based approach to splitting the document, and consider algorithms based on this approach of first splitting a document before querying.
This second approach could be useful for making querying large documents tractable.
This is because splitting a large document into small sections that are easy to query drastically lowers the number of possible factors that need to be considered.

The last topic considered in this chapter is ambiguity, were we adapted pattern ambiguity from Mateescu and Salomaa
[
80
]
to
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
We show that it is
\mathsf{PSPACE}
-complete to decide whether a given
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
is
k
-ambiguous.
This direction seems like a very interesting and promising one for future research.
Not only for finding queries for which the resulting relation is small and easy to enumerate, but knowing the size of the intermediate relations of a query could be used for heuristic query optimizers
[
69
,
71
]
.

Chapter

6
.

This chapter develops the connection between relational conjunctive queries and
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s by providing a polynomial-time algorithm that either decomposes an
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
into an acyclic
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, or determines that this is not possible.
These acyclic
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s allow for polynomial time model checking, and their results can be enumerated with polynomial delay.
This follows from the fact that for
2\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, we can treat the word equations and regular constraints as standard
{\mathsf{CQ}}
atoms as we can materialize their relations quickly.
Consequently,
Chapter

6
establishes a notion of tractable acyclicity for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s.

Due to the close connections between fragments of
\mathsf{FC}[\mathsf{REG}]
and classes of document spanners, this provides us with a large class of tractable
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s and core spanners.
We briefly develop this connection further by giving sufficient syntactic criteria for a
\mathsf{SERCQ}
to be represented as an acyclic
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
But this is only the first step in the study of tractable
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s and
\mathsf{SERCQ}
s.

An important area of future research is that of semantic acyclicity for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
and
\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
.
That is, given
\varphi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
(or
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
), decide whether there exists an equivalent
\psi\in\mathsf{FC[REG]\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
(or
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
) such that
\psi
is acyclic; and ideally, compute
\psi
.
The undecidability of many static analysis problems in
Chapter

5
gives us some indication that this problem is undecidable.

Another future research direction is faster algorithms.
It seems likely that more efficient algorithms for model checking and enumeration can be found by utilizing string algorithms rather than materializing the relations for each atom.
Alternatively, one could look into sufficient criteria for efficient model checking.

We introduced a class of parameterized patterns, called
k
-ary local patterns – for which the membership problem can be solved in polynomial time.
Another promising direction is utilizing other structural parameters.
A systematic study of the decomposition of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s into
\mathsf{2FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s of bounded treewidth would likely yield a large class of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s with polynomial-time model checking.
As a consequence, one could define a suitable notion of treewidth for core spanners; or at least, define sufficient criteria for a core spanner to be represented as an
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC[REG]}
with bounded treewidth.
Determining the exact class of
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
s with polynomial-time model checking is likely a hard problem.
This is because such a result would solve the open problem in formal languages of determining exactly what patterns have polynomial-time membership.

Chapter

7
.

From a document spanner point of view,
Chapter

7
establishes upper bounds for maintaining the three most commonly examined classes of document spanners, namely
DynPROP
for regular spanners,
DynCQ
for core spanners, and
DynFO
for generalized core spanners. One consequence of this is that a large class of regular expressions with backreference operators are in fact
DynCQ
-languages (see
Section

7.2.3
).

While the bounds for regular spanners and generalized core spanners are what one might expect from related work, the
DynCQ
-bound for core spanners could be considered surprising low (it is still open whether
{\mathsf{DynCQ}}\subset{\mathsf{DynFO}}
).

By analysing the proof of
Theorem

7.2.25
, the central construction of our main result, it seems that the most important part of maintaining core spanners is updating the equality selection operator – and, to a lesser extent, the regular constraints.
One big question for future work is whether this may have a practical use for the evaluation of core spanners.
Although some may consider this unlikely, there is at least a possibility that the techniques given could be useful.

Section

7.3
describes how
\mathsf{EP\mathchar 45\relax FC}[{\mathsf{DynCQ}}]
can be used as a convenient tool to show that languages/relations can be maintained in
DynCQ
.
We have also given many relations that can be maintained in
DynCQ
but are not selectable by generalized core spanners.
Thus showing that
DynCQ
is more expressive than core spanners, and
DynFO
is more expressive than generalized core spanners.
While the exact difference in expressive power between dynamic complexity classes and spanner classes remains open, this question is a difficult one given the lack of lower bound proof techniques for
DynCQ
and
DynFO
.

FC-Datalog.

Freydenberger and Peterfreund
[
41
]
extended
\mathsf{FC}
with iteration operators.
As with first-order logic, extending
\mathsf{FC}
with certain iteration operators allows one to capture complexity classes.
For example,
\mathsf{FC}
(or first-order logic over a linear order) extended with so-called
least fixed points
captures
\mathsf{PTIME}

[
41
]
.
This gives rise to a variant of Datalog based on words utilizing
\mathsf{FC}
called
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog, introduced in
[
41
]
.

\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog has strong connections to existing research topics.
For example,
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog can be seen as an alternative to so-called
range concatenation grammars

[
10
]
or
Hereditary Elementary Formal Systems

[
55
,
81
]
– all three of which characterize
\mathsf{PTIME}
.

Each rule of
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog is an
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax CQ}
, and non-recursive
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog is alternative notation for
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
.
Thus, one can think of
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog as
\mathsf{FC\mathchar 45\relax UCQ}
with recursion.
It is yet to be seen whether techniques introduced in this thesis can be used for
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog.
Regardless of whether this is the case,
\mathsf{FC}
-Datalog is a very promising direction for future research.
